{D} +{L} ({L} | {D}) *Let's remember that in Lex, everything must start in the first column. If somethingdoes not start in that column, PCLex passes it directly to the generated C program, without processing it. In this way it is possible to create definitions of variables, etc. Without However, this is not recommended as Lex has a specific block for this in which you can even put processor directives, which must start obligatorily in the first column.Thus, the definitions area also allows you to place pure C code that iswill translate as is to the beginning of the C program generated by PCLex. For this I 3750Lexicographic analysisthey use the delimiters "% {" and "%}". Ex .:% {#include <stdlib.h> typedef struct _Node {int value;Node * next; } Node;Node * symboltable;%}It is normal to put the declarations of global variables used in this areain the actions of the rules area and an #include from the file that implements the symbols.The reader is probably wondering what the main () function containsof the C program that PCLex generates. The truth is that PCLex does not generate any main () , rather, it must be specified by the programmer. As a general rule, when intends to run a lexical analyzer in isolation (without an associated syntactic),Normal is that the actions associated with each rule do not contain any returns and that include a main () that only invokes yylex ():void main () {yylex ();};To specify the main () and how many additional functions are estimatedappropriate, the functions area is available. This area is completely copied by PCLex in the generated C file. That is why, for practical purposes, writing code between "% {" and "%}" in the definition area is equivalent to writing it in the functions.The following example reports how many times the literal "result" appears![](image_rsrcDC1.jpg) in the input string.% {int cont = 0;%}%%​ "Outcome"​ {cont ++;}​ . | \ n​ {;}%%void main () {yylex ();printf ("result appears% d times", cont);}2.4.3.6 Functions and variables supplied by PCLexAs we already know, the basic core of the C program generated by PCLex 3851is the yylex () function , which is responsible for looking for a lexeme and executing its action associated. This process is carried out iteratively until one of the actions find a return or the input is terminated. In addition, PCLex provides other functionsmacros and supporting variables; below is the list of the most interesting In order of importance:yylex () : implements the lexicographic analyzer.yytext : contains the current lexeme yyleng : number of characters of the current lexeme.yylval : is a global variable that allows communication with the syntactic. It is not actually defined by PCLex but by the PCYacc tool that will be studied in later chapters.yyin : is of type * FILE, and points to the input file that is read. Initially points to stdin , so the input file matches the input standard.yyout : of type * FILE, it points to the output file (initially stdout ).yyerror () : it is a function that is responsible for emitting and handling errors (it removes error messages on screen). It is actually defined by PCYacc as you will see later.yylineno : integer variable that, curiously, must be created, initialized and updated by the programmer. Serves to keep track of the number line being processed. It is normally initialized to 1 and increments each time a carriage return is encountered. yywrap () : the yylex () algorithm calls this macro automatically every time that meets an EOF. The usefulness of this macro is that it can be redefined (for this you must first delete it with the directive#undef ). This macro should return false (which in C is represented by the integer![](image_rsrcDC2.jpg) 0) in case the input string has not actually ended, which it can happen in two types of situations: a) a file is being processed binary that can contain the EOF character in the middle as one more, in which case the true end of the file must be discovered by contrasting the file length with the length of the consumed string; and b) the string to be recognized is partitioned into several files, in which case when you reach the end of one of them, you have to load the next one in yyin and continue processing. yywrap () should return true (integer value 1) in case the found EOF actually identifies the end of the string to process.yyless (int n) : leaves the first n characters of the current lexeme in yytext . The rest returns them to the input, so we can say that they are de-consumed. yyleng is also conveniently modified. For example, the pattern abc * / \ n3952Lexicographic analysisIt is equivalent to:abc * \ n {yyless (yyleng-1); }input () : consumes the next character from the input and appends it to the current lexeme.For example, the program:%%​ abc​ {printf ("% s", yytext);input (); printf ("% s", yytext);} before the input "abcde" it would enter through this pattern: the lexeme before the input () (in the first printf ) is "abc", and after the input (in the second printf ) it is "Abcd".output (char c) : outputs the character c on standard output. PCLex for DOS no supports this function, but Lex for Unix does.unput (char c) : unconsumes character c and places it at the beginning of the input.For example, suppose the program:%%abc​ {printf ("% s", yytext); unput ('t'); } such​ {printf ("% s", yytext); }receives the entry "abcal". The first three characters of the lexeme match with the first pattern. Then the string "al" remains in the input, but as an unput ('t') has been made , what is actually at the input is "such", that matches the second pattern.ECHO : macro that copies the input to the output (it is the action that is applied by default to lexemes that do not fit by any explicit pattern).yymore () : allows you to go on to recognize the next lexeme, but without deleting the current content of yytext , so the new lexeme read is concatenated to the it already exists. PCLex for DOS does not support this function, but Lex for Unix.The main utility of yymore () is that it makes it easier to recognizethe literals in quotes. Suppose you want to build a pattern to recognize quoted literals. A first idea could be the Pattern:​ \ ”[^ \”] * \ ”​ / * Lexemas that start with quotes,anything, and end in quotes. * /![](image_rsrcDC3.jpg) that it would recognize strings like "Hello", "bye", and even "Ho; *". Without However a string like "Hello, \" comrade \ "" which in turn contains quotes inside will give problems because the first quote for "comrade" is considered as a closing quotation mark. The solution to this problem happens by the following Lex code block:​ \ ”[^ \”] * / \ ”​ {if (yytext [yyleng-1] == '\\')yymore ();4053elseinput();} This pattern recognizes quoted strings (except the last quotes), so that strings that have quotes inside are recognized in parts.Continuing with the example from before, "Hello, \" comrade \ "" would be recognized how:This pattern would fail if the characters before the closing quotation marks were precisely '\\'. To solve it, you would have to make use of unput and a global variable that would act as a flag . Any sentence behind de yymore () will never run as it acts as a kind of goto .REJECT : reject the current lexeme, return it to the input, and look for another pattern.REJECT tells yylex () that the lexeme found does not match actually to the current regex, and find the following expression regulate as appropriate. The REJECT macro should be the last thing a action execute since if there is something behind it, it will not be executed. E.g. To search how many times the words "keyboard" and "side" appear, you can construct the following Lex program:% {int t = 0, l = 0;%} %% keyboard​ {t ++; REJECT;} side​ {l ++;}When entering "keyboard" this program behaves as follows:1.- Enter through the pattern that reads the longest lexeme that would be "Keyboard" and executes the associated action: the variable t is increased and the current lexeme is rejected, so the entire text is returned to the entrance.2.- It displays “tec” on the screen, which is the default action when they find characters that don't match any pattern.3.- The lexeme "side" 'coincides with the second pattern and executes the associated action: variable l is increased .Finally, it is clear that the programmer should not declare anyfunction or variable whose name matches the ones we just studied. In fact PCLex also generates a series of auxiliary variables whose name consists of a single character, so it is not good to declare variables or functions with names of a single letter or starting with yy .![](image_rsrcDC4.jpg) 4154Lexicographic analysis​ 2.5​ The JFlex Lexicographic Analyzer GeneratorJFlex is a generator of lexicographic analyzers developed by Gerwin Klein as an extension to the JLex tool developed at the University of Princeton. JFlex is developed in Java and generates Java code.Programs written for JFlex have a format similar to those writtenin PCLex; in fact all permissible regular patterns in Lex are also supported by JFlex, so in this section we will focus only on the differences and extensions, both of patterns and of the skeleton that the input file to JFlex.Installing and running JFlex is trivial. Once thefile jflex-1.3.5.zip , we will have the file JFlex.jar that is only necessary in meta-compilation time, the generated parser being totally independent. The Main class of the JFlex package is the one in charge of metacompiling our input .jflex program; thus, a typical invocation is of the form:java JFlex.Main file.jflexwhich will generate a Yylex.java file that implements the lexicographic analyzer. The figure 2 . 5 illustrates the process to follow, and how the default name of the generated class it's Yylex .Figure 2.5\. Obtaining a portable Java program from a JFlex specification2.5.1 Preliminary exampleIt is interesting to start with a preliminary example on which you canargue. The example (stored in a text file called test.jflex ) is the following:/ * JFlex example * / import java.io. *;%%//% class Lexer% int% unicode//% cup% line% column% {public static void main (String [] args) { Yylex Lexical Analyzer =42![](image_rsrcDC5.jpg) 55new Yylex (new InputStreamReader (System.in));try { lexicon parser.yylex ();} catch (IOException x) {System.out.println ("Error on line" + parserLexico.yyline + "column" + parserLexico.yycolumn); }}%}Line terminator = \ r | \ n | \ r \ n whitespace = {line terminator} | [\ t \ f]% state STRING%%<YYINITIAL> ”IF” {System.out.println (“Found IF”); }<YYINITIAL> {​ "="​ {System.out.println ("Found ="); }​ "! ="​ {System.out.println ("Found! ="); }​ "THEN"​ {System.out.println ("Found THEN"); }​ {WhiteSpace}​ { /* Ignore */; }​ \ "​ {yybegin (STRING); }[: jletter:] [: jletterdigit:] * {System.out.println ("Found an ID"); }}<STRING> {[^ \ n \ "\\] {System.out.println (" I'm on a string "); }​ \ n​ {System.out.println ("A string cannot end in \\ n");}​ \\ (. | \ n)​ {System.out.println ("Found" + yytext () + "on string");}​ \ "​ {yybegin (YYINITIAL); }}​ . | \ n​ {System.out.println ("Found any character"); }As can be seen in this example, the text is divided into threesections separated by %% , just like in Lex. However, the content of each section or area differs from the former, the general structure of the shape being:Code area, imports and package%%Options and declarations area%%Rules areaThe first of these areas is destined to the importation ofpackages to be used in the regular actions next to eachpattern in the rule zone. Here you can also specify a package clause for the .java files generated by the meta-parser.In general, anything written in this area will carry over as is to thefile . java generated by JFlex., so it can also be used to write classes4356![](image_rsrcDC6.jpg) Lexicographic analysiscomplete and interfaces.The following two areas are described in depth below.2.5.2 Options and declarations areaThis area allows you to indicate to JFlex a series of options to adapt the file . java resulting from the meta-compilation and that will be the one that implements our lexicographic analyzer in Java. It also allows associating a user identifier to the most used patterns in the area of  rules.2.5.2.1 OptionsThe most interesting options can be classified into class options, fromthe analysis function, end of file, character set and counters. Allthey start with the % character and cannot be preceded by anything on the line in that appear.​ 2.5.2.1.1​ Class optionsThe most useful class options are:% class classname . By default, the class that JFlex generates and implements the lexicographic analyzer is called Yylex and is therefore written in aYylex.java file . Using this option you can change the name of said class.% interface1 implements, Interface2, etc . Generate a class (by default Yylex ) which implements the indicated interfaces. The programmer must enter the methods necessary to make this implementation effective.% extends classname . Generates a class that inherits from the indicated class.% public . Generate a public class. The class generated by default does not have modify visibility scope.Final% . Generate a final class, from which no one can inherit.% abstract . Generates an abstract class from which objects cannot be created.% {javablock%} . The programmer can also include his own declarations and methods inside the Yylex class by writing these between the % { and %} symbols .% init {Javacode% init} . It is possible to include code in the generated constructor automatically by JFlex for the Yylex class by using this option.​ 2.5.2.1.2​ Analysis function optionsThese options allow modifying the method or function in charge of performingthe lexicographic analysis itself. The most useful options are:4457% function parserFunctionName . By default, the function that starts the parsing is called yylex () and returns a value of type Yytoken . This option allows you to rename the yylex () function .![](image_rsrcDC7.jpg) % int . Makes the yylex () function return values  of type int instead of Yytoken .% intwrap . Makes the yylex () function return values  of type Integer in Yytoken place .% type typename . Makes the yylex () function return values  of type specified (whether primitive or not) instead of Yytoken .In any case, JFlex does not create the Yytoken class , it must besupplied externally, or specified in the code area.​ 2.5.2.1.3​ End of file optionsBy default, when yylex () meets the end of file character, it returns anull value . In case % int was specified in the previous options,will return the YYEOF value defined as public static final in the Yylex class . For To change this behavior, the most useful end-of-file options are:% eofclose . Causes yylex () to close the read channel as soon as the EOF.% eofval {JavaCode% eofval} . It is possible to execute a block of code when yylex () finds the EOF. This option will be used for this.​ 2.5.2.1.4​ Character set optionsThese options allow you to specify the character set in which it will beencoded the input to the lexicographic parser generated by JFlex. The posibilities They are:% 7bit . It is the default option, and it assumes that each character of the input is made up of a single byte whose most significant bit is 0, which gives 128 characters.% 8bit . It assumes that each character is made up of a complete byte, which gives 256 characters.% unicode . It assumes that the input will consist of Unicode characters. This does not it means that two bytes of the input are taken for each character butcharacter recovery is left to the platform on which it is run yylex ().% ignore . Make yylex () ignore case by using the use of the toUpperCase and toLowerCase methods of the Character class .It is clear that the first three options are mutually exclusive.Four. Five58Lexicographic analysis​ 2.5.2.1.5​ Counter optionsThese options cause the lexicographic analyzer to store in countersthe number of characters, lines, or columns where the current lexeme begins. The nomenclature is:% char . Stores in the variable yychar the number of characters between the beginning of the input channel and the beginning of the current lexeme.% line . Stores in the variable yyline the number of the line where the current lexeme.![](image_rsrcDC8.jpg) % column . stores in the variable yycolumn the column number in which the current lexeme begins.All counters start at 0.2.5.2.2 Declarations.In addition to options, the programmer can indicate declarations of two typesin the area at hand, namely, statements of lexical statements and statements of rules.​ 2.5.2.2.1​ Statements of lexical statements.Lexical states are declared by the option:% state state1, state2, etc.and they can be used in the rules area in the same way as in Lex. Also, if there is many patterns in the rule area that share the same lexical state, it is possible indicate this only once and then group all these rules in braces, such as can be seen in the preliminary example of point 2.5.1 . The initial state is given by the constant YYINITIAL and, unlike Lex, can be used as any another user-defined lexical state.A lexical action can change its lexical state by invoking the functionyybegin (LexicalState) generated by JFlex.​ 2.5.2.2.2​ Rule statements.In case a pattern is used repeatedly or when its complexityis high, it is possible to assign a name to it and use it later in any other rule enclosing it in brackets, analogous to how it was studied in Lex. The syntax is: name = pattern2.5.3 Rules area.The rules area has the same structure as in Lex, with the only differencethat it is possible to group the rules to apply in the same lexical state. How example, the rules:4659​ <YYINITIAL> "="​ {System.out.println ("Found ="); }​ <YYINITIAL> "! ="​ {System.out.println ("Found! ="); }they can also be written as:<YYINITIAL> {

| "=" | {System.out.println ("Found ="); } |

}The important differences in this area with respect to Lex are concentrated inthat JFlex includes some additional features for specifying patterns, as they are:\ u #hexadecimal - Represents the character whose Unicode value is #hexadecimal .Eg: \ u042D represents the character "Э".!: fits any lexeme except those that enter through the pattern that happens. Ex .:! (Ab) matches anything except the lexeme "ab".![](image_rsrcDC9.jpg) ~: fits a lexeme that begins with anything and ends with the first appearance of a text that fits the pattern that follows. Eg the way easier to recognize a Java-style comment (not nested) comes given by the pattern: "/ *" ~ "* /"On the other hand, JFlex includes some predefined character classes. Theseclasses are enclosed between the symbols [: and:] and each one matches the set of characters that satisfy a certain predicate. For example, the pattern [: digit:] matches those characters that satisfy the isDigit () logical function of the class Character . The list of patterns is:

| Pattern | Associated predicate |
| [: jletter:] | isJavaIdentifierStart () |
| [: jletterdigit:] | isJavaIdentifierPart () |
| [: letter:] | isLetter () |
| [: digit:] | isDigit () |
| [: uppercase:] | isUpperCase () |
| [: lowercase:] | isLowerCase () |

Finally, two other important differences from Lex are:Every rule must have an associated lexical action, even if it isempty.If a lexeme does not fit by any pattern, a lexical error occurs.The pattern \ n encompasses only the ASCII code 10 character, but not the code characterASCII 13, which is represented by the \ r pattern.2.5.4 Functions and variables of the Yylex class4760Lexicographic analysisWe have already seen two of the most important functions generated by JFlex andthat can be used by the programmer within the lexical actions. Here we remember and extend them:Yylex (Reader r) : is the constructor of the lexical analyzer. Take as parameter the input channel from which characters will be read.Yytoken yylex () : main function that implements the lexical analyzer. Taking the input of the parameter specified in the call to the Yylex constructor . You can raise an IOException , so invoking it is recommended inside a try-catch statement . String yytext () : returns the current lexeme.int yylength () : returns the number of characters in the current lexeme.void yyreset (Reader r) : close current input channel and redirect input to the new channel specified as a parameter.void yypushStream (Reader r) : save the current input stream to a stack and continues reading on the new specified channel. When it endsit will continue through the previous one, pulling it from the stack. This function is of special Importance of implementing the #include directive in languages  like C.![](image_rsrcDCA.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDCB.jpg) void yypushback (int n) : equivalent to Lex's yyless () function .yyline , yychar and yycolumn : these are the variables generated by the % line options , % char and % column respectively, and commented on in the section2.5.2.1.5.4861Chapter 3Syntactic analysis​ 3.1​ OverviewEvery programming language obeys rules that describe thesyntactic structure of well-formed programs it accepts. In Pascal, byFor example, a program is made up of blocks; a block, of sentences; a sentence, of expressions; an expression, of lexical components; and so on until get to the basic characters. You can describe the syntax of the constructs of programming languages  by means of free context grammars or by using BNF ( Backus-Naur Form ) notation .Formal grammars offer significant advantages to designers oflanguages  and compiler developers:Grammars are precise, syntactic specifications of languages  of programming.A parser can be automatically generated from a grammarsyntactic.The automatic generation process above can lead to discoveringambiguities.A grammar provides a structure to a programming language,being easier to generate code and detect errors.It is easier to expand / modify language if it is described with a grammar.![](image_rsrcDCC.jpg) Most of this topic is devoted to methods of analysissyntactic of typical use in compilers. We'll start with the basics and proper techniques for manual application. Next we will discuss the management and syntactic error recovery. We will study the formal methods of analysis using battery-powered automata and, finally, we will delve into the operation of metacompilers that automatically generate the parser of a language.​ 3.2​ Parser conceptIt is the phase of the analyzer that is in charge of checking the sequence of tokensA representing the input text, based on a given grammar. In case the input program is valid, it supplies the syntax tree that recognizes it based on to a computational representation. This tree is the starting point of the phase later in the analysis stage: the semantic analyzer.4962Syntactic analysisBut this is the theory; in practice, the parser drives the processcompilation, so that the rest of the phases evolve as the syntacticit recognizes the input sequence, so often the tree does not even actually generates.In practice, the parser also:It incorporates semantic actions in which to place the rest of the phases of thecompiler (except the lexical analyzer): from semantic analysis to code generation.Reports the nature of the syntactic errors it finds and attemptsrecover from them to continue compilation.Controls the flow of tokens recognized by the lexical analyzer.In short, it performs almost all the operations of the compilation, resulting into a working method called syntax-driven compilation .​ 3.3​ Syntactic error handlingIf a compiler had to process only correct programs, its design andimplementation would be greatly simplified. The first versions of the programs are often incorrect, and a good compiler should help the programmer to identify and locate errors. Furthermore, consider the management ofErrors can simplify the structure of a compiler and improve its response to errors. mistakes.Programming errors can be of the following types:Lexical, produced by misspelling an identifier, a keyword or aoperator.Syntactic, by an arithmetic expression or unbalanced parentheses.Semantics, as an operator applied to an incompatible operand.Logical, it can be an infinitely recursive call.Correction, when the program does not do what the programmer actually doeswished.It is clear that correction errors cannot be detected by![](image_rsrcDCD.jpg) a compiler, since they involve the abstract concept that the programmer has about the program it builds, which is unknown, and probablyunknowable, by the compiler. On the other hand, the detection of logical errors implies a very large computational effort as the compiler must be able to find out the different flows that a running program can follow which, inIn many cases, it is not only expensive, but also impossible. For all this, Current compilers focus on recognizing the first three types ofmistakes. In this topic we will talk about syntax errors, which are the ones that can prevent the correct construction of a syntactic tree.fifty63Syntax error handling is the most complicated from the point of viewof compiler creation. We are interested that when the compiler finds a error, do not cancel the compilation for good, but recover and keep looking mistakes. Recovering an error does not mean correcting it, but being able to continue building the syntactic tree despite the errors found. In view of this, the The error handler of a parser should have as objectives:Indicate errors clearly and precisely. You must report through corresponding messages of the type of error and its location.Recover from the error, so that you can continue to examine the entry.• Distinguish between errors and warnings. Warnings are often used to report valid sentences but which, because they are infrequent, they can be a source of logical errors.Do not significantly slow down the compilation.A good compiler should always be made with theerrors that may occur, thereby simplifying its structure.Also, if the compiler itself is prepared to admit even the most bugs frequent, then you can improve the response to these errors even correcting them.Several strategies to manage errors will be discussed below.once detected3.3.1 Ignore the problemThis strategy (called panic mode in English) consists of ignoring the restfrom the entrance to a safe condition. Such a condition occurs when we find a special token (for example a ';' or an 'END'). Starting from this point it continues to be analyzed normally. The tokens found from detection of the error until the error condition is discarded, as well as the sequence of tokens prior to the error that is deemed appropriate (usually until the previous safety condition).For example, suppose the following sequence of assignment statementsin C or Java:aux = a [i] a [i] = a [j]; a [j] = aux;The sequence found by the parser is shown in the figure 3 . one;clearly the semicolon from the first statement is missing and since it doesn't make sense a statement like:aux = a [i] a [i] = a [j];![](image_rsrcDCE.jpg) since the parser notices an error, and takes advantage of the fact that the ';' is used as statement terminator to ignore the rest of the input until the next ';'. Some additional considerations on this error management strategy may be studied in sections 4.3.4 and8.4.1 .5164Syntactic analysisFigure 3.1\. Syntactic error recovery ignoring erroneous sequence3.3.2 Phrase-level recoveryTry to correct the error once discovered. For example, in the case proposed in thepoint above, you might have been smart enough to insert the token ';' . Be careful with this method, as it can lead to infinite recoveries, that is, situations in which the correction attempt is not the correct one, but introduces a new error which, in turn, attempts to correct same wrong way and so on.3.3.3 Additional production rulesThis mechanism adds to the formal grammar that describes the language rulesof production to recognize the most common mistakes. Continuing with the case of point 3.3.1 , you could have put something like:wrong_sent​ ÷ sent_unfinished sent_finished sent_finished​ ÷ statement ';' sent_unfinished ÷ sentencewhich gives us greater control, and even allows us to recover and correct the problem and issue a warning message instead of an error message.3.3.4 Global CorrectionThis method tries by all means to obtain a syntactic tree for atoken sequence . If there is any error and the sequence cannot be recognized, then this method infers a syntactically correct sequence of tokens as close as possible to the original and generates the tree for that sequence. That is, the parserit asks the whole sequence of tokens to the lexicon, and what it does is return the closest thing to the input string but without errors, as well as the tree that recognizes it.​ 3.4​ Grammar used by a parserWe will focus our efforts on the study of syntactic analysis to

Basically a syntactic tree corresponds to a sentence, obeys a grammar, and constitutes a representation that is used to describe the process of derivation of said sentence. The root of the tree is the initial axiom and, according to our convenience, we will draw it on the top or bottom of the tree.As internal nodes of the tree, the non-terminal elements of theproduction rules that we apply, and each one of them will have so many children as symbols exist on the right side of the applied rule.Let's see an example using the grammar of the box 3 . 1 ; Let's supposewe must recognize the string (a + b) * a + b that the lexical analyzer has given us supplied as (id + id) * id + id and we will build the syntax tree, whichto              b              to              b It would be the one in figure 3 .2 .Note that a sequence of derivations originating from the initial axiom anddestination in the string to be recognized constitutes a representation of the syntactic tree. In this way, in Each derivation selects a leaf node from the tree that is being formed and is their children add. At each step, the leaves of the tree that is being built constitute the sentential form through which the derivation runs. On the other hand, the sequence of derivations not only represents a syntactic tree, but also gives information in which order the production rules have been applied.The construction of this tree entails the intelligent application of the rulesof production. If the sentence to be recognized is incorrect, that is, it does not belong to the language generated by the grammar, it will be impossible to obtain its syntactic tree. In others Sometimes a statement admits more than one syntactic tree. When this happens it is said that the grammar is ambiguous. This type of situation must be avoided, so that, either the grammar is changed, or disambiguous rules are applied (which we will see later). They may be:"With setback."With recursive functions.![](image_rsrcDCK.jpg) "From grammars LL (1).Ascending: They start from the input sentence, and they apply derivationsinverse (from the consequent to the antecedent), up to the axiom initial. They may be:"With setback."From grammars LR (1).We will study each of these methods in detail below.3.5.1 Downward analysis with retracementAs it is a top-down method, we start from the initial axiom and applyin sequence all possible rules to the leftmost nonterminal of the form sentence for which you are working.We can say that all sentences of a language are found asnodes of a tree representing all possible derivations from anysentential form, and which has as root the initial axiom. This is the universal tree of a grammar, so that its branches are sequences of derivations and, therefore, represent syntactic trees. Basically, we can say that the analysis method descending with backspace intends to search the universal tree for the sentence a recognize; when it finds it, the path that separates it from the root gives us the tree syntactic. However, it is not the only one, since the BNF notation and the diagramssyntax have the same power.Assuming that the BNF notation also allows expressing a languageformal, a definition of the syntax diagram will be given below and it will be demonstrated that these have, at least, the same power as the aforementioned BNF notation. This forms all possible paths from the beginning of the graph to the end, they represent valid sentential forms.3.5.2.2 Power of syntax diagramsWe will show that syntax diagrams allow us to represent the samegrammars than the BNF notation, by induction on the basic BNF operations:​ Operation​ BNF​ Syntax diagram​ Juxtaposition​ AB​ Option​ A | B![](image_rsrcDCS.jpg) g | B​ Repetition​ 1 or more times{B}0 or more times[B]Following the «Rosetta stone» that the previous table supposes, it is possibleconvert any BNF expression to its corresponding syntax diagram, since A and B can, in turn, be expressions or complex diagrams.On the other hand, anyone who has worked extensively on syntax diagrams6174Syntactic analysisYou may have noticed that it is not always easy to convert a syntax diagram any to its corresponding in BNF notation.3.5.2.3 Correspondence with execution flowsNow we are going to establish a correspondence between the execution flow ofa program and the path that can be followed in one or more syntax diagrams to recognize a valid statement. This table has as axis ofordered to the non-terminals of the grammar, and as abscissa to the terminals (including in pseudotoken EOF). The content of each inner box (which corresponds to the column of a terminal and the row of a nonterminal) contains either a production, or it is empty, which is interpreted as a rejection of the chain to recognize.In general, we can say that an LL (1) grammar is one in which it isenough to examine only one symbol at the input, to know which rule to apply, starting from the initial axiom and making derivations to the left. All grammar recognizable by the method of syntax diagrams following the procedure seen in the previous point is LL (1).The method consists of following an algorithm starting from:The string to recognize, along with a pointer, which tells us which is the token current pre-search; denoted by a said token .A stack of symbols (terminals and non-terminals); we will denote by X the top from this stack.A syntax check table uniquely associated with a grammar.In this text, the way in which this table is obtained from of grammar. We will denote by M this table, which will have a row by each non-terminal of the starting grammar, and one column for each terminal including EOF: M [N × Tc {$}].As always, the input string will end in EOF which, from now on,we will denote by the symbol '$'. The aforementioned algorithm consists of consulting repeatedly the M table until accepting or rejecting the sentence. Starting from a pile having the $ at its base and the initial axiom S of the grammar above, each step of query consists of following one of the following points (they are exclusive):1.- If X = a = $ then ACCEPT.2.- If X = a… $ thenX is removed from the stack- and the pointer is advanced.6780Syntactic analysis3.- If X 0 T and X… a then REJECT.4.- If X 0 N then we consult the entry M [X, a] of the table, and:If M [X, a] is empty: REJECT.![](image_rsrcDCY.jpg) If M [X, a] is not empty, X is removed from the stack and theconsequent in reverse order. Example: If M [X, a] = {X 6 UVY}, X is removed from the stack, and UVY is put in reverse order, like show the figure 3 . 19 .Figure 3.19\. Rule application in the LL method (1)As in top-down analysis with recursive functions, aOnce a rule has been applied, it will no longer be de-applied by any kind of backtracking.One of the main drawbacks of this type of analysis is that the numberof grammars LL (1) is relatively small; However, when a grammar is notis LL (1), it is usually possible to translate it to obtain an equivalent that is LL (1), after a proper study. The same does not happen with functions6982Syntactic analysisrecursive, since these have to be reconstructed by hand with the consequent loss time and error prone.3.5.4 Overview of bottom-up analysisBefore particularizing in the different types of bottom-up analysis, it is It is convenient to study the concepts and methodology common to all of them. The objective of bottom-up analysis consists of building the syntactic tree from the bottom above, that is, from the tokens to the initial axiom, which decreases the number of misapplied rules regarding the top-down case (if we talk about the case with backspace) or broadens the number of grammars that can be analyzed (if we speak of case LL (1)).Whether there is backspace or not, at any given time, the input string It will be divided into two parts, called α and β:β : represents the chunk of the input string (sequence of tokens ) by* consume: β 0 T. It will always coincide with some piece of the right part of the input string. As can be assumed, initially β coincides with the string to be fully recognized (including the end EOF).α : will always match the rest of the input string, the chunk that is they will have applied some production rules in reverse: α 0 (NcT). *For example, if we wanted to recognize "id + id + id", starting from the grammar![](image_rsrcDD0.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDD1.jpg) of the picture 3\. 3 , the syntactic tree would begin to be built from the empty tree (α = g) and with the entire input string to consume (β = id + id + id):, and after consuming the first token :, and now we can apply the rule æ backwards, so that α is F:, to which the rule Ñ can now be applied, producing:. Thus, little by little the tree is built:7083As can be seen, α represents the syntactic tree seen from above as It is built upwards: α is an ascending sentence form.3.5.4.1 Operations in a bottom-up analyzerAs a parser builds the tree, it facesto a different configuration (the α-β pair configuration is called) and must take a decision on the next step or operation to perform. Basically it hasfour different operations, and each type of bottom-up parser is distinguished from the others based on intelligence on when to apply each of these operations. Any bottom-up analysis mechanism consists of starting from a configuration initial and gradually applying operations, each of which allows to go from one source configuration to another destination. The process will finish when the configuration destination becomes such that α represents the complete syntactic tree and β has consumed all tokens . The available operations are as follows:1.- ACCEPT: the string is accepted: β / EOF and α / S (initial axiom).2.- REJECT: the input string is not valid.![](image_rsrcDD2.jpg) 3.- Reduce : consists of applying a production rule backwards to some elements located at the extreme right of α. For example, if we have the setting:​ α / [XX ... X one​ twop + 1​ p X​ ... However, it is unfeasible for practical applications as its inefficiency remains unacceptable.7386Syntactic analysis3.5.6 Bottom-up analysis of LR grammars (1)In this section an efficient syntactic analysis technique will be introducedascendant that can be used to process a wide class of grammars from free context. The technique is called LR (k) parsing. The abbreviation LRThis is because the input string is examined from left to right (in English, Left-to-right ), while the "R" indicates that the process provides the syntactic tree by means of the sequence of derivations to the right (in English, Rightmost derivation ) in reverse order. Finally, the "k" refers to the number of pre-search tokens used to make decisions about whether to reduce or displace. When omitted, assume k, is 1.LR analysis is attractive for several reasons.Can recognize the vast majority of programming languages  that can be generated using context-free grammars.The method of operation of these analyzers has the advantage oflocate a syntactic error almost at the same time it occurs with what is acquired great compile-time efficiency versus less adequate procedures such as backtracking. Further, error messages pinpoint the source of the error.The main drawback of the method is that it involves too much workmanually build an LR parser for a language grammarof typical programming, being necessary to use a specialized tool to it: an automatic generator of LR parsers. Fortunately, there are several of these generators available. Later we will study the design and use of some of them: the Yacc, Cup and JavaCC metacompilers. With Yacc or Cup you can write a free context grammar with its parser automatically generatedsyntactic. If the grammar contains ambiguities or other difficult-to-understand constructions parse in a left-to-right scan of the input, the metacompiler can locate problematic production rules and report them to the designer.This technique, like that of LL (1), bases its operation on the existenceof a special table uniquely associated with a grammar. There are several techniques to build said table, and each one of them produces a “substitute” for the method principal. Most authors focus on three main methods. The first of them, called Simple LR ( SLR , in English) is the easiest to apply, but the least powerful of the three as you may not be able to produce an analysis table syntactic for some grammars that other methods do. The second method,![](image_rsrcDD5.jpg) called Canonical LR, it is the most powerful but the most expensive. The third method, called It lr with lookahead ( Look Ahead LR in English), it is between the other two in in terms of power and cost. The LALR method works with most grammars programming languages  and, with very little effort, can be implemented in efficient way. Basically one method is more powerful than another depending on the number of grammars to which it can be applied. The broader the spectrum of The more complex supported grammars the method becomes.7487Functionally speaking, an LR analyzer consists of two parts welldifferentiated: a) a process program and b) an analysis table. The program of The process has, as will be seen below, a very simple operation andit remains unchanged from analyzer to analyzer. According to the grammar to be processed the content of the analysis table, which is the one that fully identifies to the analyzer.The figure 3 .25 shows a synoptic diagram of the general structure of aLR analyzer. As can be seen from it, the parser processes a string of input terminated with the $ symbol representing the EOF delimiter. This chain is reads from left to right, and single symbol recognition makes it possible to take timely decisions (LR (1)).Figure 3.25\. ![](image_rsrcDD9.jpg) grammars.​ 3.5.6.1.1​ Right or left recursionOne of the most basic approaches is deciding whether to userules recursive left or recursive right. For example, to recognize a sequence of identifiers separated by commas like:id, id, idit is possible to choose between two different grammars:to)​ â list ÷ ID to​ | list ',' IDb)​ â list ÷ ID to​ | ID ',' listWell, when it comes to recognizing a sentence we canobserve that very different trees are obtained, as in the figure 3\. 26 by recognizing “go, Yo hice". In turn, if we apply an LR recognition, the sequence of steps applied differs radically: in case b) they move sequentially, one by one, all the chain tokens and, finally, several reductions are made in a row, depending on the chain length; in case a) several tokens (the comma and an ID) are moved and reduce by Ï, and so on, the displacements and reductions.In this way, with recursion to the right, the stack α increases in sizeuntil the reductions begin. Therefore, before lists of identifiers large enough, an overflow could occur. Most convenient, so it is to use left recursive grammars so that the stack size remains stable. In those cases where the recursion to the right is absolutely necessary (some of these cases will be presented to us in chapters7992Syntactic analysis![](image_rsrcDDA.jpg) subsequent) should avoid supplying excessively long chains that can produce overflows.Figure 3.26\. Recognition of the same sentence by equivalent grammars: a) recursive left, and b) recursive right​ 3.5.6.1.2​ ConflictsA conflict occurs when the parser is unable to generate the tablesyntax check for a grammar. There are basically two types of conflicts:Shift / Reduce ( Shift / Reduce ). They are usually due to ambiguous grammars.Reduce / Reduce ( Reduce / Reduce ). Because the grammar does not admit a LR (k) analysis.The Shift / Shrink conflict appears when it appears in the action table In the same box both an R to reduce and a D to displace, the conflict is thatthe program does not know whether to reduce or displace. For example, such a simple grammar how:​ EXPR​ ÷ EXPR '+' EXPRFigure 3.27\. Example of ambiguity8093| numit is obviously ambiguous (it is recursive on the right and on the left simultaneously), then before an entry as simple as "num + num + num" two different syntactic trees can be produced (see figure 3 .27 ).On the other hand, the Reduce / Reduce conflict appears when the analyzer canapply two or more rules to certain situations. This situation usually appears when the grammar is not LR (1), that is, a pre- token is not enough search to make the decision of what action to take. For example, suppose the grammar:Î S ÷ aaBdd Ï | aCdÐ B ÷ aÑ  C ÷ yywhich only recognizes the two sequence of tokens "aaadd" and "aaad". Well, a so simple grammar is not LR (1) (see figure 3 . 28 ). Suppose that in this figure![](image_rsrcDDB.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDDC.jpg) you want to recognize the sequence "aaad"; in this case the correct thing would be to reduce by Ñ. Without However, it all depends on whether after the pre-search token (a “d”), another “d” comes or wear EOF. An LR (2) parser would be able to make the right decision, but not one LR (1).Figure 3.28\. Example of Reduce / Reduce conflict. The interrogations indicate that the analyzer does not know what comes nextReduce / Reduce conflicts can be eliminated in most cases.This is achieved by delaying the reduction decision at the last minute. For example, the grammar:S ÷ aaadd | aaadis equivalent to the previous one, but it is LR (1), since it is only decided to reduce before the EOF from the end, at which point it will be in the correct state based on the tokens that have been read in full.3.5.6.2 Conclusions on the LR analysis (1)In the heading 3.5.6 how LR analyzers work was studied usingthe use of their corresponding analysis tables. In the final example you can be observed as the state that is always at the top of the stack contains in8194Syntactic analysisat all times the information necessary for the reduction, if this proceeds. Further, we have intentionally left out the structure of the process program since it is essentially a finite automaton with a stack.In general, it can be stated that, given the structure of LR analyzers, withthe single inspection of k symbols in the input string to the right of the last consumed, it is possible to decide exactly what movement to perform (reduction, displacement, etc). It is for this reason that it is usually called such grammars as LR (k). As already mentioned, in practice almost all Programming languages  can be analyzed using LR grammars (1).The LR (1) tables devised by Knuth in 1965 are too large forgrammars of traditional programming languages. In 1969 De Remer and Korenjack discovered ways to compact these boards, making them practical and manageable this type of analyzers. The algorithm applied to these tables is the same already seen. We have also seen that there are three main types of grammars LR (k) that vary depending on their power being included in each other of the following form:SLR (k) d LALR (k) d LR (k)but not their respective languages  that match in their sets, that is, if there is a grammar LR (1) that recognizes the language L, then there is also a grammar SLR (1) which recognizes L.The Yacc and Cup metacompilers use LALR analysis (1). The automatonmust keep information about its configuration (α stack), and to keep![](image_rsrcDDD.jpg) information about β is communicated with Lex, who takes care of the meta-compilation to lexical level.8295Chapter 4Attributed grammars​ 4.1​ Semantic analysisIn addition to controlling that a program complies with the rules of grammarlanguage, you have to check that what you want to do makes sense. The analysis semantic gives a coherent meaning to what we have done in the analysis syntactic. The semantic check ensures that the types that intervene in the expressions are compatible or that the actual parameters of a function areconsistent with the formal parameters: for example, it does not usually make much sense to multiply a string of characters by an integer.We will start by looking at a simple example in which the concept is introducedattribute by constructing the interpreter of a calculator.4.1.1 Attributes and semantic actionsAn example is developed below to have a global vision of thistheme. Suppose you want to build a small calculator that performs the + and * operations. The grammar will be as follows: E ÷ E + T | TT ÷ T * F| FF ÷ (E)| num![](image_rsrcDDE.jpg) and we want to calculate: 33 + 12 + 20\. The steps to follow are:Figure 4.1\. Recognition tree for “num + num + num ". The numbers in circles indicate the sequence of reductions according to a LR analysis (1)8396Attributed grammarsLexical analysis. The input string is converted to a sequence of tokens , eg with PCLex: 33 + 12 + 20 - num + num + numSyntactic analysis. Using the above grammar and, p. e.g., PCYacc se produces the syntactic tree of the figure 4 . 1 .We have managed to build the syntactic tree, which does not mean that it issemantically correct. If we want to build an interpreter, the following step is to know what result each operation gives us, and for this we have labeled the tree indicating the order in which thereductions. The order in which the rules are applied gives us the parse law.The order in which the production rules are applied is very close to the semantics of what is to be recognized. And this fact we must take advantage of. For this we are going to decorate the syntactic tree associating values  to the terminals and non-terminals that appear in it. Terminals arewe will assign that value through an action associated with the corresponding pattern in the lexical analysis, as follows:[0-9] + {Convert yytext to an integer and associate that integer to the NUM token; return NUM;}therefore what will arrive at the parser is:​ num. +33\. num. nothing+. num.​ 12​ nothing​ twenty![](image_rsrcDDF.jpg) Let's see what use could be made of these attributes for, as does the syntactic recognition, build the result to return. TheFigure 4.2\. Propagation of leaf attributes towards the root8497The idea is that each non-terminal of the grammar has an associated attribute that agglutinate, in some way, the information of the syntactic sub-tree of which it is root.The figure 4 . 2 illustrates the propagation of attributes from terminals tonon-terminals. This propagation can be implemented by executing associated actions to each production rule, in such a way that the action will be executed every time it is reduce by its associated rule, in the same way that we could associate an action to a lexical analyzer pattern. In this way, with the associations of values  to terminal and nonterminal and associations of semantic actions to patterns and to the production rules, we can evaluate the behavior of a program, it is that is, we generate code (concrete or abstract; we can consider that an interpreter generates abstract code, since at no time does it physically exist). Thiscan be seen in the following table (the subscripts only serve to differentiate between different instances of the same grammatical symbol when it appears several times in the same rule).

| Production rules | Associated semantic actions |
| ​ E ÷ E + Tone​ two | ​ {E = E + T;one​ two} |
| E ÷ T | {E = T;} |
| ​ T ÷ T * Fone​ two | ​ {T = T * F;one​ two} |
| T ÷ F | {T = F;} |
| F ÷ (E) | {F = E;} |
| F ÷ num | {F = NUM;} |

These actions must be executed every time it is syntactically reduced bythe associated rule.Based on the operation of a parser with recursive functions,it is as if each function returns a value that represents the sub-tree of which it is root. The implementation in the LR (1) case is a bit more complex, storing the attributes on stack α such that, except for the initial state, α stores tuples of the form (state, symbol, attribute), so that every symbol has an attribute associated even when it is not necessary at all (as in the case of the token + from the example above). The changes produced are illustrated in figure 4 . 3 .Thus, an attribute is information associated with a terminal or a non-terminal.An action or semantic rule is an algorithm that can access the attributes of the terminals and / or non-terminals. As a semantic action you can not only put a assignment to an attribute, additionally code can be added; eg if the association is made:![](image_rsrcDDG.jpg) ​ Î E ÷ E + Tone​ two​ {E = E + T; one​ twoprintf ("% d \ n", E)}              oneusing pseudo-C notation, then each time the Î rule is applied,will display on the screen the value of the attribute of the rule which, in the example, will be the values  45 and 65\. If you only wanted the global resulting value to appear, you could add a new rule S ÷ E and pass the printf of rule Î to this one:​ S ÷ E​ {printf ("% d \ n", E); }8598Attributed grammars​ E ÷ E + Tone​ two​ {E = E + T; one​ two}In this way, the construction of the syntactic tree would have one more reduction, and when If this was done, the result would appear on the screen. Note how the addition of this rule does not modify grammar in the sense that it continues to recognize the same language but, nevertheless, it provides us with a clear utility by allowing us to visualize only the final result of the calculation.Figure 4.3\. Stack management α without attributes and with attributes4.1.2 Execution of a semantic actionThere are two ways to associate semantic rules with production rules:Syntax-directed definition : consists of associating a semantic action to a production rule, but that association does not indicate when it should be execute said semantic action. It is assumed that in a first phase builds the complete syntax tree and then executes the semantic actions in a sequence that allows the calculation of all the attributes of the tree nodes.Translation scheme : is the same as a syntax-driven definition except that additionally, information is assumed or provided about when semantic actions must be executed. What's more, it is also possible insert actions between the symbols of the consequent of a rule of86![](image_rsrcDDH.jpg) 99production, e.g .: A ÷ cd {A = c + d} B. In the LR case the shares are run when reductions are made. In the case with functions recursive actions are interspersed with the code intended to recognize each BNF expression.As long as we have a translation scheme with embedded actionsit can be passed to its equivalent syntax-driven definition. For example, him scheme:​ A ÷ cd {action} B​ onecan become​ A ÷​ cd NB one​ N ÷one​ g {action}​ onewhich is equivalent. Basically, the transformation consists of introducing a no dummy terminal (N in the example) that replaces each intermediate semantic actionone (action), and create a new production rule of type epsilon with said non-terminalone as antecedent and with said semantic action as action.At first glance, this transformation may appear to be troublesome;eg when converting the schema:A ÷ cd {printf ("% d \ n", c + d); } Bin:​ A ÷ cd NB​ one​ N ÷ g {printf ("% d \ n", c + d); one​ }a semantic action appears that refers to the attributes of c and d associated with a rule in which c and d do not intervene. However, since there is only one rule in which N appears in the antecedent and also only one rule in which N appearsone              one in the consequent, it is clear that whenever an analyzer tries to reduce to N or              one invoke the recursive function that implements N, the last two tokensone that have been consumed will be c and d, so the semantic action can be executed working on the attributes of these last two tokens , although apparently there seems to be something wrong when you glance lightly at the definition Syntax-driven obtained after transformation of the translation scheme.When working with production rules (not recursive functions),we may run into some other problem. For example, if you have:A ÷ cd {A = c + d} Band it becomes:​ A ÷ cd NB​ oneN ÷ g {A = c + d}oneFigure 4.4\. Scheme of stack α after reducing by rule N 6 g. one The semantic action cannot access the attribute of A because A has not been reduced yet and has not it's on the stack87![](image_rsrcDDJ.jpg) 100Attributed grammarsthen in the stack you have what the figure illustrates 4 . 4 .In general, the use of attributes in a translation scheme obeysto common sense rules once the transformations that ametacompiler automatically applies to intermediate semantic actions, try LR parsers or recursive functions. In the case of bottom-up analysis things may not be so clear first thing and it is convenient to expose clearly what are these rules about the use of attributes in actions semantics of an LR schema :An attribute can only be used (in an action) behind the symbol to whichbelongs.The antecedent attribute can only be used in the action (of the end) of yourruler. Ex .:ORA ÷ cd NB {only here can the attribute of A be used}oneAnd N1 ÷ g {the attribute of A cannot be used here}In an intermediate action, you can only use the attributes of thesymbols that precede it. Following the previous examples, if a substitution of an intermediate action and you get:​ A ÷ cd N​ oneN ÷ gonesince in the action of N you can only make use of the attributes of c and d (whichone is what precedes it).All of this will be seen in later sections in greater detail.​ 4.2​ Syntax-driven translationFrom this section the translation of languages  guided byfree context grammars. Information is associated with each construction of the language programming by providing attributes to grammar symbols thatrepresent that construction. Attribute values  are calculated using rules Semantic (actions) associated with grammatical production rules.As we have seen, there are two notations for associating semantic rules withproduction rules:Syntax-driven definitions are high-level specifications fortranslations. The user does not need to explicitly specify the order in which the execution of the actions takes place.The translation schemes indicate the order in which thesemantic rules.Conceptually, both with syntax-driven definitions and withthe translation schemes, the component chain is parsed input lexicons, the parse tree is built, and then the tree to evaluate the semantic rules at its nodes. The evaluation of the rules88101![](image_rsrcDDK.jpg) semantics can generate code, save information in a symbol table, emit error messages or perform other activities, such as evaluating expressions arithmetic on a calculator. The translation of the lexical component string is the result obtained when evaluating the semantic rules.In practice, not all implementations have to follow the bottom line.letter this very clear scheme of one phase after another. There are special cases of Syntax-driven definitions that can be implemented in a single pass evaluating semantic rules during parsing, without constructing explicitly a parse tree or graph showing the dependencies between attributes.4.2.1 Syntax-driven definitionA syntax-driven definition is a context-free grammar in thethat each grammar symbol (terminal and nonterminal) has a set of attributes associated, divided into two subsets called synthesized attributes and attributes inherited from said grammar symbol. If each node of a tree is considered syntactic has associated a record with fields to store information, then an attribute corresponds to the name of a field.An attribute can store anything: a string, a number, a type,a memory location, etc. The value of an attribute at a node in a syntactic tree is defined by a semantic rule associated with the production rule used for get said node. The value of a synthesized attribute of a node is calculated from of the attribute values  of its child nodes in the syntactic tree; the value of a inherited attribute is calculated from the values  of the attributes in the nodes brothers and father. Each node in the tree represents an instance of a symbol grammatical and therefore has its own values  for each attribute.The semantic rules establish the dependencies between the attributes;dependencies that can be represented by a graph, once the tree is built full syntactic. An evaluation order is obtained from the dependency graph of all semantic rules. The evaluation of semantic rules defines the Attribute values  in the parse tree nodes for the string of entry. A semantic rule can also have side effects, such asExample display a value or update a global variable. As statedpreviously, in practice a translator does not need to explicitly build a tree parsing or a dependency graph; just produce the same result for each input string.A syntactic tree showing the attribute values  at each node isnames a decorated or annotated syntactic tree . The process of calculatingAttribute values  in nodes is called annotating or decorating the syntax tree .4.2.2 Formal outline of a definition directed by syntax![](image_rsrcDDM.jpg) 89102Attributed grammarsFormally, in a syntax-driven definition, each production ruleA ÷ αhas associated a set of semantic rules, each one of the form​ b: = f (c, c, ..., c)one​ two​ kwhere f is a function and, or:1.- b is a synthesized attribute of A and c, c, ..., c are attributes of theone              two              k α symbols, or2.- b is an attribute inherited from one of the symbols of α, and c, c, ..., c are

| attributes of the remaining symbols of α or A. | one | two | k |
| In any case, the attribute b is said to depend on the attributes c, c, ..., c.An attribute grammar is a syntax-driven definition in whichfunctions in semantic rules cannot have collateral effects.Semantic rule assignments are often written asexpressions. Occasionally the sole purpose of a semantic rule in a Syntax-driven definition is to create a side effect. Said semantic rules they are written as procedure calls or program fragments. Can beconsider as rules that define the values  of fictitious synthesized attributes of the nonterminal of the antecedent of the associated production rule, so that no show neither the dummy attribute nor the: = sign of the semantic rule.E.g. the syntax-driven definition of the table 4 .1 is for a translator whoimplements a calculator. For each production rule of E,T and F, the semantic rule calculates the value of the val attribute for the antecedent from the values  of the consequent attributes.The num lexical component has a synthesized attribute val_lex whose valueit is provided by the lexical analyzer. The rule associated with productionL ÷ E '\ n'where L is the initial axiom, it is just a procedure that visualizes as a result the value of the arithmetic expression generated by E; this rule can be considered defines a false attribute for the nonterminal L. We will also see an example with JavaCC but, for now, we'll continue to delve into syntax-driven definitions.In a syntax-driven definition, tokens are assumed to have onlysynthesized attributes, since the definition does not provide any semantic rules for they. The lexical analyzer is the one that generally provides the values  of the terminal attributes. Furthermore it is assumed that the initial symbol has no inherited attribute, unless otherwise indicated, as in many cases lack a father and siblings.4.2.2.1 Synthesized attributesSynthesized attributes are widely used in practice. A definitionsyntax-driven that uses exclusively synthesized attributes is called![](image_rsrcDDN.jpg) definition with synthesized attributes . With a definition with synthesized attributes you can decorate a syntactic tree by evaluating the semantic rules ascendingly, calculating the attributes from the leaf nodes to the root.For example, the definition with synthesized attributes of the table 4 . 1 specifiesa calculator that reads an input line containing an arithmetic expression thatincludes digits (we assume that the num token is associated with the pattern [0-9] +), parentheses, the + and * operators, followed by a carriage return character ('\ n'), and prints the value of the expression. For example, given the string "3 * 5 + 4" followed by a return of carriage, the system displays the value 19\. Figure 4 . 5 contains the syntactic tree decorated for the proposed entrance. Parsing tree with annotations to recognize "3 * 5 + 4 \ n".Although a syntax-driven definition says nothing about how to go91104Attributed grammarscalculating the attribute values, it is clear that a viable sequence must exist of calculations that allows such calculations. The corresponding semantic rule, F.val: = num.val_lex, sets the F.val attribute in the node to have the value 3 since the value of num.val_lex in the child of this node is 3\. Similarly, in the parent of this F, the attribute T.val has the value 3.Next consider the root node of the production T ÷ T * F. The valueof the T.val attribute in this node is defined by T.val: = T .val * F.val. When              one apply the semantic rule at this node, T .val has the value 3 and F.val the value 5\. Byone Therefore, T.val acquires the value 15 at this node.Finally, the rule associated with production for the initial nonterminal, L ÷ E \ n, displays the value of the expression represented by E, this is E.val.4.2.2.2 Inherited attributesAn inherited attribute is one whose value in a parse tree node![](image_rsrcDDP.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDDR.jpg) Syntactic is defined from the attributes of its father and / or its siblings. The Inherited attributes serve to express the dependency of a construction on a programming language in the context in which it appears. For example, you can use an inherited attribute to check if a handle appears on the side left or right of an assignment to decide if the address orthe value of the identifier.
