A translation scheme is like a syntax-driven definition, with the exception that the order of evaluation of semantic rules is explicitly shown. The position in which some action is executed is given in braces and is written on the right side of the rule of production. For example:98111![](image_rsrcDDX.jpg) A ÷ cd {printf (c + d)} BWhen designing a translation scheme, you must respect somelimitations to ensure that the value of an attribute is available when a action refers to him. These limitations, derived from the type of parsing chosen to build the tree, they ensure that the actions do not refer to a attribute that has not yet been calculated.The simplest example occurs when only synthesized attributes are needed.In this case, you can build the translation scheme by creating an action that consist of an assignment for each semantic rule and placing this action at the end of the right side of the associated production rule. For example, the production rule T ÷ T * F and the semantic action T.val: = T .val * F.val result in theone              one following production and semantic action:​ T ÷ T * F {T.val: = T .val * F.val}one​ oneIf you have both inherited and synthesized attributes, you should be moreprudent, so there are some rules to be followed when using the attributes of grammar symbols:1.- An inherited attribute for a symbol on the right side of a rule of Production must be calculated in an action before that symbol.2.- An action must not refer to a synthesized attribute of a symbol that be to the right of the action.3.- A synthesized attribute for the non-terminal on the left can only be calculate after all the attributes it does have been calculated reference. The action that calculates these attributes must be placed, usually at the end of the right side of the production.Well-defined translation schemes that meet these constraintsthey are said to be well defined . Example:D ÷ T {L.her = T.type} L;T ÷ int {T.tipo = "integer"}T ÷ real {T.type = “real”}L ÷ {L .her = L.her} L, id {add type (id.ptr_tbs, L.her)}one              one L ÷ id {add type (id.ptr_tbs, L.her)}4.2.4 LALR analysis with attributesIn this section we will study how the LALR (1) analysis with attributes works. Let us remember that the LALR (1) is capable of knowing at all times which rule to apply without margin of error (before a grammar that meets the LALR (1) constraints). EastThe fact is what is used to carry out actions safely. In theIn case of backtracking, it would be possible that after executing an action it is necessary to go back because we have realized that this rule was not correct. In such case, how undo an action ?. As this is impossible, in a backtracking analysis it is not99112Attributed grammars![](image_rsrcDDY.jpg) possible to execute actions at the same time as the analysis is done, but it would be necessary to first the sentence, and at a later stage execute the actions safely.4.2.4.1 Reduce / Reduce ConflictsThere are translation schemes that cannot be recognized by parsers LALR (1) directly. In particular, semantic actions are usually executed only when making a reduction, and never when moving. Therefore, a translation scheme with interspersed actions becomes another equivalent that it only contains actions at the end of the rule, as illustrated in the section 4.1.2 . TODespite this, this transformation can lead to situations that cease to be LALR (1), as illustrated in figure 4 .10 .Figure 4.10\. Grammar example that is not LALR (1) after having been transformed with epsilon rules.In this case there is a reduce / reduce conflict, because the grammar does notis LALR. As already indicated, a reduce / reduce conflict occurs when it arrives a moment of the analysis in which it is not known which rule to apply. In the above case, if the string "abcd" is entered, when the first two tokens have been consumed (“A” and “b”) we cannot know which rule g to apply, if U or V, since the pre- token search is "c" and this happens to both U and V. To solve this problemWe would need a LALR (3) analysis, since it is not enough to see the “c”; neither it is enough to see the "d"; it is necessary to go one character further, to the "e" or the EOF to know by which rule to reduce and, therefore, which action to apply.4.2.4.2 Move / reduce conflictsThere are more confusing cases, which occur in grammars that are ambiguous, that is,those in which a sentence can have more than one syntactic tree. Such is the case of a grammar as simple as:S ÷ aBadd S ÷ aCd÷ a÷ aaWhen reading the string "aaad", when the first two "a" and the token are consumedpre-search is the third “a”, it is not known whether to reduce the last “a” consumed to B or move to reduce next to C. It all depends on how many "d" you end with the chain which is still unknown.100113![](image_rsrcDDZ.jpg)Figure 4.11\. Ambiguity in two nested ifs in C: case a) else associated with the external if case b) else associated with the inner ifAn example of a grammar to which something similar happens is thecorresponding to the recognition of nested if statements in the C language.grammar is:S ÷ if COND then SS ÷ if COND then S else S S ÷ id = EXPRin which displace / reduce conflicts occur before an input such as:​ if COND then ifone​ COND then S elsetwo S​ one              twoas illustrated in the figure 4 . 11 .When a C compiler encounters a statement like this it opts forconstruct the second syntactic tree following the general rule “match each else with the closest previous unpaired then ”. This rule to remove ambiguities can be incorporated directly into the grammar by adding some additional production rules:​ S​ ÷ S Complete​ |​ SIncomplete​ SComplete​ ÷​ if COND then Scomplete else Scomplete101114Attributed grammars​ |​ id = EXPR​ SIncomplete​ ÷ if COND then S​ |​ if COND then S Complete else S Incompletethat always associate the else to the most immediate if. So a complete SC is either a if-then-else statement that does not contain incomplete statements or any other non-conditional statement class (such as an assignment). That is to say, inside an if with else only complete ifs are allowed, which makes all else's are grouped together before we start to recognize if's without else.​ 4.3​ The PCYacc parser generatorPCYacc is a metacompiler that accepts as input a grammar of![](image_rsrcDE0.jpg) free context, and outputs the finite automaton that recognizes the language generated by said grammar. Although PCYacc does not accept all grammars but only LALR ( LookAhead LR ), most programming languages  are they can express using such a grammar. It also allows associating actions to each production rule and assign an attribute to each grammar symbol(terminal or non-terminal). This makes it easy for the developer to create schematics translation into syntax-driven recognition. Since the automaton that generates PCYacc is written in C language, all semantic actions andNecessary declarations must be written by the developer in this same language.PCYacc starts from terminal symbols ( tokens ), which must be generatedby a lexicographic analyzer that can be implemented through PCLex, or ad hoc following some conventions imposed by PCYacc.4.3.1 Format of a Yacc programThe language in which the input programs to PCYacc must be writtenwe will call Yacc. Thus, a source program in Yacc has the following syntax:Definitions area%%Rules area%%Function areaTo illustrate a source program in YACC, we will start from the followinggrammar that allows you to recognize arithmetic expressions:E ÷ E + T | TT ÷ T * F | FF ÷ M (E) | M id | M num M ÷ - | gEach of these areas will be progressively described below.4.3.1.1 Definitions area102115This area is made up of two optional sections. The first of themis delimited by the symbols % { and %} and allows encoding ordinary declarations in C. Everything that is declared here will be visible in the rules and functions areas, for which is usually used to create the symbol table, or to include it ( #include ). The% { and %} symbols must necessarily start in column 0 (just like all the clauses that will be seen below)The second section allows you to declare the lexical components ( tokens ) usedin grammar. For this, the clause % token is used followed by a list ofterminals (without separating them by commas); following the example of point4.3.1 should to declare:% token ID NUMAlso, for the sake of readability, as many clauses can be used% token as needed; By convention, tokens are written in uppercase.Internally, PCYacc encodes each token as an integer,starting from 257 (since 0 to 255 are used to represent the charactersASCII, and 256 represents the error token ). Therefore the clause% token ID NUMIt is equivalent to![](image_rsrcDE1.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDE2.jpg) ​ # define ID no.​ one​ # define NUM no.​ twoObviously, the numbers or codes assigned by PCYacc to each token are always different.Notwithstanding the foregoing, not all terminals have to be listed inclauses % token . Specifically, terminals can be expressed in two ways:If its associated lexeme consists of a single character, and the parserlexicon returns it as is (its ASCII code), then they can be indicated directly in a production rule without declaring it in a clause % token . Example: '+'.Any other terminal must be declared in the declarations part, sothat the rules will refer to them based on their name (not code, which it's just an internal representation).Finally, in this area the initial axiom of thegrammar of the form:% start noTerminalIf this clause is omitted, PCYacc considers as the initial axiom the antecedent of the first grammar rule to appear in the rules area.4.3.1.2 Creating the lexical analyzerWe will create the lexical analyzers through the PCLex tool, andnot ad hoc , in order to facilitate the work. In the case of the example proposed in the point 4.3.1 , identifiers and numbers must be recognized, while the103116Attributed grammarsThe rest of the terminals, being made up of a single character, can be manipulated through its ASCII code. In this way, assuming that the blanks, 2 tabs and carriage returns act simply as separators, we have

| two main options:%%​ /* Option 1 */[0 - 9] +​ {return NUM; }[A- Z] [A - Z0 -9] * {return ID; }[b¯ / \ t \ n]​ {; } |  |
| .​ {return yytext [0]; }Y%%​ /* Option 2 */[0 - 9] +​ {return NUM; }[A- Z] [A - Z0 -9] * {return ID; }[b¯ / \ t \ n]​ {; } | / * Syntactic error. It passes the error to a.si. * / |
| '(' &#124; ')' &#124; '*' &#124; '+'​ {return yytext [0]; } | / * The a.si. only receive valid tokens * / |
| .​ {printf ("Lexical error. \ n"); } |
|  |  |

These options behave differently in the face of lexical errors,such as when the user types an '@' where a '+' is expected or any other operator. In Option 1, the parser receives the ASCII code of the '@' as token , due to the pattern . ; in this case the analyzer is, forTherefore, faced with an unexpected token , which leads him to abort the analysis by issuing a syntactic error message. In Option 2 the lexical analyzer protects the syntactic taking charge of emitting the error messages before the invalid lexemes; in![](image_rsrcDE3.jpg) in this case the error messages are considered lexicographical; furthermore, the developer You can decide whether or not to continue recognition by making use of the halt () function of C.In the following examples, we opt for Option 1, since it is the Least effort method and concentrates error handling on the analyzer syntactic.Finally, note that the Lex program does not include the main () function , sincePCYacc provides a syntax-driven model and it is to the parser thatmain control must be yielded, not the lexicon. Therefore, it will be the function area of the Yacc program who incorporates in main () a call to the parser, via the yyparse () function :% token ID NUM%%...%%void main () { yyparse (); twoWhite space will be represented by the character "b¯ &".104117}4.3.1.3 Rules areaThis is the most important area since it specifies the rules ofproduction that form the grammar whose language we want to recognize. Every rule grammatically have the form: nonTerminal: consistent;where consequent represents a sequence of zero or more terminals and not terminals.The set of production rules in our example would be written in Yaccas illustrated in Table 4 .5 where, by convention, symbols are not Terminals are written in lowercase and terminals in uppercase, arrows are replaced by ':' and, in addition, each rule must end in ';'​ Grammar​ Yacc notation%% / * rules area * /​ E ÷ E + T​ e:​ e '+' t​ |​ T​ | t;​ T ÷ T * F​ t:​ t '*' f​ |​ F​ |​ F;​ F ÷​ I )​ f:​ I ')'​ |​ M id​ |​ m ID​ |​ M num​ |​ m NUM;​ M ÷ g​ m:​ / * Epsilon * /​ |​ -​ |​ '-'![](image_rsrcDE4.jpg) ;Table 4.5 Correspondence between a formal grammar and its equivalent in Yacc notation.Along with the terminals and non-terminals of the consequent, can be included Semantic actions in the form of C code delimited by the symbols { and } .Example:e: e '+' t {printf ("This is a sum. \ n");};If there are several rules with the same antecedent, it is convenient to group themindicating this only once and separating each consequent by the symbol '|', with so just a semicolon at the end of the last consequent. Therefore the rules105118Attributed grammarsgrammatical:e: e '+' t {printf ("This is a sum. \ n"); };e: t​ {printf ("This is a term. \ n"); }; can be expressed in Yacc as:e: e '+' t {printf ("This is a sum. \ n"); }​ | t​ {printf ("This is a term. \ n"); };4.3.1.4 Function areaThe third section of a specification in Yacc consists of supporting routineswritten in C. Here a lexical analyzer must be provided using a function called yylex () . If necessary, other procedures can be added, such as support routines or compilation summary.The lexical analyzer yylex () produces pairs formed by a token and its valueassociated attribute. If a token is returned such as NUM, or ID, saidtoken must be declared in the first section of the specification in Yacc, The value of the attribute associated with a token is communicated to the parser through the variable yylval , as will be seen in point 4.3.2 . In case of integrating Yacc with Lex, in this section will have to make an #include of the file generated by PCLex.Specifically, this section must specify at least the functions:main () , which should start the parser by calling yyparse () .void yyerror (char * s) that receives a string that describes the error. In this moment the variable yychar contains the terminal that has been received and that Was expected.4.3.2 Attribute managementAs already indicated, a rule can include semantic actions,![](image_rsrcDE5.jpg) which are nothing more than a sequence of local declarations and statements in C language. But the true power of actions in Yacc is that each terminal or non-terminal an attribute can be associated with it. The attribute names are always the same and depend on the position that the symbol occupies within the ruler and not on the symbol in yes; in this way the name $$ refers to the attribute of the antecedent of the rule, while $ i refers to the attribute associated with the i-th grammar symbol of the consequent, that is:The antecedent attribute is $$ .erThe attribute of the 1 symbol of the consequent is $ 1 .The attribute of the 2nd symbol of the consequent is $ 2 .Etc.The semantic action will be executed as long as it is reduced by the rule ofassociated production, so normally it is in charge of calculating a value for106119$$ based on the $ i. For example, if we assume that the attribute of all symbols of our grammar (terminals and non-terminals) is of integer type, it would have:%%

| Øe: e '+' t | {$$ = $ 1 + $ 3;} |
| Ù &#124; t; | {$$ = $ 1;} |
| Út: t '*' f | {$$ = $ 1 * $ 3; } |
| Û &#124; F; | {$$ = $ 1; } |
| Üf: m '(' e ')' | {$$ = $ 1 * $ 3; } |
| Ý &#124; m ID | {$$ = $ 1 * $ 2; } |
| Þ &#124; m NUM | {$$ = $ 1 * $ 2; } |

;​ ßm:​ / * Epsilon * / {$$ = +1; }​ to​ |​ '-'​ {$$ = -1; };On the other hand, in the rules Ý and Þ of the previous example it can be seen that$ 2 refers to the attributes associated with ID and NUM respectively; but, being ID and NUM terminals, what values  do their attributes have? Who decides what values  to save in them?. The answer is that the lexical analyzer is the one who has to take care of assign a value to the attributes of the terminals. Remember that in the section 2.4.3.6 it was studied that PCLex provided the global variable yylval that allowed associate additional information with a token . Well, by means of this variable theThe lexical analyzer can communicate attributes to the syntactic. In this way, when the The lexical analyzer receives a string like "27 + 13 + 25", it should not only generate the token sequence NUM '+' NUM '+' NUM, it should also load the valueof the attribute of each NUM, and must do so before returning the token (before doing return NUM; ). In our case we will associate to each NUM the integer value that represents its lexeme, with the objective that the semantic actions of the syntactic can operate with it. The Lex pattern and the associated lexical action are: [0-9] + {yylval = atoi (yytext); return NUM; }The atoi () function converts from text to integer. Note that wheneverinvoke atoi () , the conversion will be successful, since the lexeme stored in yytext it cannot contain more than digits thanks to the associated pattern. Furthermore, it is assumed that the variable yylval is also integer.PCYacc needs some additional information in order to perform acorrect attribute management, that is, we have to inform you of the type of attribute that has associated each symbol of the grammar, whether terminal or non-terminal. For it The macro YYSTYPE must be defined that indicates what type all the attributes are; in other words, PCYacc assumes that all attributes are of type YYSTYPE , so that you have to give this macro a value. In this way, if we want all attributes are of type int , it would be enough to write![](image_rsrcDE6.jpg) % {#define YYSTYPE int107120Attributed grammars%}as a global declarations section in the definitions area. In fact, PCYacc forces a declaration like this to be included in Yacc programs even if attributes are used for nothing: PCYacc requires associating attributes always and know your type.However, it is clear that not all symbols in a grammarwill need attributes of the same type, being very common that numbers have associated an integer attribute, and that the identifiers have a pointer to a table of symbols that store important information about it. In this case, it can be declaredYYSTYPE as a union of C so that, although all symbols in the grammar will have as an attribute said union , each one will use only the field that specify. Although this method may be a bit wasted on memory, it allows for a Consistent management of the parsing stack, leading to greater efficiency.Furthermore, the mechanism of using a union as type of YYSTYPE is such a practice It is common for PCYacc to have a clause that allows defining YYSTYPE and declaring the union in one step. The clause has the form:%Union {/ * Body of a C union * /}Remember that a union in C allows you to define records with a variant part. Moreover, in a union everything is part variant, that is, all the fields declared in the body of a union share the same memory space, so that the union size is the largest field it contains. Continuing with our For example, the union would have the form:%Union {int number; node * ptrNode;}With this, YYSTYPE has become a union (no longer requiredinclude the #define YYSTYPE), so any symbol can doreference to the number field or the ptrNode field , but not both. As a rule, each grammar symbol, regardless of the rule in which it appears uses always the same field of % union ; therefore, PCYacc supplies a mechanism to indicate which of the fields is the one to be used for each symbol. With this in addition, we save continuous references to the % union fields . The mechanism consists of including angle brackets in the% token clause of the definitions the field to be used for each terminal of the grammar; forindicate the type of the non-terminal attributes, the % type clause will be used which, otherwise, it is equal to the % token except that it lists non-terminals instead of terminals. Following this notation, our example would be:%Union {int number;![](image_rsrcDE7.jpg) 108121node * ptrNode;}% token <number> NUM% token <ptrNode> ID% type <number> etfm%%

| Øe: e '+' t | {$$ = $ 1 + $ 3;} |
| Ù &#124; t; | {$$ = $ 1;} |
| Út:​ t '*' f | {$$ = $ 1 * $ 3; } |
| Û &#124; F; | {$$ = $ 1; } |
| Üf: m '(' e ')' | {$$ = $ 1 * $ 3; } |
| Ý &#124; m ID | {$$ = / * Extract some information from (* $ 2) * / ; } |
| Þ &#124; m NUM | {$$ = $ 1 * $ 2; } |

;​ ßm:​ / * Epsilon * / {$$ = +1; }​ to​ |​ '-'​ {$$ = -1; };Note how, even though YYSTYPE is of type union , $$ and $ i are notrefer to their fields, thanks to the fact that the % token and % type declarations make these references for us. Moreover, if at some point we use the attribute of some symbol and we have not specified its type in a % token or % type clause, PCYacc it will issue an error message during meta-compilation. These transformations are performed by PCYacc, so the lexical actions, managed by PCLex, when referenced to yylval must explicitly indicate the field to work with. HeLex program that implements the lexical analyzer of our grammar would be:​ %%​ /* Option 1 */​ [0 - 9] +​ {yylval.numero = atoi (yytext); return NUM; }[A- Z] [A - Z0 -9] * {yylval.ptrNode = ...; return ID;}​ [b¯ / \ t \ n]​ {; }​ .​ {return yytext [0]; }We will leave the management of the attribute associated with the user identifiersFigure 4.12\. Syntactic tree for recognize a string where no identifiers are involved.109![](image_rsrcDE8.jpg) 122Attributed grammarsfor the chapter dedicated to the symbol table. For now we will focus only on the numeric constants. The figure? shows the generated tree to recognize the string "27 + 13 + 25"; attribute values  appear as subscripts.4.3.2.1 Intermediate actionsIt is also possible to place actions in the middle of a rule, that is, between thesymbols of a consequent. However, it was previously said that PCYacc builds parsers that execute a semantic action every time it is reduced by its rule associated in the construction of a syntactic tree. So when does a intermediate action? In these cases, PCYacc performs a grammar conversion provides another equivalent in which the intermediate action appears to the right of a new rule of the form: N ÷ g, where i is an arbitrary number generated byiPCYacc. For example,A: B {$$ = 1; } C {x = $ 2; y = $ 3; };becomesNi: / * Epsilon * / {$$ = 1; };​ A: B Ni C​ {x = $ 2; y = $ 3;};The execution of semantic actions is subject to the mechanism seen in thepoint 4.1.2.4.3.3 AmbiguityPCYacc not only allows you to easily express a grammar to be recognized andgenerate your parser, but also provides tools that facilitate elimination of ambiguities.The grammar proposed in the section 4.3.1 is a device to forceproduct and division have higher priority than addition and subtraction, and the least unary is the highest priority operator. However, from the point of viewexclusively syntactic, the language generated by said grammar is much more easy to express using grammar​ and​ :​ e '+' e​ |​ e '-' e​ |​ e '*' e​ |​ e '/' e​ |​ '-' e​ |​ '(' e ')'​ |​ ID​ |​ NUM;which, as is evident, is ambiguous. For example, before the entry "27 * 13 + 25" can get the syntactic trees of figure 4 . 13 ; in case a) first it is multiplies and then adds, while in b) it first adds and thenmultiply. Since the product has priority over the sum, only case a) is valid from the semantic point of view, otherwise a result is obtained110123![](image_rsrcDE9.jpg) incorrect.Figure 4.13\. Syntactic trees that recognize a valid sentence according to a ambiguous grammar. Note how semantically results are produced different according to the order of evaluation. The correct option is a), since the product takes precedence over sum.​ This situation occurs because when the parser has expr.              27'*' expr. and encounters the '+', a shift / shrink conflict occurs: if10 displaces you get tree b), and if you reduce it you get tree a). This same problem would have occurred if you had two sums, instead of a product and a sum; analogously, a tree like a) would have been the correct one, since the sum is associative on the left. In general, if a rule is left recursiveimplies that the containing operator is left associative; and the same for the right. That is why the proposed grammar is ambiguous, since each rule is simultaneously recursive to the right and to the left, which implies that the operators are left and right associative at the same time, which does not have sense. Although most operators are left associative, alsothere is on the right, as is the case with the exponentiation operator. What's more, some operators are not even associative such as modulo operator (remainder) of a division.Although we have already seen that this problem can be solved by changing thegrammar, PCYacc provides a mechanism to resolve ambiguities caused by displace / reduce conflicts without the need to introduce changes in the proposed grammar. Unless instructed otherwise, PCYacc will resolve all conflicts in the parser action table using both following premises:A reduce / reduce conflict is resolved by choosing the conflicting rulethat was written first in the Yacc program.A shift / shrink conflict is resolved in favor of shift.In addition to these premises, PCYacc provides the developer with the possibilitysolving grammar conflicts by yourself. The solution comes from the hand of the precedence and associativity clauses: % left , % right , % nonassoc and % prec . 111124Attributed grammarsThe clauses % left , % right and % nonassoc allow to indicate the associativity of a operator ( left -left: shrink, right -right: move and nonassoc -not associative: error); Furthermore, the order in which the clauses are specified allows PCYacc to know which operators have higher priority over others. These clauses are placed in the definitions area, usually after the list of terminals. For example, clause % left '-'![](image_rsrcDEA.jpg) makes the rule e: e '-' e unambiguous. Note that for PCYacc there is no concept of operator (arithmetic or not), but in these clauses any symbol where a shift / shrink conflict occurs, whether terminal or not terminal.If multiple symbols are written to the same % left , % right, or % nonassocwe will be indicating that everyone has the same priority. A specification of the shape:% left '-' '+' % left '*' '/'indicates that '+' and '-' have the same priority and are left associative and than '*' and '/' too. Since '*' and '/' come after '-' and '+', this informs PCYacc that '*' and '/' have higher priority than '+' and '-'.There are situations in which the same terminal can represent severaloperations, as in the case of the sign '-' which can be the symbol of the subtraction or minus unary. Since it only makes sense for the '-' to appear in a single clause it is impossible to indicate that, when it is the least binary, the priority is lower than that of the product, while if it is unary the priority is higher. To solve this situation, PCYacc provides the % prec modifier which, placed next to the consequent of a rule, modifies the priority and associativity of the same. % prec must be accompanied by an operator previously listed in any of the clauses% left , % right, or % nonassoc , from which it inherits precedence and priority. In our example case could be written:% left '+' '-'% left '*' '/'%%​ and​ :​ e '+' e​ |​ e '-' e​ |​ e '*' e​ |​ e '/' e​ |​ '-' e​ % prec '*'​ |​ '(' e ')'​ |​ ID​ |​ NUM;indicating in this way that the unary minus rule has as much priority as the of the product and that, in case of conflict, is also associative on the left. Not112125![](image_rsrcDEB.jpg) However, this solution presents two problems: 1) the priority of the minus unary is not equal to the product, if not superior; and 2) the unary minus is not associative by the left, but on the right (although this would not give problems, since there is no ambiguity in the formation of the tree associated with a sentence such as “--- 5”, just as illustrates figure 4 .14 ).Figure 4.14\. Although the grammar is ambiguous, if there are several "minus unary" operators in a row, these can only be associated to the right.To solve these problems, one can resort to a device that consists ofdeclare a terminal representing a dummy operator of the priority and associativity required with the sole objective of using it later in a % prec .This new token will never be returned by the lexicographic analyzer. In addition, terminals listed in a % left , % right or % nonassoc statement is not necessary declare them also in the corresponding % token , unless they have an attribute of the % union . Thus, the final solution to the problem of priority and associativity is given by the Yacc program block:% left '+' '-'% left '*' '/'% right LESS_UNARY%%​ and​ :​ e '+' e​ |​ e '-' e​ |​ e '*' e​ |​ e '/' e​ |​ '-' e​ % prec MINUS_UNARY​ |​ '(' e ')'​ |​ ID​ |​ NUM;4.3.4 Error handlingPerhaps one of the biggest shortcomings of PCYacc is that oferror handling since, by default, when the parser that generates encounters a syntactic error, it simply stops execution with a sparse " syntax error " message .113126Attributed grammarsThis execution break can be avoided through the correct use of the tokenspecial mistake . Basically, if a syntactic error occurs the parser generated by PCYacc discards part of the analysis stack and part of the input that remains to be read, but only until a situation is reached where the error can be recovered, which it is achieved through the reserved token error . A typical example of token usage error is as follows:​ prog​ :​ / * Epsilon * /​ |​ prog sent ';'​ |​ prog error ';';​ sent​ :​ ID '=' NUM;The last rule of this piece of grammar is to tell PCYacc that![](image_rsrcDEC.jpg) between the non-terminal prog and the terminal ';' a syntactic error may appear. Suppose we have the sentence to recognize “a = 6; b = 7; c = 8; d = 6; " in the that a point has been "slipped" after the identifier c ; when the analyzer is the token '.' realizes there is a syntactic error and searches the grammar for all rules that contain the error symbol ; then it removes symbols from the the top of the stack, until the symbols above the stack match the located to the left of the error symbol in any of the rules in which it appears.It then removes tokens from the input until it finds one thatFigure 4.15\. Recovering from a syntactic error. "Recover" does not want say "correct" but be able to continue parsing114127matches the one just to the right in the selected error rule. AOnce done, insert the error token on the stack, scroll and continue with the analysis trying to reduce by that error rule. To the terminal just to the right of error in an error rule is called a security token , since it allows the analyzer to reach a safe condition in the analysis. As a security token usually choose the termination of sentence or declaration, as in our case, inthe one that we have chosen the semicolon. The figure4 .15 graphically illustrates the process followed for error recovery.Finally, for everything to work properly it is convenientassociate the invocation of a special PCYacc macro with the error rule named yyerrok :

| prog | : | / * Epsilon * / |  |
|  | &#124; | prog sent ';' |  |
|  | &#124;; | prog error ';' | {yyerrok; } |
| sent | : | ID '=' NUM |  |

;Running this macro informs the parser that therecovery was successful. If not invoked, the generated parser![](image_rsrcDED.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDEE.jpg) by PCYacc enters a state called "security condition", and will remain in that been up to having shifted three tokens . The safety condition prevents parser to emit more error messages (before new syntactic errors), and has for Aim to avoid cascading errors due to improper error recovery. The invocation of yyerrok takes the parser out of the safety condition, so that This will inform us of all, absolutely all, the syntactic errors, even the due to incorrect error recovery.Despite being able to recover syntactic errors, PCYacc does not generateanalyzers that correctly report the error, but are only limited toreport the existence of these but without specifying their nature. This lack can be correct by following the steps below:In the Yacc functions area, place the directive: #include "errorlib.c"and not include the yyerror (char * s) function , which is already hard-coded inside from the errorlib.c file .Compile the YACC program as:pcyacc -d -pyaccpar.c file.yac-d creates the file yytab.h that has a description of the stack of the parser and the code associated with each token .-pyaccpar.c causes a skeleton to be used to contain the finite stack automaton that implements PCYacc. Instead of taking the default skeleton, is taken to yaccpar.c which contains the 115128Attributed grammarsnew error handling functions. yaccpar.c makes use of its once, from the errorlib.h file .Run the programtokens.comThis program takes the file yytab.h , and generates the file yytok.h that is nothing more than the sequence of token names quoted and separated by commas. This file is used by errorlib.c to indicate what tokens were expected.Compile file.c (for example, with Turbo C):tc file.cIn this way, when file.exe is executed and an error occurssyntactic, you will get a clarifying error message:"Error in line ...: found the token ... and expected one of the tokens ..." where the ellipsis are automatically filled in by the analyzersyntax with the line of the error, the wrong token that caused it and the list of tokens that would have been valid, respectively.4.3.5 Final example![](image_rsrcDEF.jpg) Finally, joining all the Yacc code blocks that we have been seeing In the previous sections, the example of the calculator would be:

| % {#define YYSTYPE int%}% token NUMBER% left '+' '-'% left '*' '/'% right LESS_UNARY%%prog​ :​ / * Epsilon * / |  |
| ​ &#124;​ prog expr ';' | {printf ("=% d \ n", $ 2); } |
| ​ &#124;​ prog error ';'; | {yyerrok; } |
| expr​ :​ expr '+' expr | {$$ = $ 1 + $ 3; } |
| ​ &#124;​ expr '-' expr | {$$ = $ 1 - $ 3; } |
| ​ &#124;​ expr '*' expr | {$$ = $ 1 * $ 3; } |
| ​ &#124;​ expr '/' expr | {$$ = $ 1 / $ 3; } |

​ |​ '-' expr% prec LESS_UNARY {$$ = - $ 2; }

| &#124; | '(' expr ')' | {$$ = $ 2; } |
| &#124;; | NUMBER | {$$ = $ 1; } |

%%#include "lexico.c" void main () {yyparse (); } void yyerror (char * s) {printf ("% s \ n", s); }116129​ 4.4​ The Cup parser generatorCup is a LALR parser developed at the Institute ofGeorgia (USA) technology that generates Java code and allows you to entersemantic actions written in that language. Use a fairly similar notation to that of PCYacc and also based on production rules.4.4.1 Main differencesBy way of introduction, the main differences with PCYacc are:The antecedent of a rule is separated from the consequent by :: =, insteadby:.It is not possible to use simple characters (´ + ´, ´-´, ´, ´etc.) As terminals ina production rule, so all terminals must be declared explicitly: PLUS, MINUS, COMMA, etc.Instead of % token use terminal.Instead of % type use non terminal.The list of terminals must be separated with commas and end in a semicolon,like that of non-terminals. In both lists all the terminals and non-terminals, whether they have an attribute or not.If a terminal or non-terminal has an associated attribute, it cannot beprimitive type but, necessarily, it must be an object ( Object ). ByTherefore, instead of using int you should use Integer , instead of char you should use Character , etc.There is no % union , but the type of each terminal is directly indicated and non-terminal using the name of the corresponding class: Integer ,Character , MyRecord , etc. The use of brackets is also not necessary angular to indicate this type.In intermediate semantic actions, the attributes of the consequent thatpreceding the action can be used in read-only mode (as if they were from the final Java type ).![](image_rsrcDEG.jpg) Instead of % left , % right and % nonassoc are used, respectively precedence left, precedence right, and precedence nonassoc. However, the clause % prec is used the same as in PCYacc.Instead of % start , start with is used .There are no sections that should be separated by the %% symbols.You can use, as if it were a Java program, the clausesimport and package .117130Attributed grammarsBefore starting the execution of the generated analyzer, aJava code block using clause: init with {: Java_code_block:};To connect the generated analyze with the lexical analyzer, usethe clause: scan with {: java_block_quereturns_an_object_of_the_class_sym:};With these small differences we can see below how it would look The specification of our calculator:package first;import java_cup.runtime. *;/ * Initialization of the lexical analyzer (if necessary) * / init with {: scanner.init (); :};/ * Request from a terminal to the lexical analyzer. * / scan with {: return scanner.next_token (); :};/ * Terminals * /terminal PUNTOYCOMA, PLUS, MINUS, POR, BETWEEN, MODULE; terminal UMENOS, LPAREN, RPAREN; terminal Integer NUMBER;/ * Terminals * /non terminal expr_list, expr_part; non terminal Integer expr, term, factor;/ * Precedents * / precedence left MORE, LESS; precedence left BY, BETWEEN, MODULE; precedence left UMENOS;/ * Grammar * /

| expr_list |  | :: =​ expr_list expr POINTYCOM |
|  |  | &#124;​ / * Epsilon * /; |
| expr | :: = | expr MORE expr |
|  | &#124; | expr MINUS expr |
|  | &#124; | expr BY expr |
|  | &#124; | expr BETWEEN expr |
|  | &#124; | expr MODULE expr |
|  | &#124; | LESS expr% price UMENOS |
|  | &#124; | LPAREN expr RPAREN |
|  | &#124;; | NUMBER |

The Cup itself is written in Java, so for its execution, it is necessaryhave some JRE ( Java Runtime Environment ) installed . The main application comes represented by the java_cup.Main class , in such a way that if the example of the calculated is stored in a file called calculator.cup , it would be compiled from the shape:![](image_rsrcDEH.jpg) java java_cup.Main calculator.cupwhich produces the sym.java and parser.java files . As can be seen, Cup no118131respects the (widely accepted) convention of capitalizing classes.The sym.java class contains the necessary constants to be able to refer to the terminals: sym.MAS, sym.POR, etc., which makes the parser work easier lexicographic, which must return said terminals through sentences of the form: return new Symbol (sym.MAS); for example.To continue our calculator example, you need to include actionssemantics and to be able to make references to the attributes of both the symbols of the consequent, as of the antecedent nonterminal. Similar to how it happensWith Yacc, semantic actions are encapsulated between the {: and :} characters . Without However, the way of referring the attributes differs notably from the method used by Yacc.To begin with, the antecedent attribute is not called $$ , but RESULT ; of Similarly, the attributes of the consequent are not numbered but named, which it means that it is the developer himself who must decide what name to give it to the attribute of each symbol of the consequent. This name is indicated to Cup placing it after the terminal / non-terminal in the production rule and separated from it by the colon character. In this way, to a rule like:​ expr​ :: =​ expr PLUS expr;it would be given semantic meaning of the form:​ expr​ :: =​ expr: eIzq PLUS expr: eRch{: RESULT = new Integer (eIzq.intValue () +eDch.intValue ()); :};Following this mechanism, adding the rest of the semantic actions toour calculator example gets:import java_cup.runtime. *;/ * Initialization of the lexical analyzer (if necessary) * / init with {: scanner.init (); :};/ * Request from a terminal to the lexical analyzer * / scan with {: return scanner.next_token (); :};/ * Terminals without attribute * /terminal PUNTOYCOMA, PLUS, MINUS, POR, BETWEEN, MODULE; terminal UMENOS, LPAREN, RPAREN;/ * Terminals with associated attribute * / terminal Integer NUMBER;/ * Number of terminals without attribute * / non terminal expr_list;/ * Number of terminals with associated attribute * / non terminal Integer expr; / * Precedents * / precedence left MORE, LESS; precedence left BY, BETWEEN, MODULE; precedence left UMENOS;![](image_rsrcDEJ.jpg) 119132Attributed grammars/ * Grammar * / expr_list :: = expr_list expr: e POINT AND COMMA {: System.out.println ("=" + e); :}​ |​ EXPR_LIST POINT AND COME error​ |​ / * Epsilon * /;expr :: = expr: e1 PLUS expr: e2{: RESULT = new Integer (e1.intValue () + e2.intValue ()); :}| expr: e1 MINUS expr: e2{: RESULT = new Integer (e1.intValue () - e2.intValue ()); :}| expr: e1 BY expr: e2{: RESULT = new Integer (e1.intValue () * e2.intValue ()); :}| expr: e1 BETWEEN expr: e2{: RESULT = new Integer (e1.intValue () / e2.intValue ()); :}| expr: e1 MODULE expr: e2{: RESULT = new Integer (e1.intValue ()% e2.intValue ()); :} | NUMBER: n{: RESULT = n; :} | LESS expr: e% prec UMENOS{: RESULT = new Integer (0 - e.intValue ()); :} | LPAREN expr: e RPAREN{: RESULT = e; :};As has been hinted previously, for all this to work it isnecessary to interconnect it with a lexicographic analyzer that, before each terminalreturn an object of type java_cup.runtime.Symbol . Objects of this class have a value field of type Object that represents their attribute, and which the lexicographic analyzer must assign an object consistent with the specified class in the terminal declaration made in Cup.4.4.2 Complete syntax.A Cup file is made up of five main blocks that we will see atcontinuation.4.4.2.1 Package specification and imports.Under this first block the classes that are needed are optionally indicatedto import. You can also indicate the package to which you want to make the generated classes.4.4.2.2 User code.Cup encompasses a separate non-public class (included within the fileparser.java ) to all user-specified semantic actions. If he developer may want to include a java block inside this class using the statement:action code {: java_block:}Variables, functions, etc. can be declared in this block. all of type120![](image_rsrcDEK.jpg) 133static as there are no accessible objects by which to reference components not static. Everything that is declared here will be accessible to semantic actions.It is also possible to make Java declarations within the class itselfparser , by declaring:parser code {: java_block:}which is very useful to include the main () method that starts our application.The following two statements serve to communicate with thelexical analyzer. The first is:init with {: java_block:}and executes the indicated code just before making the request for the first token ; he The objective may be to open a file, initialize storage structures, etc.Finally, Cup can communicate with any lexical analyzer, sincethe declaration:scan with {: java_block:}lets you specify the code block that returns the next token on input.4.4.2.3 Symbol listsThis section lists all the terminals and non-terminals of thegrammar. You can have multiple lists of terminals and non-terminals, as long as these are not repeated. Each terminal list is preceded by the word terminal , and each list of non-terminals is preceded by the words non terminal . The elements Both lists must be separated by commas, ending with a semicolon.With these lists it is also possible to specify the attribute of each symbol, sinceterminal or not, knowing that these must necessarily inherit from Object , that is, they cannot be primitive values. To do this, symbols whose attribute type is matching can be grouped in the same list to which said right type will be associated behind the word terminal , of the form:terminal ClassName terminal1, terminal2, etc .;O wellnon terminal ClassName noTerminal1, noTerminal2, etc .;4.4.2.4 Associativity and precedence.This section has the same semantics as its counterpart in PCYacc, that is,allows to resolve conflicts move / reduce before certain terminals. This way:precedence left terminal1, terminal2, etc .;chooses to reduce rather than displace when encountering a conflict in which the next token is terminal1 or terminal2, etc. On the other hand:precedence right terminal1, terminal2, etc .;chooses to shift in the same cases. Finally:precedence nonassoc terminal1, terminal2, etc .;121134Attributed grammars![](image_rsrcDEM.jpg) would throw a syntactic error if encountering a shift / reduce conflict in analysis time.As many clauses of this type as considered necessary can be indicated,knowing that the order in which they appear causes it to be reduced first when finding the terminals of the last lists, that is, the lower a clause is foundprecedence, the more priority you will have when reducing for it. In this way, it is normal to find things like:precedence left ADD, SUBTRACT; precedence left MULTIPLY, DIVIDE;which means that, in case of ambiguity, MULTIPLY and DIVIDE have more priority than ADD and SUBTRACT, and that all operations are associative to the left. These kinds of constructions only make sense in ambiguous grammars, such as it could be:

| expr | :: = | expr MORE expr |
|  | &#124; | expr MINUS expr |
|  | &#124; | expr BY expr |
|  | &#124; | expr BETWEEN expr |
|  | &#124; | LPAREN expr RPAREN |
|  | &#124; | NUMBER |

;Symbols that do not appear in any of these lists are assumed to havelowest priority.4.4.2.5 Grammar.The last section of a Cup program is the grammar to be recognized, expressedthrough production rules that must end in a semicolon, and where the symbol :: = serves as an arrow.The grammar can start with a statement that says what the axiom isinitial. If this clause is omitted, it is assumed that the initial axiom is the antecedent of the first rule. Said declaration is made in the form:start with noTerminal;As in Yacc, it is possible to change the priority and precedence of arule that produces conflict move / reduce indicating the clause:% prec terminalnext to her.The production rules allow to house semantic actions inside them,which are delimited by the symbols {: and :} . In these actions you can place any block of Java code, being of special importance the accesses to the attributes of the rule symbols. The attribute of the antecedent is given by theJava RESULT variable , while the attributes of the consequent symbols are accessible by the names that the user has specified for each of the122135![](image_rsrcDEN.jpg) they. This name is indicated to the right of the symbol in question and separated from it by two points. Its use within the semantic action must be consistent with the type assigned to the symbol when it was indicated in the symbol list. Must be paid special Be careful when using intermediate semantic actions since, in this case, the attributes of the accessible symbols of the consequent may only be used in read, which usually forces to give them an initial value from Jflex. An example of this situation can be studied in the section 8.4.6 .On the other hand, and as with PCYacc, the special symbol errorallows error recovery using the panic mode method . In section7.4.4 proper error handling in Cup is illustrated in more detail.4.4.3 Execution of Cup.The Cup metacompiler itself is written in Java, so its executionit obeys the model of any Java program. The main class is java_cup.Main , and takes its input from standard input:java java_cup.Main <inputFileIt is also possible to specify some options to the metacompiler, from theshape:java java_cup.Main options <inputFilewhere the most interesting options are:-package packagename : the parser and sym classes will be located in the package indicated.parser parsername : the parser will be called parsername and not parser .symbols symbolName : the class that groups the symbols of the grammar it will be called symbolName and not sym .expect number : by default, Cup aborts metacompilation when encounters a reduce / reduce conflict. If you want the behavior is identical to PCYacc, that is, choose the first rule in order of appearance in case of such conflict, it is necessary to tell Cup exactly the number of reduce / reduce conflicts expected. It is indicated using this option.-progress : makes Cup inform as it processes the input grammar.-dump : produces a readable dump of the grammar, the states of the automatongenerated by Cup, and from the analysis tables. if you want to get only the analysis tables (used to resolve conflicts), use can be made of from the more concise option -dump_states which skips grammar and tables analysis.123136Attributed grammarsOn the other hand, for Cup to conveniently communicate with the analyzerlexical, it must implement the java_cup.runtime.Scanner interface , defined as: public interface Scanner { public Symbol next_token () throws java.lang.Exception;}Finally, for our application to work it is necessary to include aJava main () function that constructs an object of type parser , associates an object of type Scanner (which will perform the lexicographic analysis), and start the process by invoking to the parser () function within a try-catch statement . This can be done from the shape: parser code {:public static void main (String [] arg) {![](image_rsrcDEP.jpg) / * Create a parser object * / parser parserObj = new parser (); / * Assign the Scanner * /Scanner myAnalyzerLexico = new Yylex (new InputStreamReader (System.in));parserObj.setScanner (myLexicScanner);try { parserObj.parse ();} catch (Exception x) {System.out.println ("Fatal error.");}}:};assuming that Yylex is the class that implements the lexical analyzer.4.4.4 Communication with JFlexJFlex incorporates the % cup option which is equivalent to the clauses:% implements java_cup.runtime.scanner% function next_token% type java_cup.runtime.Symbol% eofval { return new java_cup.runtime.Symbol (sym.EOF);% eofval}% eofcloseIf the user has changed the name of the class that groups the symbols (fordefault sym ), you must also use the directive in JFlex:% cupsym symbolNamefor % cup to generate the code correctly.As can be seen from the previous clauses, the lexicographic analyzerit must return the tokens to the syntax in the form of objects of class Symbol . Is class, which is in the java_cup.runtime package and therefore must be imported by the lexicographic, it has two constructors:Symbol (int n). Making use of the integer constants defined in the class124137sym , this constructor allows you to create objects without an associated attribute.Symbol (int n, Object o). Analogous to the previous one, but with one parameter additional that constitutes the value of the attribute associated with the token to be returned.In this way, the lexical analyzer necessary to recognize the grammarCup that implements our calculator, would be given by the following JFlex program:import java_cup.runtime. *;%%% unicode% cup% line% column![](image_rsrcDER.jpg) %%

| "+" | {return new Symbol (sym.MAS); } |
| "-" | {return new Symbol (sym.MENOS); } |
| "*" | {return new Symbol (sym.POR); } |
| "/" | {return new Symbol (sym.ENTRE); } |
| "%" | {return new Symbol (sym.MODULE); } |
| ";" | {return new Symbol (sym.POINTYCOMA); } |
| "(" | {return new Symbol (sym.LPAREN); } |
| ")" | {return new Symbol (sym.RPAREN); } |
| [: digit:] +[\ t \ r \ n] + {;} | {return new Symbol (sym.NUMBER, new Integer (yytext ())); } |

125138![](image_rsrcDES.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDET.jpg) Attributed grammars126139Chapter 5JavaCC​ 5.1​ IntroductionJavaCC ( Java Compiler Compiler - Metacompiler in Java) is the mainmetacompiler in JavaCC, both for its capabilities and for its scope of diffusion. It is a tool that facilitates the construction of analyzerslexical and syntactic by the method of recursive functions, although it allows a relaxed notation very similar to BNF. In this way, the parsers generated They use the descending technique when obtaining the syntactic tree.5.1.1 General characteristicsJavaCC integrates the lexicographic analyzer and thesyntactic, and the code it generates is independent of any external library, which gives it an interesting property of independence from the environment. TO broadly speaking, its main characteristics are the following:Generates top-down parsers, allowing the use of grammars fromgeneral purpose and the use of attributes both synthesized and inherited during the construction of the syntax tree.The lexical and syntactic specifications are located in a single file. Of this way the grammar can be read and maintained more easily. Not However, when introducing semantic actions, we recommend using certain comments to improve readability.![](image_rsrcDEU.jpg) Supports the use of lexical states and the ability to add lexical actionsincluding a block of Java code after a token identifier.It incorporates different types of tokens: normal (TOKEN), special(SPECIAL_TOKEN), spacers (SKIP), and continuation (MORE). It allows you to work with clearer specifications, while allowing a better handling of error and warning messages by JavaCC in meta-compilation time.Special tokens are ignored by the generated scanner, but areavailable to be processed by the developer.The lexical specification can define tokens in such a way that they are notdifferentiate uppercase from lowercase either globally or in a concrete pattern.It adopts its own BNF notation by using its own symbols127140JavaCCof regular expressions, such as (A) *, (A) +.Generates an LL (1) parser by default. However, there may be portions of the grammar other than LL (1), which is solved in JavaCC through the ability to resolve displace / displace ambiguities locally at the point of conflict. In other words, allow the a.si. I know transform into LL (k) only at such points, but LL (1) is conserved at the rest of the rules improving efficiency.Among the top-down parser generators, JavaCC isone of those with better error handling. The generated analyzers by JavaCC they are able to locate exactly the location of the errors, providing comprehensive diagnostic information.Allows Unicode-encoded inputs, so specificationsLexicals can also include any Unicode character. This facilitates description of language elements, such as Java identifiers that allow certain Unicode characters that are not ASCII.Allows debugging both the generated parser and the lexicographic,using the DEBUG_PARSER, DEBUG_LOOKAHEAD, and DEBUG_TOKEN_MANAGER.JavaCC offers many different options to customize yourbehavior and behavior of the generated parsers.Includes the JJTree tool, a preprocessor for tree developmentwith very powerful features.Includes the JJDoc tool that converts grammar files intodocumentation files.It is highly efficient, which makes it suitable for professional environments and hasbecome one of the most widespread metacompilers (perhaps the one that more, above JFlex / Cup).JavaCC is currently being maintained by the java.net code groupopen, and official documentation can be found on the site![](image_rsrcDEV.jpg) https://javacc.dev.java.net . Unfortunately, such documentation is too formal and does not incorporate any tutorial learning, although it is the best source of information once slightly familiar with the tool, what is the goal of this section.5.1.2 Preliminary example.As with Cup, we will start our journey with an examplepreliminary information on the structure of a JavaCC input program and the notation used for this. The name "JavaCC" will be used to refer to both the JavaCC tool as well as the language it supports to describe grammars.128141So our example written in JavaCC is: options {LOOKAHEAD = 1; }PARSER_BEGIN (Example) public class Example { public static void main (String args []) throws ParseException { Example myParser = new Example (System.in); myParser .exprlist ();}}PARSER_END (Example)SKIP: {"b¯ /"​ |​ "\ t"​ |​ "\ n"​ |​ "\ r"}TOKEN [IGNORE_CASE]: {<ID: (["a" - "z"]) +>​ |​ <NUM: (["0" - "9"]) +>}void listExpr (): {} {(BasicExpr () ";") +} void BasicExpr (): {} {LOOKAHEAD (2)<ID> "(" expr () ")"​ |​ "(" expr () ")"​ |​ "@NEW" <ID>​ |​ <ID> "." <ID>}void expr (): {} {"@SOMETHING"​ |​ <NUM>}JavaCC programs are usually stored in files with a .jj extension . So,the previous example would be stored in the file Example.jj , so that the metacompilation is done by invoking the batch file javacc.bat (which in turn invokes the javacc.class class from the javacc.jar file ) like this:javacc Example.jjwhich will produce the output files:![](image_rsrcDEW.jpg) Example.java: is the parser.Example TokenManager.java: This is the lexicographic analyzer.ExampleConstants.java: interface that associates a code with each token.129142JavaCCLikewise, it stores the name given by the developer in the form of String.to each token, so that the parser can issue messages clear and explanatory errors.in addition to the necessary classes:ParseException.java: class that implements syntactic errors. When the parser encounters one of these errors throws an exception which can be captured at any ancestor node of the syntactic tree that it was being built.SimpleCharStream.java: class that implements the input channels of the which the lexical analyzer can read. Incorporate constructors to convert the traditional type channels: InputStream and Reader .Token.java: class that implements the object through which they communicate the lexical and syntactic analyzer.TokenMgrError.java: class that implements lexicographical errors. East type of bugs cannot be caught easily, so the developermust collect all possible lexemes and generate his own errors before the incorrect.if they did not already exist in the directory from which it is metacompiled. The process can seen in figure 5\. 1 . To compile all the files produced by JavaCC just compile the main output file ( Example.java according to the figure) who produces the chain compilation of all the others.Figure 5.1\. Metacompilation process with JavaCC. Starting from a single file ( Example.jj ), JavaCC produces 3 dependent files and 4 others that are always identical.​ 5.2​ Structure of a program in JavaCCAs can be seen in the proposed example, the basic structure of a JavaCC program is:options {Options area}PARSER_BEGIN (ClassName)Java compilation unit with class name Classname![](image_rsrcDEX.jpg) 130143PARSER_END (ClassName)Area tokensBNF function areaThe options area allows you to specify some guidelines to help you JavaCC to generate lexical-parsers that are well more efficient, well more adapted to the specific needs of the developer. In the example we have indicated that, by default, the indicated grammar is of type LL (1), except if, at some point, indicates otherwise.The PARSER_BEGIN and PARSER_END clauses are used to tell JavaCC the name of our main class, as well as to encompass both this and to any others who want to include support, such as, for example, a manager of symbol tables. In the example it can be seen that the main class constitutes the parser itself as the main () function creates an object of type Example while passing as a parameter in the constructor the source you want consume input: keyboard ( System.in ).The class created by JavaCC will incorporate a function for each non-terminal of therules area. Each function will be responsible for consuming the part of the input that it underlies its associated nonterminal in the recognition syntax tree. By Therefore, assuming that the initial axiom is listExpr , a call of the form myParser.exprlist () will consume all the input, if it is acceptable.The following two areas can be mixed, although the most common is usuallyindicate first the tokens and finally the rules in BNF, especially for reasons clarity in the code.In the example, tokens of two types have been indicated . The tokens grouped underthe SKIP clause are those that will be consumed without being passed to the parser syntactic; in our case they are: space, tab, carriage return (CR- Carry Return ) and line feed (LF- Line Feed ).The tokens under the TOKEN clause constitute the normal tokens , althoughthe developer can also indicate this type of tokens in the BNF rules themselves, as with the patterns "(", ")", ";", etc. The declaration of each token is grouped in angle brackets and consists of the name of the token followed by the pattern associated and separated from it by two points. The lexicographic patterns are described similar to PCLex. The example illustrates the recognition of an identifier made up of letters only (either uppercase or lowercase thanks to the modifier [IGNORE_CASE] of the TOKEN clause ) and an integer.The last area of  the example illustrates the creation of three non-terminals and their rulesAssociated BNF. Since each nonterminal is going to be converted by JavaCC into a Java function, its declaration is identical to that of said function and is subject to the same restrictions than any other function in Java. The body of each of these functions will be built by JavaCC and will have as its purpose the proper consumption131![](image_rsrcDEY.jpg) 144JavaCCof tokens based on the BNF expression indicated in the specification.The BNF notation used makes use of the symbol * which represents repetition0 or more times, + for repetition 1 or more times, | for optionality, parentheses to group, etc. The terminals can be indicated in two ways, either by placing between angle brackets any of those declared in the previous phase, or indicating their pattern directly if it consists of a constant string of characters(Actually, JavaCC allows any regular expression, but this is not useful forour purposes). In the example, it is worth noting the inclusion of the "@" character in the ad hoc terminals NEW and ALGO , since otherwise they would be considered identifiers by matching the token ID pattern . The "NEW" and "ALGO" patterns would have been correctly recognized if the token ID had been declared after the appearance of these in the BNF rules.Each of these will be studied in greater detail below.sections.5.2.1 Options.This section begins with the options keyword followed by a listof one or more options in braces. The entire section can be omitted as it does not is mandatoryOptions can be specified in both the grammar file andon the command line. The same option should not be set more than once, although in the case that they are specified in the command line, they have greater priority than those specified in the file.Option names are capitalized, ending in ";" andThey are separated from the value associated with them by the "=" sign. Below are detailedbriefly the most important options and the functions they perform:LOOKAHEAD : indicates the amount of tokens that are taken into account by the parser before making a decision. The default value is 1 lo that will perform LL (1) analysis. The smaller the number of tokens of pre-search, the faster the analyzes will be performed. This value can be modify in certain specific rules.CHOICE_AMBIGUITY_CHECK - Used for JavaCC to providemore accurate error messages when you come across multiple options with a prefix of length equal to or greater than LOOKAHEAD . In theseIn these cases, JavaCC usually reports that an error has been found and advises that a lookahead equal to or greater than a certain number n is tested . YesCHOICE_AMBIGUITY_CHECK is k , so JavaCC will tryfigure out the exact lookahead we need (forinform us of it) as long as it does not exceed the k value . TheUsing this option provides more accurate information, but at the cost132145![](image_rsrcDEZ.jpg) longer meta-compilation time. Its default value is 2.FORCE_LA_CHECK : one of the main problems that can be encountered JavaCC in the construction of the parser lies in the existence of common prefixes in different options of the same rule, which makes it vary the number of pre -search tokens required to be able to choose one of these options. When the LOOKAHEAD option takes the value 1, JavaCC performs error checking by prefix checks on alloptions where the developer has not indicated a LOOKEAHEAD local, reporting on screen of such situations. If LOOKAHEAD is greater than 1, then JavaCC does not perform such control on any option. Yes is given to FORCE_LA_CHECK the value true (defaults to false ), the prefix check will be performed on all options including those have local LOOKEAHEAD , even though this is correct.STATIC : if the value is true (default value), all methods and variables are create as static both in the lexical analyzer and in the syntactic what notably improves its execution. However, this makes it only possible perform an analysis each time and prevents, simultaneously, different objects of the same class can process different input sources. Yes is defined as static, the same object can process different sources sequentially, invoking ReInit () every time you want to start with a new. If it is not static, different objects can be constructed (with new ) to process each source.DEBUG_PARSER : This option is used to obtain information from Run-time debugging of the generated parser. hisdefault value is false , although the trace of actions and decisions taken can be turned on or off by program by invokingenable_tracing () and disable_tracing () respectively. In a similar way the DEBUG_LOOKAHEAD options can be used , andDEBUG_TOKEN_MANAGER .BUILD_PARSER : tells JavaCC whether or not to generate the parser class syntactic. Its default value is true , as is the option BUILD_TOKEN_MANAGER , which causes both to be generated analyzers.IGNORE_CASE : if its value is true , the lexicographic analyzer ( token manager ) generated does not differentiate between uppercase letters and lowercase when it comes to recognizing lexemes. It is especially useful in the recognition of languages  such as HTML. Its default value is false .COMMON_TOKEN_ACTION : if the value of this option is true , all Invocations to the getNextToken () method of the parser class lexicographic, cause a call to the CommonTokenAction () method after the recognition of each lexeme and after executing the action133146JavaCCassociated lexical, if any. The user must define this last method in the TOKEN_MGR_DECLS section of the token area . The default value is false .![](image_rsrcDF0.jpg) UNICODE_INPUT : if set to true , a capable lexical analyzer is generated to handle a Unicode-encoded entry. By default it is false what assumes that the input will be in ASCII.OUTPUT_DIRECTORY : is an option whose default value is the directory current. Controls where the output files are generated.5.2.2 Token areaThis area allows the construction of a lexicographic analyzer according to theparticular needs of each project. In JavaCC's own jargon the Lexical analyzers are called token managers .JavaCC differentiates four types of tokens or terminals, depending on whatyou must do with each lexeme associated with them:SKIP: ignore the lexeme.MORE: searches for the next lexeme but concatenating it to the alreadyrecovered.TOKEN: obtains a lexeme and creates an object of type Token that returns to parser (or whoever invoked it). This return occursautomatically without the developer having to specify any statement return on no lexical action.SPECIAL_TOKEN: same as SKIP but stores lexemes in such a waythat can be recovered if necessary. It can be useful whenconstruct source-source translators in such a way that they want to preserve the comments even though they do not influence the translation process.Once a lexeme has been found, whatever its type, it is possible to run a Lexical action.The format of this phase is as follows:TOKEN_MGR_DECLS: {java block}<LexicalState> TOKEN [IGNORE_CASE]: {<token1: pattern1> {LexicalAction1}: newLexicalState1​ |​ <token2: pattern2> {LexicalAction2}: newLexicalState2​ |​ ...}<LexicalState> TOKEN [IGNORE_CASE]: {<token1: pattern1> {LexicalAction1}: newLexicalState1134147​ |​ <token2: pattern2> {LexicalAction2}: newLexicalState2​ |​ ...}...where:The word TOKEN can be replaced by SKIP, MORE, or SPECIAL_TOKEN.The [IGNORE_CASE] clause is optional but, if set, they should notforget the brackets.The pattern can be preceded by a token name and separated from it by colon, but it is not required (for example, in separators it would not have sense).![](image_rsrcDF1.jpg) If the pattern is a quoted constant without associated token, they can the angle brackets that surround it and, therefore, the name should be omitted.Lexical states are any identifier, preferably inuppercase, or the word DEFAULT representing the lexical stateinitial. They can be omitted. A list of lexical statements may also be indicated separated by commas.If the lexical state is omitted before the word TOKEN, it means that theGrouping patterns can be applied in any lexical state.If the lexical state is omitted after a pattern, it means that, after finding alexeme that fits with it, will remain in the same lexical state.Lexical actions are made up of Java code and are optional.Lexical actions have access to everything that has been declared withinthe optional clause TOKEN_MGR_DECLS.Lexical actions are executed within a token manager after recover the lexeme in question.JavaCC follows the same premises as PCLex when looking for lexemes,that is, always look for the longest possible lexeme and, if it fits For two patterns, go for the one that appears first.If JavaCC finds a lexeme that does not match any pattern it produces aLexical error. This situation should be avoided by the developer by using recognition of erroneous lexemes and the issuance of the corresponding Error message.5.2.2.1 Special characters for JavaCC patternsCharacters that have a special meaning when formingregular expressions are:"": Used to delimit any string of characters.<>: used to reference a previously defined token . Sometimes it can be interesting to create tokens with the sole purpose of using them within others, such as:TOKEN:{135148JavaCC<LITERAL_COMA_FLOTANTE:(["0" - "9"]) + "," (["0" - "9"]) * (<EXPONENT>)? (["f", "F", "d", "D"])?| "." (["0" - "9"]) + (<EXPONENT>)? (["f", "F", "d", "D"])?| (["0" - "9"]) + <EXPONENT> (["f", "F", "d", "D"])?| (["0" - "9"]) + (<EXPONENT>)? ["f", "F", "d", "D"]>|<EXPONENT: ["e", "E"] (["+", "-"])? (["0" - "9"]) +> }![](image_rsrcDF2.jpg) The problem with this example is that the lexical analyzer can recognize the string "E123" as being of type EXPONENT , when actually it should be an ID. In situations like this, the EXPONENT token can be declared as (note the use of the "#" character): < # EXPONENT: ["e", "E"] (["+", "-"])? (["0" - "9"]) +> which tells JavaCC that it is not a full-fledged token , but that you should use it as a model of use in those other patterns that reference.*: indicates repetition 0 or more times of what precedes it in parentheses. Ex .: (“Ab”) * matches g, “ab”, “abab”, etc. The pattern "ab" * is incorrect since that the symbol "*" must be preceded by a pattern in parentheses.+: indicates repetition 1 or more times of what precedes it in parentheses.?: indicates that what precedes it in parentheses is optional.|: indicates optionality. Ex .: ("ab" | "cd") matches either "ab" or "cd".[]: used to express lists of characters. Inside may appear:Single characters enclosed in quotation marks and separated by commas.Eg: ["a", "b", "c"] that will match the lexemes "a", "b" or "c".Character ranges made up of two single characters betweenquotes and separated by a hyphen. Ex .: ["a" - "c"] that will match the lexemes "a", "b" or "c".Any combination of the above. Ex .: ["a" - "z", "A" - "Z", “Ñ”, “Ñ”] that will match any Spanish letter without signs diacritics (accents or umlauts).~ []: serves to express a list of characters complementary to the one given as seen above for []. For example, the ~ [] pattern is a way to emulate the pattern (. | \ n) of PCLex.JavaCC always automatically creates the <EOF> token that matches theend of file character.On the other hand, there is no lexical statement declaration area, as inPCLex or JFlex, but these are used directly. A simple example for recognizing non-nested Java or C style comments would be:136149SKIP: {"/ *": InsideComment}<InComment> SKIP: {"* /": DEFAULT}<InComment> MORE: {<~ []>}5.2.2.2 Accessible elements in a lexical actionThe developer can associate lexical actions to a pattern that will beexecuted by the lexical analyzer every time it finds a chord lexeme. In these actions, some useful variables are available, such as:image : StringBuffer type variable that stores the current lexeme. A copy of said lexeme is also stored in the token field of the variable matchedToken . If the last accepted token was of type MORE , then image will accumulate the current lexeme to what it already contains. E.g. if we have:![](image_rsrcDF3.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDF4.jpg) <DEFAULT> MORE: {

| "to" {}<S1> MORE: { | Ø;}: S1 |
