before the input "abcd", the variable image will have the values  "a", "ab", "aB" and “aBcd” at points Ø, Ù, Ú and Û of the code respectively.lenghtOfMatch : variable of type integer that indicates the length of the last lexeme recovered. Following the previous example, this variable would take the values 1, 1, 1 and 2 at points Ø, Ù, Ú and Û of the code respectively. The length full image can be calculated via the length () method of the class StringBuffer .curLexState : variable of type integer that indicates the current lexical state. The Lexical states can be manipulated directly through the name that the developer finds them given, since JavaCC initializes the correspondingJava constants in one of the files it generates ( ExampleConstants.java according to the example in section 5.1.2 ).matchedToken : Token type variable that stores the current token . Following 137150JavaCCthe previous example, matchedToken.token would take the values  "a", "ab", "ab" and “abcd” at points Ø, Ù, Ú and Û of the code respectively.switchTo (int estadoLéxico) : function that allows changing by program to a new lexical state. If this function is invoked from a lexical action it must be the last thing it runs. Obviously, for it to be effective, it must not use the clause : newLexicalState1 after the lexical action. This function can be very useful in recognition, p. eg from nested comments:TOKEN_MGR_DECLS: { int numComments = 0;}SKIP: {"/ *" {numComments = 1;}: InsideComment}<InComment> SKIP: {"/ *" {numComments ++;}|​ "* /" {numComments--; if (numComments == 0) switchTo (DEFAULT); }​ |​ <(~ [\ * \\ b¯ / \ t \ n \ r]) +>​ |​ <[\ * \\ b¯ / \ t \ n \ t]>}Lexical actions can also use whatever was declared inthe optional clause TOKEN_MGR_DECLS . the following example displays the length of a quoted string using various patterns anda common variable longString (note the importance of the order of the two last patterns since otherwise the quotation marks would never be recognized.closing):TOKEN_MGR_DECLS: {int longString;}MORE: {![](image_rsrcDF5.jpg) "\" "{longString = 0;}: InsideString}<IntroString> TOKEN: {<STRLIT: "\" "> {System.out.println (" Size = "+ longString);}:DEFAULT}<IntroString> MORE: {<~ ["\ n", "\ r"]> {longString ++;}}If COMMON_TOKEN_ACTION option is true it must be specified The TOKEN_MGR_DECLS clause is mandatory, which, as a minimum, must contain the CommonTokenAction () function , whose header is:void CommonTokenAction (Token t)and that it will be invoked every time any lexeme is recovered regardless of its type, such and as seen in the section 5.2.1 .1381515.2.2.3 The Token class and the token managerAs already mentioned, each time the lexical analyzer finds alexeme corresponding to a normal token , returns an object of class Token . The variables and main functions of this class are:image : String type variable that stores the recognized lexeme.kind : for each pattern to which the developer associates a name, JavaCC includes an integer constant in the class of constants it generates. The kind variable stores one of these integers, specifically the name of the pattern by which his lexeme fitted.beginLine , beginColumn , endLine and endColumn : variables of type integer indicating, respectively, the line and column of the beginning and end of the lexeme.specialToken : Token type variable that refers to the previous token if and only if it was of a special kind; otherwise it will store the null value .Finally, indicate that JavaCC provides in the parser classLexical ( ExampleTokenManager according to the example of point5.1.2 ) two methods that They can be interesting, if you want to build just that parser, namely:A constructor that takes an object of type CharStream from the which will take the input to analyze.The getNextToken () function that returns the following object of type Token found at the entrance. Developer must control token type <EOF> to not consume beyond the end of the input.The following example illustrates a JavaCC program that is intended onlyemploy the lexical analyzer part. Although the BUILD_PARSER option is to false , JavaCC forces to indicate the PARSER_BEGIN and PARSER_END clauses , and a class of the same name inside, even if it's empty.options {/ * Do not generate the class Example * /BUILD_PARSER = false; }PARSER_BEGIN (Example)![](image_rsrcDF6.jpg) public class Example {}PARSER_END (Example)/ * Declaration of the main () function in the token manager * /TOKEN_MGR_DECLS: {public static void main (String args []) throws ParseException {Example TokenManager myLexer = new ExampleTokenManager (new SimpleCharStream (System.in));Token t; while ((t = myLexer.getNextToken ()). kind! = ExampleTokenManager.EOF)139152JavaCCSystem.out.println ("You have written" + t.image); }}SKIP: {""| "\ t"| "\ n"​ |​ "\ r"}TOKEN [IGNORE_CASE]: {<ID: (["a" - "z"]) +>}In this way, the main () function is included directly in the tokenmanager through the TOKEN_MGR_DECLS clause , and in it we build a input channel of the type expected by the lexical analyzer, to whom we pass it on its builder. The input channels that the token manager can read are from type CharStream ; JavaCC provides the SimpleCharStream class that inherits from CharStream to facilitate the creation of acceptable channels to be analyzed lexicographically. SimpleCharStream is a wrapper or stratum for classesInputStream and Reader having constructors that take objects as parameters of either of these two types.Once this is done, the tokens are recovered one by one displayingthe corresponding message on the screen. The same effect could have been obtained typing:options {BUILD_PARSER = false; }PARSER_BEGIN (Example) public class Example {} PARSER_END (Example)TOKEN_MGR_DECLS: {public static void main (String args []) throws ParseException { new ExampleTokenManager (new SimpleCharStream (System.in)). getNextToken ();}}SKIP: {""| "\ t"| "\ n"| "\ r"}SKIP [IGNORE_CASE]: {<ID: (["a" - "z"]) +> {System.out.println ("You have written" + image); }}much more compact and where the modifications with respect to the previous equivalent.![](image_rsrcDF7.jpg) 5.2.3 BNF function areaAs already mentioned, JavaCC generates a parser140153descending implemented based on recursive functions, so that each no terminal of our grammar will be converted to a different function, whose implementation will be generated by JavaCC.This area has the following structure: typeReturn functionJava (param):

| one​ {codigoJava}​ one​ {exprBNF}​ one | one | one |
| typeReturn functionJava (param):two​ {codigoJava}​ two​ {exprBNF}​ two... | two | two |

Since each nonterminal will be converted to a function in Java, thedeveloper must not only indicate his name, but the full header of said function. This fact is of fundamental importance since:«JavaCC allows the exchange of attributes between BNF rules by means of the passing parameters and obtaining results at the time of use of a non-terminal (or what is the same, invoke the function that implements) which is equivalent, respectively, to sending attributes down and up in the syntactic tree. 'On the other hand, the BNF expressions exprBNF can make use of theifollowing components:noTerminal () : represents the use of the non-terminal noTerminal . Note the use of function invocation parentheses.<>: allow you to refer to a terminal declared in the token area . Ex .:<NUMBER>"": Allow to express anonymous tokens , enclosing a literal string type characters.*: indicates repetition 0 or more times of what precedes it in parentheses.+: indicates repetition 1 or more times of what precedes it in parentheses.?: indicates that what precedes it in parentheses is optional.[]: has the same meaning as?, so (pattern)? It is equivalent to [Pattern].|: indicates optionality. Eg: ("text1" | "text2") matches with "text1" and with "Text2".In this way, a way to recognize expressions would be given by the grammar:void expr (): {} { term () ( ( “+” | ”-“ ) term () ) *![](image_rsrcDF8.jpg) 141154JavaCC}void term (): {} { fact () ( ( “*” | ”/“ ) fact () ) *}void fact (): {} {<NUMBER>​ |​ <ID>|​ " ( " Expr () " ) " }The Java code section allows you to locate Java statements and statementsibeginning of the implementation of a non-terminal function. Among other things, this can allow you to get a trace of the actions that our parser takes to recognize a sentence. For example, the program: void expr (): {System.out.println ("Recognizing an expression"); } { term () ( ( “+” | ”-“ ) term () ) *}void term (): {System.out.println ("Recognizing a term"); } { fact () ( ( “*” | ”/“ ) fact () ) *}void fact (): {System.out.println ("Recognizing a factor"); } {<NUMBER>​ |​ <ID>|​ " ( " Expr () " ) " }before the input "23 + 5 * (8-5)" would produce the output:Recognizing an expressionRecognizing a termRecognizing a factorRecognizing a termRecognizing a factorRecognizing a factorRecognizing an expressionRecognizing a termRecognizing a factorRecognizing a termRecognizing a factor![](image_rsrcDF9.jpg) Figure 5.2\. Syntactic tree that recognizes an expression based on rules BNF. For clarity they have not drawn the NUMBER tokens 142155which coincides with the preorder traversal of the syntactic tree that recognizes said sentence, and shown in Figure 5 .2 .Note that when BNF notation is used, the number of symbols under a The same rule may vary if repetition is used in it. Eg, continuing with the grammar above, the expr rule can give rise to as many children as you want in the syntactic tree; serve as a demonstration the tree associated with the recognition of "23 + 5 + 7 + 8 + 5", illustrated in the figure 5 .3, and whose corroboration is given by the parser output:Recognizing an expressionRecognizing a termRecognizing a factorRecognizing a termRecognizing a factorRecognizing a termRecognizing a factorRecognizing a termRecognizing a factorRecognizing a termRecognizing a factorFigure 5.3\. The syntactic tree associated with a BNF expression with expansions of type * or + may have an indeterminate number of children As already mentioned, JavaCC generates by default type LL (1) parsers, whichwhich means that they are able to choose between different flow options by consulting so just the next token in the input. However, we already know that not all grammars are LL (1), so there may be situations where it is necessary to query more than one token of the entrance in order to make a decision. For example, the following grammar: void expr (): {} {<ID>​ |​ <ID> "." expr ()​ |​ <ID> "()"}it is not LL (1) because, before an input like "cont.aux ()" the parser is as the first token with an <ID> , but since the three possible options of expr () start with <ID> , as there is not enough information to know which of them to choose. In this case, a pre-search of two tokens would allow us to decide correctly.To do this, the LOOKAHEAD option seen in section 5.2.1 allows you to tell JavaCC the type of grammar that is being provided, that is, the number of tokens that You must pre-search before making a decision.Notwithstanding the foregoing, in most cases, the grammars of the languages  of 143156JavaCC![](image_rsrcDFA.jpg) programming contain a majority of LL (1) expressions, and only a few rules for which may require a pre-search of 2, or 3 at the most. In these cases, you will get a more efficient parser if you leave the default pre-lookup (to 1), and tell itto JavaCC in which specific cases you should use a superior pre-search. This is achieved with the pre-search or local lookahead , which has the form:​ LOOKAHEAD ( n ) pattern​ oneand that it is considered in itself a pattern as well. The meaning of this clause is very​ simple: it tells the parser that, before deciding to enter through the pattern ,              onemake sure this is correct by taking n tokens . In this way, the above grammar is you can leave as LL (1) and indicate the local pre-search of the form: void expr (): {} {LOOKAHEAD (2) <ID>​ |​ LOOKAHEAD (2) <ID> "." expr ()​ |​ <ID> "()"}Furthermore, JavaCC itself is capable of deciding whether the pre-search specified by thedeveloper is adequate or not, reporting it in negative case both due to excess and default.​ 5.3​ Attribute management.JavaCC lacks a specific attribute management, but allows the passage ofparameters and the return of values  each time a non-terminal is invoked, as shown introduced at the point 5.2.3 . In this way, every time a non-terminal is invoked, we can assign the returned value to a global variable declared in the Java code block associated with said rule; this value acts as a synthesized attribute. Also, when declares a nonterminal, it can have formal parameters, such that it must be invoked with real parameters that will act as inherited attributes.Unfortunately, JavaCC also does not allow associating attributes to terminals, sothat it should be the semantic actions that work directly with the lexemes. Although This can be a hassle in most cases, it has the advantage that each actionsemantics you can extract the information that suits you from the lexeme, instead of always using the same attribute in all the consequents in which the same terminal appears. | <NUMBER> | {return Integer.parseInt (token.image); } |
| &#124; "(" accum = expr () ")"} | {return accum; } |

| int accum1 = 0, accum2 = 0;} { |  |  |

In addition, it is possible to place semantic actions inside BNF subexpressions (as in the case of exprand term , at points Ø, Ù, Ú and Û). A semantic action will be executed every time the flow of recognition goes through them. For example, before the entry 23 + 5 + 11 is it will pass 2 times through the point Ø, since it is necessary to pass 2 times through the subexpression (“+” Term ()) * to recognize that entry.Also, note that a semantic action placed just after aterminal can access the token object associated with it, through the variable token . This is so despite the pre-search that has been indicated, since it is the parser which loads said variable just after consuming each terminal, and regardless of which ones you had to pre-search to take a decision on which option to make.When semantic actions are included inside a BNF expression, This becomes extremely rare, being very difficult to recognize grammar in a sea of code. To avoid this problem it is recommended to place a comment in front of each nonterminal the pure BNF expression that defines it.On the other hand, when it is convenient for a terminal t to always have the sameattribute in all the consequents in which it appears, it is usually more readable to create a rule T :: = t , associate to T the attribute calculated from t and, in the rest of the rules, use T instead of t .Following the two previous tips, the example of the calculator would be:/ *expr :: = term (("+" | "-") term) */int expr (): { int accum1 = 0, accum2 = 0; } {

With it JavaCC will provide the parser class with the variables:error_line , error_column : variables of type integer that represent the line and the column in which the error was detected.error_string : String type variable that stores the lexeme that has the error occurred. If the pre-search ( lookahead ) has been set to a value greater than 1, error_string can contain a sequence of lexemes.expected_tokens : String [] type variable that contains the tokens that they would have been valid. Again, if the pre-search has been set to a value greater than 1, each item in expected_tokens can be a sequence of tokens146159instead of a loose token .Every time the parser encounters a syntax error it will invoketo the token_error () function , which will need to be rewritten by the developer so This will need to create a new class that inherits from the parser class.Similarly, the lexical analyzer can make use of theelements:error_line , error_column : line and column of the lexical error.error_after : character string containing the piece of lexeme to be tried to recognize.curChar : character that produced the lexical error.LexicalError () : function invoked by the manager token when it is found a lexical error.![](image_rsrcDFD.jpg) 5.4.2 Modern versions of JavaCCCurrently, JavaCC handles lexical and syntactic errors by launching theTokenMgrError and ParseException exceptions , whose classes are generated by JavaCC automatically if they don't already exist in the working directory. The philosophy is that developer should prevent lexical errors from occurring by controlling properly any input lexeme including invalid ones, in which case it should issue the corresponding error message.In this way, the developer can modify the ParseException classto adapt it to your needs. By default, this class has the variables:currentToken : of type Token , it stores the token object that has produced the error.expectedTokenSequences : of type int [] [] has the same objective as the variable expected_tokens seen in the previous section.tokenImage : of type String [] l stores the last lexemes read. If the pre-search is at 1, then it is true:tokenImage [0] .equals (currentToken.image);On the other hand, error recovery does not require any modification ofthe ParseException class , but instead uses a clause similar to the tryJava catch to catch the exception at a convenient point. Said clause is also considered a pattern, so it can appear anywhere one is expected. The clause is of the form:try {Pattern catch (AnyException x) {Java Recovery Code147160JavaCC}}Note that the JavaCC try-catch clause can catch anyexception, and not just the ParseException , so the developer cancreate your own exceptions and raise them where and when they occur. For example, it may be useful to create the exception SymbolsTableException .The recovery code usually works in panic mode , consuming all theentry until you reach a security token . The following example illustrates how recover errors in statements ending in semicolons. void FinishedStatement (): {} {try {​ (statement () <POINTANDOMA>) catch (ParseException e) {System.out.println (e.toString ()); Token t; do {t = getNextToken ();} while (t.kind! = POINT ANDOMA);}}​ 5.5​ Final exampleAssembling the different parts that have been seen throughout this![](image_rsrcDFE.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDFF.jpg) chapter, and observing the advice given in terms of comments, the example complete calculator with error control would be:PARSER_BEGIN (Calculator) public class Calculator {public static void main (String args []) throws ParseException {new Calculator (System.in) .gramma ();}}PARSER_END (Calculator)SKIP: {""| "\ t"| "\ n"| "\ r"}TOKEN [IGNORE_CASE]:{<NUMBER: (["0" - "9"]) +>​ |​ <PUNTOYCOMA: ";">}/ *grammar :: = (exprFinished) +/grammar void (): {} {148161(exprFinished ()) +}/ * exprFinished :: = expr ";"/void exprFinished (): { int result;} { try { result = expr () <POINTANDOMA> {System.out.println ("=" + result); }} catch (ParseException x) {System.out.println (x.toString ()); Token t; do { t = getNextToken ();} while (t.kind! = POINT ANDOMA);}}/ * expr :: = term (("+" | "-") term) */int expr (): { int accum1 = 0, accum2 = 0;} { accum1 = term ()​ (("+" accum2 = term () {accum1 + = accum2;})| ("-" accum2 = term () {accum1- = accum2;}) ) *{return accum1; }}/ * term :: = fact (("*" | "/") fact) *![](image_rsrcDFG.jpg) /int term (): { int accum1 = 0, accum2 = 0;} { accum1 = fact ()​ (("*" accum2 = fact () {accum1 * = accum2;})| ("/" accum2 = fact () {accum1 / = accum2;}) ) *{return accum1; }}/ * fact :: = NUMBER | "(" expr ")"/int fact (): { int accum = 0, uMinus = 1;} {149162JavaCC("-" {uLess = -1;})?​ (​ <NUMBER>​ {return Integer.parseInt (token.image) * uLess; }| "(" accum = expr () ")" {return accum * uLess; })}SKIP: {<ILLEGAL: (~ [])> {System.out.println ("Character:" + image + "not expected.");}}Note how the PUNTOYCOMA terminal had to be defined in order torefer to it in error handling.On the other hand, the order of the regular expressions is fundamental, soso the rule:<ILLEGAL: (~ [])>collect all the lexemes that have not entered by any of the previous rules, including ad hoc ones specified in the BNF grammar itself.​ 5.6​ The JJTree utilityJJTree is the preprocessor for JavaCC. Insert actions that buildparser trees in the JavaCC source. The output generated by JJTree runs through of JavaCC to create the parser.5.6.1 FeaturesBy default, JJTree generates code to build the parse node treefor each non-terminal of our language. This operation can be changed from so that certain non-terminals do not generate nodes, or generate a node from a expansion of a production rule.JJTree defines a Java interface type Node, which all nodes of the treeparser must implement. The interface provides methods for operations like initialization of the parent type of a generated node, as well as addition and retrieval of children.JJTree can operate in two different ways: simple or multi (to searchbetter terms). In simple mode, each node in the parser tree is of type![](image_rsrcDFH.jpg) SimpleNode ; in multi mode, the type of the nodes in the parse tree is derived from the name of the node. If no implementations are provided for the node class, JJTree will generate implementations based on the SimpleNode class , being able to modify the implementations so that they can be useful to us.5.6.2 Practical ExampleAt the end of this document there is a section (Execution Steps) in thewhich indicates the steps to be taken to compile .jjt files and .jj. A trailer is shown below.For the execution of JJTree it is necessary to feed the input of the following 150163way:jjtree example1.jjtAll the files that are generated automatically can be consultedin the Source Files section.Suppose we create a file called example1.jjt with the followingspecs:PARSER_BEGIN (example1)class example1 { public static void main (String args []) {System.out.println ("Keyboard input"); example1 ins = new example1 (System.in); try {SimpleNode node = ins.start (); node.dump ("");} catch (Exception e) {System.out.println (e.getMessage ()); e.printStackTrace ();}}}PARSER_END (example1)SKIP: {

}TOKEN: / * LITERAL * /{<INT:<DECIMAL>​ |​ <HEX>​ |​ <OCTAL>>|<#DECIMAL: ["1" - "9"] (["0" - "9"]) *>|<#HEX: "0" ["x", "X"] (["0" - "9", "a" - "f", "A" - "F"]) +>|<#OCTAL: "0" (["0" - "7"]) *>![](image_rsrcDFJ.jpg) }TOKEN: / * IDENTIFIERS * /{151164JavaCC<IDENTIFIER: <LETTER> (<LETTER> | <DIGIT>) *>|<#LETTER: ["_", "a" - "z", "A" - "Z"]>|<#DIGIT: ["0" - "9"]>}SimpleNode Start (): {} {Expression () ";"{return jjtThis; }} void Expression (): {} {ExpSum () }void ExpSum (): {}{ExpMult () (("+" | "-") ExpMult ()) *} void ExpMult (): {}{ExpUnary () (("*" | "/" | "%") ExpUnary ()) *} void ExpUnaria (): {}{"(" Expression () ")" | Identifier () | <ENTER>}void Identifier (): {} {<IDENTIFIER>}The e.printStackTrace () statement ; tells me that I get the content of thestack when an error has occurred. The character string passed to the method indicates the prefix that is added to nodes when they are displayed on the screen. All this explained throughout the document.If we run jjtree example1.jjt we get four files:Node.java ,SimpleNode.java ,example1TreeConstants.java andJJTexample1State.javaThe content of example1TreeConstants.java is as follows:public interface example1TreeConstants {public int JJTHOME = 0; public int JJTEXPRESION = 1; public int JJTEXPSUMA = 2; public int JJTEXPMULT = 3; public int JJTEXPUNARIA = 4; public int JJTIDENTIFIER = 5; public String [] jjtNodeName = { "Start","Expression","ExpSuma","ExpMult","ExpUnaria","Identifier",}; }Once this is done, if we want our grammar to recognize asentence introduced by the input assigned in the source header (in this case the keyboard), we have to execute the following commandjavacc example1.jjThe file example1.jj has been generated automatically by recognizing JJTree our grammar correct.Files automatically generated by javacc for any .jj sourceare specified in the Source Files section .We compile the .java file that we have obtained with javac . Said executableWe find it in version 1.5 of the Java JDK. After this step, we obtain the executable that will launch the reading of the string to recognize:javac example1.java java example1If we provide you with the following entry![](image_rsrcDFM.jpg) 153166JavaCC(id + id) * (id + id)the generated tree is the following:StartExpressionExpSumaExpMultExpunaryExpressionExpSumaExpMultExpunaryIdentifierExpMultExpunaryIdentifierExpunaryExpressionExpSumaExpMultExpunaryIdentifierExpMultExpunaryIdentifierWe see that an integer value is assigned to each non-terminal (functionjava in the .jjt file ) uniquely, and that by default, a name is assigned to each node (non-terminal). This name, if not assigned by the user, is the same as the name of the method that implements the non-terminal. To be able to assign a name different from the one set by default, it is necessary to use the character # followed by name that we want to assign.5.6.2.1 Renaming nodes using ## is a constructor that is used so that the user can call each nodein a different way than JavaCC calls it by default. Must operate in multi mode so that each node can be derived from the name that we put immediately after of the # constructor. It also serves to simplify the syntax and remove from the stack nodes that do not contribute information to the tree generated by our grammar.In the options area, the multi mode must be active as followsway:options {MULTI = true;}If we use the latter for the same example ( example1bis.jjt ), the syntaxwould: void Expression () #void: {}154167![](image_rsrcDFN.jpg){ExpSum ()} void ExpSuma () #void: {}{(ExpMult () (("+" | "-") ExpMult ()) *) # Sum (> 1)} void ExpMult () #void: {} {(ExpUnaria () (("*" | "/" | "%") ExpUnaria ()) *) # Mult (> 1)} void ExpUnaria () #void: {} {"(" Expression () ")" | Identifier () | <LITERAL_ENTERO>}And the file example1bisTreeConstans would be:public interface example1bisTreeConstants {public int JJTHOME = 0; public int JJTVOID = 1; public int JJTSUMA = 2; public int JJTMULT = 3; public int JJTIDENTIFIER = 4; public String [] jjtNodeName = {"Start","void","Sum","Mult","Identifier",};}Feeding the input again with the same string, we get the followingoutput: (stack trace) StartMult SumIdentifierIdentifierSumIdentifierIdentifierWe observe that those non-terminals that we have renamed as#void, they do not appear in the output corresponding to the stack. JJTree will never do again reference to this node. This node will not be closed, unless the method user-defined closeNodeHook () can be used in a call with this node as a parameter.Otherwise, the number of children of the node specified in the expressioninteger of the defined node, or all nodes that were stored in the stack in a conditional node scope, they are added to the node (said number is the arity of the node; if it is conditional, #Id (> 1) is defined ) . He order in which they are added is not specified.The jjtClose () method is called .The node is stored on the stack.If the NODE_SCOPE_HOOK option is active, the parser method user-defined closeNodeScope () is called and uses the node as a parameter.If the node is not the root node, it is added as a child structure to anothernode and its jjtParent () method is called .5.6.7 Visitor SupportJJTree provides some basic support for the visitor layout pattern. If the VISITOR option is true, JJTree will add a jjtAccept () method in all classes of node that it generates, in addition to the creation of a user interface that can be implemented and passed to nodes.The name of such an interface is constructed by adding Visitor to the nameof the parser. The interface is generated every time JJTree runs, so represents exactly the set of nodes used by the parser. Any decorated node will not be affected by this option.NODE_FACTORY ( false by default): use a generation method to build nodes with the following structure:public static Node jjtCreate (int id)NODE_PACKAGE ( "" by default): the package to generate the node in a class. By default, it contains the parser package.NODE_PREFIX ( "AST" by default): the prefix used to build the node class names from the node ids inmulti mode. Must be used consistently with equivalent options provided by JavaCC. 169182![](image_rsrcDG4.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDG5.jpg) JavaCC170183Chapter 6Symbol table​ 6.1​ OverviewAlso called "table of names" or "table of identifiers", it issimply from a high-performance data structure that stores all the necessary information about user identifiers. It has two functions main:Carry out semantic checks.Generate code.Also, this structure remains in memory only in dump time.compilation, not execution, except in those cases where it is compiled with debugging options. Interpreters also usually maintain the symbol table in memory during execution, since it occurs simultaneously with the translation.The symbol table stores the information that is currentlyyou need about the program variables; information such as: name, type, memory location address, size, etc. Adequate and effective management of The symbol table is very important, since its manipulation consumes a large part compile time. Hence its efficiency is critical.The symbol table is also used to store information regarding the![](image_rsrcDG6.jpg) user-created data types, enumerated types, and generally any identifier created by the user. In these cases, the developer can choose to mix the different kinds of identifiers in a single table, or have several tables, where each of them will house a different class of identifiers:variable table, data type table, user functions table, etc. In what We are still going to focus mainly on user variables.​ 6.2​ Information about user identifiersThe information that the developer decides to store in this table will dependof the specific characteristics of the translator you are developing. Between this information may include:• Name of the element. The name or identifier can be stored by limiting or not the length of it. If stored with limit using a size fixed maximum for each name, creation speed can be increased and manipulation, but at the cost of limiting the length of names to a few 171184Symbol tablecases and wasting space in most cases. The alternative method consists ofenable the memory that we need in each case to save the name. In C this is easy with type char *; if we do the compiler in Modula-2, forFor example, the ADDRESS type should be used. In the case of Java, this does not it involves some difficulty thanks to the String type. Searches in the table symbols are usually made by this name; therefore, and to speed up the this operation, the table of symbols usually be a hash table ( hash ) where search and insert operations have a cost approximately constant (. O (1)).Type of the element. When storing variables, it is essential know the type of data to which each of them belongs, whether it is primitive as if not, in order to be able to control that the use made ofsuch variables is consistent with the type with which they were declared. The chapter 7 is exclusively dedicated to this management.Memory address where its value will be stored at runtime.This address is necessary, because instructions that refer to a variable must know where to find the value of that variable at execution in order to be able to generate machine code, whether it is global and local variables. In languages  that do not allow recursion, addresses are assigned sequentially as the addresses are made.statements. In languages  with block structures, the address is given with relative to the beginning of the memory area assigned to that block (function or procedure) specifically.Value of the element. When working with simple interpreters, and since compilation and execution times overlap in an interpreter, you can it will be easier to manage the variables if we store their values  in the symbol table. In a compiler, however, the symbol table does not never stores the value.![](image_rsrcDG7.jpg) Number of dimensions. If the variable to be stored is an array, also its dimensions can be stored. Although this information may be extracted from the type structure, it can be explicitly stated for a more efficient control.Types of formal parameters. If the identifier to store belongs to a function or procedure, it is necessary to store the types of the formal parameters to control that any invocation of this function is made with consistent real parameters. The return type is also stores as the type of the element.Other information. In order to obtain statistical summaries and information varies, it may be interesting to store other data: line numbers in those that have used an identifier, line number in which it was declared, activation record size (see section 9.3.2), if it is a variable172185global or local, in which function was it declared if local, etc.​ 6.3​ Symbol table considerationsThe symbol table can be initialized with some useful information, which canbe stored in a single structure or in several:Constants: PI, E, NUMERO_AVOGADRO, etc.Library functions: EXP, LOG, SQRT, etc.Reserved words. Some lexicographic analyzers do not recognizereserved words directly, but only recognizeuser. Once one of the input is taken, they look for it in the table of reserved words in case it coincides with any; if found, they return the token to the parser associated in the table; if not, they return it as a true identifier. This facilitates work to the lexicographic, moreover, since this table is invariable, it can be stored in a perfect hash table (the one in which all searches are exactly O (1)) thus constituting a more efficient alternative to PCLex or JavaCC as we have seen it.Figure 6.1\. Accesses to the symbol table by the different phases of a compilerIn general, as the analysis stage progresses and new![](image_rsrcDG8.jpg) identifier declarations, the lexical analyzer, or the parser depending on the strategy we follow will insert new entries in the symbol table, avoiding always the existence of repeated entries. The semantic analyzer performs the Context-sensitive checks thanks to the symbol table and, already in the stage For synthesis, the intermediate code generator uses the associated memory addresses to each identifier to generate a program equivalent to the input one. By ruleIn general, the code optimizer does not need to make use of it, although nothing prevents can access it.Although the efficiency in the accesses and manipulation of the symbol table173186Symbol tableare essential to make a good compiler, it is usually convenient to perform a progressive development of it. Thus, in the early phases of the construction of the compiler, given its complexity it is better to include a symbol table as simple as possible possible (e.g. based on a simply chained list) to avoid errorsthat are disseminated through the rest of the compiler phases. Once the various phases function properly, all effort can be concentrated on optimizing management from the symbol table. So that this process does not influence the phases already developed compiler, it is necessary to provide the symbol table with a clear interface defined and unchanged from the beginning: in the optimization of the symbol table, modifies its implementation, but maintains its interface. This whole process can see in figure 6 . 1 .This figure mentions an important distinction between the construction ofcompilers for languages  that force the programmer to declare all variables(C, Modula-2, Pascal, Java, etc.), and languages  in which variables can be used directly without the need to declare them (BASIC, Logo, etc.). When a language It has a declaration area and a statement area, the appearance of an identifier has a very different semantics depending on the area you are in:If it appears in the declaration area, then you must insert theidentifier in the symbol table, together with the information that know. If the identifier was already in the table, then it has beenA redeclaration has occurred and a semantic error message should be issued.If it appears in the sentences area, then you have to look for the identifierin the symbol table to control that the use that is being made of it is consistent with its type. If the identifier is not found in the table, then is that it has not been previously declared, which is usually considered an error semantic to inform the programmer.In these cases, the lexicographic analyzer, when aidentifier does not know in which area it has been found, so it is not easy to include a lexical action that discriminates between performing an insert or a search; So, it must be the parser who does these operations. Thus, the attribute of Identifier token is usually its own name.However, there are a few programming languages  in which it is notvariables need to be declared, either because there is only a single data type (such as in our calculator example where everything is considered integer values) or because the name of the variable itself serves to discern its type (in BASIC the variables ending in "$" are of type string). In these cases, the lexicographic analyzer can insert the identifier into the symbol table the first time it appears; before inserting it performs a search operation in case![](image_rsrcDG9.jpg) already existed. And whatever the case, you get the entry or index of the table in which it is found or has been included; This index will be the value that the lexical analyzer passes to the syntactic as an attribute of each identifier.174187Finally, it is important to emphasize that the symbol table containsuseful information to be able to compile, and therefore only exists at compile time, and not of execution. However, in an interpreter since compilation and execution occurs at the same time, the symbol table remains in memory for the entire weather.​ 6.4​ Example: a calculator with variablesAt this point, we are going to increase the possibilities of the calculatorproposed in previous sections, including the possibility of manipulating variables. To simplify the design we will not include an area for variable declarations, but that these can be used directly by initializing to 0, since only we have the integer type.To enter the variables in the calculator we need a newsyntactic construction with which to be able to give it values: it is about the assignment. In addition, we will continue with the expression evaluation and display statement to which we will prepend the reserved word PRINT . In any expression you may intervene a variable that will be evaluated to the value it has at that moment. Thus, before the entrance:Ø a: = 7 * 3;Ù b: = 3 * a;Ú a: = a + b;you want to store in the variable a the value 21, in the b the value 63 and then modify a to contain the value 84\. To achieve our purposes we will use a symbol table in which we will store only the name of each variable, like this as its value since we are dealing with an interpreter. The symbol table will haveFigure 6.2\. One of the simplest (and most inefficient) symbol tables that can be builta simply chained unordered list structure (see figure 6\. 2 ), sinceclarity in our development takes precedence over efficiency. In developments with Java we will use the HashMap class .Fig . 6 . 3 shows how the symbol table should be updated toreflect the proposed assignments. Thus, in the Ø sentence, once the expression 7 * 3 to the value 21 the symbol table will be inspected for the identifier a . Since it is missing, the lexical analyzer will create it by initializing it to 0; then the rule associated with the assignment will modify the input of the a to![](image_rsrcDGA.jpg) 175188Symbol tableFigure 6.3\. Status of the symbol table after executing some assignment statementsassign it the value 21\. In the Ù statement, the evaluation of the expression requires searching a in the symbol table to collect its value -21- to multiply it by 3\. The result -63- is stored in the input associated with b in the same way as was done for the variable a in the Ø statement. Finally, the Ú statement evaluates a new expression in which a and b intervene , assigning their sum -84- to the entry of a in the table of symbols.6.4.1 Symbol table interfaceAs already mentioned, the interface of the symbol table should beclear from the start so that any changes to the implementation from the symbol table has no impact on compiler stages anymore developed. The basic operations that it must have are:create () : create an empty table.insert (symbol) : adds the given symbol to the table.search (name) : returns the symbol whose name matches theparameter. If the symbol does not exist, it returns null.print () : for information purposes, it displays the list of variables stored in the symbol table along with their associated values.6.4.2 Solution with Lex / YaccWe assume that there is no declaration area in our language, sothe insertion of the symbols in the table must be done from a lexical action. Is verification ensures the detection and subsequent communication to the programmer certain bugs that make debugging easier but cannot bemanaged from the exclusively syntactic point of view. Of this this kind of errors, the most common are derived from the use of data types, either provided by the language itself (primitive types), or created by the programmer based on syntactic constructions provided by the language (user types). Basically, data types are used precisely for the programmer to declare for what purpose are you going to use each variable (to store an integer, a real number, a character string, etc.), so that the compiler can automatically detect in which situations the programmer has made a mistake (for example, For example, if you tried to add a whole number and a string of characters).Type management ensures that the type of a syntactic constructionmatch what is intended in your context. For example, the predefined arithmetic operator mod in Modula-2 requires integer numeric operands, so using theType management must ensure that these operands are of type integer. Equal In this way, type management must ensure that dereferencing is applied only to a pointer, that the indexing is done only on an array, that a function the correct number and type of arguments are applied, etc.The information collected by the type manager is also useful when it comes togenerate code. For example, arithmetic operators typically apply both to integers as to reals, and perhaps other types, and the context of each one of them to determine the memory space required to store calculations intermediate. A symbol or function is said to be overloaded when it represents different operations in different contexts; for example, the sum (expressed by the symbol " + ") is usually overloaded for integers and for reals. The overload can be accompanied by a type conversion, where the compiler provides the operator to convert an operand to the type expected by the context. Consider for example expressions like "x + i" where x is of real type and i is of integer type. Since the representation of integers and reals is different within a computer, and different machine instructions are used for operations on integers and reals, the compiler may have to first convert one of the operators to ensure both operands are of the same type when you have187![](image_rsrcDGP.jpg) 200Type managementplace the sum. The grammar of figure 7 .1 generates expressions consisting of integer and real constants to which the arithmetic operator of addition + applies. When two integers are added the result is integer, and if it is not real. In this way, each subexpression has associated a type and a dependent storage space of this, just as it will have a value associated with it at runtime, as shown illustrated in the tree to the right of this figure.Figure 7.1\. Example of calculation of the type of each subexpression. The sum of integers produces an integer, but if a real value is involved, then the result will be a realA different notion of overload is that of polymorphism , which appears inobject-oriented languages. In these languages  there is a hierarchy of inheritance in which the more specialized types inherit from the more general types, in a that specialized types may incorporate features that are lacking in general, and can even change the operation of certain methods of the classes ancestors in what is called function rewriting . In these languages, a function is polymorphic when it has general type parameters, but can be executed with arguments of specialized types of the same hereditary line, that is, descendants of the general type, triggering in each case a different action by means of dynamic linking . According to dynamic bonding, in time of execution detects the type of the actual parameter and executes its own methods, in instead of the father's, if they are rewritten. It is one thing for our language to have some overloaded predefined functions, and something else allowing definition of overloaded functions. In the latter case, you have to solve in some way conflicts in the symbol table, since the same identifier for the name of function has associated different parameters and bodies.​ 7.2​ Nominal, structural and functional compatibilityWhen you are going to build a new programming language, it turns out It is essential to establish a rigorous and well-documented criterion of compatibility between types, in such a way that the programmers who are going to use that language know what to expect.We understand that two types A and B are compatible when, given twoexpressions e and eto of types b​ A and B respectively, can be used interchangeably in a given context. There are many different contexts, of which the most188201![](image_rsrcDGR.jpg) usual is the assignment, so that if the variable v is of type A and a              to assignment of the form v: = e , we will say that to              b              A and B are compatible for assignment. Another context is given by arithmetic operations, such as addition: if a language admits expressions of the form e + e , we will say that to              b A and B are compatible for the sum. In the latter case, types A and B could represent, for example, types int and float of C.Basically three compatibility criteria can be established between types:nominal, structural and functional. Nominal compatibility is the strictest, that is, the one that imposes the strongest conditions to decide when two variables can be assign to each other. Functional compatibility is the most relaxed, while structural is at an intermediate level. Of course, the developer of compilers can set other criteria that are midway between any of these compatibilities.To explain the difference between these criteria we will use the following twotypes defined in C:typedef struct {char name [20]; char surnames [35]; int age; } employee ; employee e1, e2;Ytypedef struct {char name [20]; char surnames [35]; int age; } customer ; client c1, c2;The customer and employee types have different names, that is, they are differentfrom the nominal point of view. However, its content or structure is the same, therefore they are equivalent from the structural point of view. In this way, a language with nominal level compatibility prevents making assignments like e1 = c1 , but lets make e1 = e2 , since e1 and e2 belong to exactly the same type(since there cannot be two types with the same name). A language withStructural type compatibility allows you to do both assignments, since only requires that the structure of the l-value and the r-value be identical which is true for employee and customer types .Nominal compatibility has strong implications when declaringvariables of anonymous types. Continuing with the example, we would have:struct {char name [20]; char surnames [35];189202Type managementint age;} e1, e2;Ystruct {char name [20]; char surnames [35]; int age;![](image_rsrcDGS.jpg) } c1, c2; where the types of the variables e1 , e2 , c1 and c2 are unnamed. By rule In general, in these situations, a language with nominal type compatibility creates a type with an internal name for each anonymous declaration, which in the example is translates into the creation of two types, one to which e1 and e2 will belong (and which, therefore, will be nominally compatible), and another to which c1 and c2 will belong (which also will be nominally compatible).On the other hand, functional compatibility does not even require thatexpressions have the same structure. Two expressions e and e of types A and B              to              b respectively they are functionally compatible if A and B have different structures but they can be used interchangeably in some context. The most usual case consists of be able to add integer and decimal values. As a general rule, integers are usually represent two's complement, while decimal numbers are represented in floating point. Although the same number of octets are used to represent both, their structure is essentially different, but mostprogramming allows you to perform arithmetic operations between them. In these languages there is a functional compatibility between the integer type and the decimal type.Functional compatibility requires the compiler to detect the situationsin which expressions of types with different structures are correctly mixed, by object to generate code that performs the type conversion of the expression minus accurate to the most accurate. For example, when adding integers with decimals, the integers must be transformed to decimals and only then perform the operation. This Automatic operation is called promotion (of the English promotion ).In object-oriented languages  there is also support forinheritance: if a class B inherits from another A ( B is an A ), as a general rule it can An object of class B should be used in any situation where an object of type A , since B is an A and therefore meets all the functional requirements for impersonate it.​ 7.3​ Managing primitive typesAt this point, the management of a basic calculator will be studied, when in thisthe character string type is included, in addition to the integer type with which it has already beenworked in previous chapters.7.3.1 Starting grammar190203The grammar to be recognized will allow you to manage values  of type integer and of typecharacter string. Strings can be concatenated by overloading theoperator " + " that, remember, also allows adding integers. Through two functions that are part of the language will allow the conversion between both types. To avoid get lost in unnecessary details, some arithmetic operations have been removed and multiple assignment. Also, to illustrate that carriage return has not always to be considered as a separator, in the proposed grammar it has been used as a terminator, doing the semicolon functions of example grammars previous. The grammar expressed in Yacc format is as follows:prog: prog assign '\ n'| prog PRINT expr '\ n'| prog error '\ n'| / * Epsilon * /;assign: ASIG ID expr![](image_rsrcDGT.jpg) ; expr: expr '+' expr| expr '*' expr| TO_ENTER '(' expr ')'| A_CADENA '(' expr ')'| ID| NUMBER| CHAIN;The same grammar for JavaCC remains:grammar void (): {} {(sentFinished ()) *}void sentFinished (): {} {<PRINT> expr () <RETURNODECARRO>​ |​ <ID> <ASIG> expr () <RETORNODECARRO>​ |​ / * Error handling * /}void expr (): {} { term () (“+” term ()) *}void term (): {} { fact () ("*" fact ()) *}void fact (): {} {<ID>​ |​ <NUMBER>​ |​ <CHAIN>​ |​ <To_String> "(" expr () ")"​ |​ <A_ENTER> "(" expr () ")"}The semantic role of each construction is:191204Type managementIt is possible to assign the value of an expression to a variable. It's not possible do multiple assignments: ASIG ID expr .You can work with values  of two types: character strings and numbers integers.The grammar does not have a zone where the programmer can declare the typeof each variable, but it will be defined by the one of the first assignment valid that is done. That is, if the first time the variable x appears, assigns the value 65, so the type of x is integer. The type of a variable it cannot change during the execution of a program.It is possible to display the value of an expression: PRINT expr . Is statement allows, therefore, to display both integer values  and strings of characters, so if we consider it from a functional point of view, we can say that PRINT is a polymorphic function.![](image_rsrcDGU.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDGV.jpg) With the integers we will be able to do addition and multiplication. Instead with chains it doesn't make much sense to do any of these operations, although we will use the addition symbol to allow string concatenations. It is not possible to add or concatenate integers and chains.It is possible to carry out type conversions using functions of theprogramming language. These are different than the pre-built functions (built-ins), which are stored in libraries but whose names are not they are actually part of the syntax of the language. These functions are:A_String (expr) : has as parameter an expression of type integer and returns the text string that represents it as a result.A_ENTER (expr) : its parameter is a character type expression which represents a number and returns its integer value as a result.7.3.2 Construction stepsOnce the grammar to be used is clear, it is convenient to continuethe steps below to complete the construction of the translator. In these steps it is common to have to go back in order to reorganize the grammar to adapt their constructions to the requirements of the metacompilers.7.3.2.1 Proposal of an exampleIn order to continue the construction steps, it is usually a good ideapresent an example of input to the translator and the desired output. The entrance appears in black, output in blue, and error messages in red:​ OR​ b: = "Comrade Trotsky"​ OR​ a: = 7​ OR​ c: = 12 + 3192205​ OR​ PRINT c + 5twenty​ OR​ PRINT bComrade Trotsky​ Y​ PRINT b + 7Strings and integers cannot be added.​ Þ​ PRINT b + ", founder of the USSR."Comrade Trotsky, founder of the USSR.​ H.H​ PRINT A_ENTERO (“23”) * 4927.3.2.2 Definition of the symbol tableIn this step, the structure of the symbol table is defined, if it isneeded for the problem, as well as its interface.In this case, we will need the symbol table, since it iswill store the values  of the identifiers. It will also be necessary to save the type of each variable in order to control that its use is consistent with the intentions of your declaration (implicit in the first assignment). In this way, the type of a variable will be stored in the symbol table by a character with the following meaning:'c': character string type.'e': integer type.'i': undefined type. This type will be used in case that in the first assignment an attempt is made to give a variable the value resulting from an erroneous expression.It seems clear that since there are variables of different types, an input![](image_rsrcDGW.jpg) from the symbol table must be able to store both a string and an integer, in a function of the type of the variable it represents. In C language this is solved by a union structure , while in Java you will have an Object handle that, independing on the type of the variable, it will point to an Integer or a String . Fig . 7 .two graphically shows the structure of the symbol table.Figure 7.2\. Situation of the symbol table after entering the sentences of the point 7.3.2.17.3.2.3 Attribute mappingThe next step is to study which attribute has to be associated with theterminals and non-terminals.193206Type management​ 7.3.2.3.1​ Terminal attributesWith respect to the NUMBER and CHAIN terminals , since they representinteger and string constants, it is wise to assign them as attributes the constants they represent. In the case of NUMERO , aconversion of the lexeme read to the corresponding integer, since we are interested in operate arithmetically with it. As for the character string, it will be necessary to delete the opening and closing quotation marks as they are just delimiters that are not part of the literal itself.On the other hand, and as has already been commented, since we are defining alanguage without an area of  explicit declarations, it will be the lexical analyzer who inserts the identifiers in the symbol table, in order to unload the syntactic of some chores. The terminal attribute ID is therefore an entry in this table. Basically, the lexicographic analyzer, when it encounters an identifier, willlook in the table; if it is, it associates its input as an attribute to the ID found and returns to the syntactic; if it is not, it creates a new entry in the table and returns it to the syntactic. He The problem that arises here is: what is a new entry in the table of symbols? Let's analyze, for example, the first sentence of section 7.3.2.1 :b = "Comrade Trotsky"When the lexical analyzer finds the identifier b , it looks for it in the table of symbols (which is initially empty) and, not finding it, creates a new node in it. This node will have a name "b", but what is its type? The type of b depends on the value given in the first assignment (in this case a character string),but the problem is that the lexical analyzer does not see beyond the lexeme in which finds at this moment, "b", so it is still unable to know the type of the expression behind the assignment terminal. The type of b is implicit in the time when its first assignment is made, but the time the parser Lexical insertion into the symbol table is before the moment it is![](image_rsrcDGX.jpg) know the type of the assigned expression. The solution is that the parser will be the one who puts the type when the first assignment is concluded; meanwhile, the lexicographic will indicate an undefined transitory type: "i". Fig . 7 . 3 shows the contents of the symbol table at the precise moment when the lexical analyzer returns to the syntactic the entry in the symbol table corresponding to the c of the Ú sentence.Figure 7.3\. The table entry marked in red has just been inserted by the parser lexical that, since it does not know the type of the newly inserted variable c , sets it as undefined:i194207​ 7.3.2.3.2​ Non-terminal attributesSo far, in our calculator example, the nonterminal expr hadas an attribute the integer value it represented. However, in the case that we now occupies, the concept of expression denoted by the nonterminal expr serves to represent both integer and string expressions. This duality looks goodimplemented through the concept of union in C, in a similar way to how done on the nodes of the symbol table. In this way, the attribute of expr is a record with a field to indicate its type and a union to indicate its value, either integer or string. In the case of Java, the solution is even simpler since the attribute can be a simple Object ; if said Object is instanceof Integer the type will be whole; if the Object is instanceof String the type will be character string; and if the null the type will be undefined. In any case, for the sake of readability, it is alsoIt is possible to associate an object with two fields, type and value, as in the solution in C. The figure 7\. 5 shows an example of the associated attributes when the Ú sentence of section 7.3.2.1 . In this particular case, the expr attributemust conceptually match part of the structure of a node in the symbols as shown in figure 7\. 4 .Figure 7.5\. Recognition of an assignment statement and attributes associated with the terminals and non-terminals of the syntactic treeThe attribute associated with an expression allows you to manipulate semantic errors.For example, a statement of the form:![](image_rsrcDGY.jpg) c = 8 + "hello"is syntactically correct since there is a syntactic tree that recognizes it, but it isFigure 7.4\. The attribute of an expression is a type and a value (integer or string based on of type), which matches part of a node from the symbol table (shaded area)195208Type managementsemantically incorrect, as we will not allow mixing numbers and strings in a sum. Therefore, the resulting expression in the syntactic tree will have the type value 'i' of undefined, as shown in the tree in the figure 7 . 6.Figure 7.6\. An error occurs semantic because you cannot add a integer and a string. The expression resulting is therefore undefined7.3.2.4 Semantic actionsIn this section, semantic actions will be studied from two points ofview:O The type compatibility checks.O The actions to be taken in case the restrictions of types.As a general rule, the study of semantic actions is usually donestarting with the rules by which it is first reduced in a syntax treeanyone, since these are usually the simplest, and this is how the problem is addressed in the same order in which semantic actions will end up being executed. The rules: expr              :              NUMBER​ |​ CHAINare the simplest since the associated semantic action must only assign the type to expr and copy the token attribute as value of expr .Rule:​ expr​ :​ expr '*' expryou must control that the two expressions are of type integer. If they are, the type of the expr The resulting value is also an integer and the value will be the product of the expressions of the consequent. Otherwise, an expression of undefined type will result.Rule:​ expr​ :​ expr '+' expris a bit more complicated, since the '+' operator is overloaded and with it you can both add integers and concatenate strings. Therefore, the action semantics must check that the types of the antecedent expressions areequal. If they are integers, an integer expression is produced that is the sum of the other two. If they are character strings produces an expression that is the concatenation of the other two. In any other case an undefined expression is produced.The rules:![](image_rsrcDGZ.jpg) ​ expr​ :​ TO_ENTER '(' expr ')'196209​ |​ A_CADENA '(' expr ')'take, respectively, expressions of type string and integer and return integers and strings, also respectively. In any other case an expression is producedindefinite. The A_String () function converts the value of its parameter into a string characters that are digits. The A_ENTER () function converts its parameter to a number, as long as the string consists of a sequence of digits; in case Otherwise an undefined expression is also generated.Rule:​ expr​ :​ IDis trivial, since the parser receives the input from the lexicon tablesymbols in which the variable resides. Therefore, since the attribute of an expression share part of the structure of a symbol table node, in this action semantics all you have to do is copy said piece (see figure 7 .4).The semantics of the assignment given by the rule: assign​ :              ASIG ID exprit's a bit more complex. Remember that the type of a variable is given by thekind of what is assigned to you first. For example, if in the first assignment to the variable a is assigned the value 7, then its type is integer throughout implementation of the program; if it is assigned "Ana", then it will be of type string. Therefore, the assignment must:If the type of the l-value is undefined, it is assigned the value and the type of the rvalue, whatever it is.If the type of the l-value is defined, then:If the types of the l-value and the r-value coincide then it is assigned to the l-value the value of the r-value.If the types do not match, an error message is issued and the l-value it is left intact.Implicit in this explanation is what to do when attempting to assign avariable an undefined expression: if the variable is still undefined it is left as is; if not, the variable does not become indefinite, but remains as it is. If before a indefinite assignment it occurred to us to put the variable in an undefined state, the sequence:

| a = 7 | U Works |
| a = 7 + "hello" | X Fail (and change type from a to undefined) |
| a = "bad" | U Works (but shouldn't) |

I would change the value and type of to , which is wrong with respect to the semantics of our language.Once the type is assigned to a variable, it is always the same; yes duringexecution is assigned a wrong expression, the type of the variable will remain the same itself and its value will not change either. This behavior could be modified adding a boolean variable to the symbol table to tell me if the value of the variable is known or not, in such a way that a wrong assignment would put this![](image_rsrcDH0.jpg) 197210Type managementvariable to false , as illustrated in figure 7\. 7 .Figure 7.7\. Some examples of type constructors are:POINTER TO. To create pointers.RECORD OF. To create records.210223ARRAY OF. To create tables and arrays.![](image_rsrcDHD.jpg) PROCEDURE RETURNS. To create functions that return a result.These are not really types but type constructors since, althoughthey allow to build complex types, they do not make sense by themselves and must be applied on a base type that can be another type constructor or a type primitive. Type constructors are therefore recursive. The registry case is more special because it can be applied to Cartesian product of various types.All this leads us to two fundamental differences regarding the managementof primitive types that was done in the previous section. First, the language to define requires a declaration area where to enumerate the variables of aprogram and indicate its type. In other words, the type of a variable will no longer be given for its first assignment but it must be explicitly declared. And secondSecond, since a complex type can be arbitrarily long, it cannotbe encoded with a single letter ('e' for integers, 'c' for strings, etc.) but instead some other method will have to be used.On the other hand, when using a declared variable of any of the typesabove, you need to use a type modifier to reference the components. For example:^. Placed next to a pointer, it represents what is being pointed to..countryside. Placed next to a record, it represents one of its fields.[]. Placed next to an array, it represents one of its elements.(). Placed next to a function, it invokes it and represents the value of shoot.As can be seen, each type constructor has its own modifierof type and these are not interchangeable, that is, the “^” can only be applied to pointers nothing more, the "." it can only be applied to records, etc. This gives us a table that one-to-one relates each type constructor with its modifier:​ Type constructors​ Type modifiers​ POINTER TO​ ^​ RECORD OF​ .countryside​ ARRAY OF​ []​ PROCEDURE RETURNS​ ()It is also important to realize that a type modifier does not havewhy apply exclusively to a variable, but can be applied to aexpression that has, in turn, another type modifier, eg: "ptr ^ .name" refers to to the record name field pointed to by ptr , and the modifier "." has been applied to the expression ptr ^ and not to a variable directly.7.4.1 Objectives and proposal of an exampleIn the following points, the management of the following complex types will be created: 211224Type managementPointersArrays of unknown dimensions.Functions without parameters.The fact of not knowing the dimensions of the arrays and of ignoring thefunction parameters will allow us to focus exclusively on managing types. On the other hand, we will support the basic types:Logical or Boolean.![](image_rsrcDHE.jpg) Whole.Real.Character.In total we have four primitive types and three type constructors. Is It is important to note that for each primitive type it is necessary to supply the programmer some mechanism with which he can define constants of each of these types: Boolean , integer, real, and character constants .In what follows we will build the type management of a compiler, whichmeans that we will put the calculator example aside and focus on in recognizing valid statements and expressions. To check that our compiler is working fine, every time the programmer enters a expression should show us a message where, in natural language, the type of that expression. The tokens that represent constants do not need any attribute since that our goal is not to process values  or generate equivalent code. This should not concern us given the amounts of memory that are handled today. In case memory was a problem would have to use some kind of bit-based encoding. If this does not exist, the type is216229undefined.According to this encoding, the codes 111 , 001 , 010 , 011 and 000 can only bebe at the bottom of the pile; what's more, codes 100 , 101 and 110 can never be in the base since they require another type below on which to apply. This fact allows us to use the same codes to represent both primitive types and type constructors: if it is at the base of the stack, it refers to a type primitive and otherwise what is referenced is a constructor. In the language we are defining, there is a zone of declarations and variable usage area (although they can be intermixed) by Therefore it is the parser who inserts the identifiers in the table of symbols at the time they are declared.As can be seen in the prog rules , there is an epsilon rule that217230Type managementallows you to enter through it right at the beginning of the analysis, as the base step of the recursion of the prog rules on itself.In the decl and type rules , we do recursive grammar to the right. Of course, for this it is necessary to have the type stack as an attribute of the non-terminal decl , so that stack must be propagated in bothproduction from the non-terminal of the consequent to the antecedent, that is, to decl . In conclusion, both type and decl have a type stack as attributes.Let us now go on to study the rules of expressions. The goal is that aexpression also has a type stack associated with it so that, once completed parsing the entire expression, its type stack is passed as a parameter to a function that translates its content into a natural language sentence.Expressions can be divided into three blocks: 1) primitives, formed bya constant of any type; 2) simple, formed by a variable; and 3) complex, made up of a simple expression with type modifiers to its right.Expressions of type 1 have a type stack associated with a singlecharacter, which will correspond to the type of the recognized literal:'i' for NUM.'r' for NUMREAL.'c' for CHARACTER.'b' for CTELOGICA.Expressions of type 2 have a type stack exactly the same asof the variable they represent. If the variable has not been declared then the stack types will only have a 'u' representing the undefined type (you cannot use the letter 'i' because it would be confused with the 'i' of INTEGER ).![](image_rsrcDHR.jpg) 219232Type managementExpressions of type 3 start from an expression (which has a stack of typesassociated) along with a type modifier. In this case, check that the type main expression (represented by the top of its type stack), it supports with the type modifier, as seen at the end of the section 7.4.2.2 . For example, if we find “expr ^”, “expr [NUM]” or “expr ()”, check that the typeAlpha's principal is 'p', 'a' or 'f' respectively. Assuming that the variable a has been declared as it appears in the figure 7\. 12 , the figure shows how the stack of types during recognition of the expression "a ^ [3]".The moment it reduces by the rule: expr​ :​ IDthe symbol table is searched for the variable that is given as an ID attribute ; a once found, the attribute of expr will be a copy of the type stack of that variable.As the rules apply:​ expr​ :​ expr '^'​ |​ expr '[' NUM ']'​ |​ expr '(' ')';it is verified that the modifier of the applied rule is compatible with the typefrom the top of the stack of the expression to which it is applied. If compatible, then the type of the resulting expression (the antecedent) will be the same as that of the consequent but without the top. If an error message should not be issued and the resulting expression will be undefined type.In short, type constructors build the stack by inserting charactersin it, while type modifiers destroy the stack by removing characters by the top.7.4.3 Solution with Lex / YaccThe most interesting thing about this solution lies in the implementation of the type ofstack data and its operations, especially the controls of the array limits that have been included.
