The details of the object language are confined to the stage final, if this is possible, making it easy to reuse the frontend to create other compilers of the same language but that generate code for other platforms. Of this way, although a priori it may be easier to translate a source program directly to the object language, the two main advantages of using a formFigure 8.1\. Building a compiler using stage division frontend and backend stage is done using intermediate code independent of the target machine and in which the source program is encoded. The resulting intermediate code program is the output of the frontend stage and the entrance to the backend235248Code generationintermediate independent of the target machine are:Retargeting is facilitated, that is, you can create a compiler for adifferent machine by linking a final stage for the new machine to a stage existing initial.A code optimizer can be applied to the intermediate representationindependent of the machine, which also allows reuse of this phase of the compiler regardless of the target machine.Fig . 8 .1 shows how the developer should choose to create acompiler through a versatile layout or through an easy layout. The final choice It depends on the utility that is going to be given to the short compiler source code or medium term, that is, if you are going to create several compilers or just one.The versatile philosophy in compiler construction reaches its extreme in![](image_rsrcDJ7.jpg) the implementation of languages  that are compiled and pseudo-interpreted in execution. This means that at compile time a code is generated own machine of a virtual microprocessor (called P-code in UCSD Pascal, bytecodes in Java, etc.) which, in turn, is interpreted at runtime via what is called the execution engine . These languages  cannot be classified as interpreted since what is interpreted at run time is not exactly the source program; but neither can they be considered fully compiled since they are that is generated at compile time is not exactly machine code. IsDesign philosophy has the advantage that it facilitates the portability of programs. He The most current language that works with this philosophy is Java, which uses .class files , instead of .exe . .Class files contain bytecodes that are subjected to a Java Virtual Machine (JVM) at runtime, for you to interpret. The JVM does the virtual microprocessor and the bytecodes act as instructions machine. Of course, to be able to execute the bytecodes on different platforms it is Each of them must have an adapted JVM implementation.This chapter shows how you can use the analysis methodssyntax-driven for translating a source program to a target program equivalent written in intermediate code. Since variable declarations do not generate code, the following sections focus on the sentences specific to a imperative programming language, especially assignments and clauses control of the execution flow. Generation of intermediate code can be interleaved in the parsing by means of the appropriate semantic actions.It is also important to note that sometimes it can be interesting to builda source-source translator instead of a full compiler. For example, yes we have a C compiler and we want to build a Pascal compiler,it may be much more convenient to build a program that translates from Pascal to C, so that, once the C program equivalent to Pascal's has been obtained, it will suffice to compile this one to get the desired result. Basically, the techniquesyou will see in this chapter to generate intermediate code can also be applied to the high-level code generation.236249​ 8.2​ Triplet codeIn order to facilitate the understanding of the code generation phase,we will not focus on the pure machine code of any specific microprocessor, but in an intermediate code close to any of them. This approximation It will also facilitate the optimization of the generated code.Each of the instructions that we can generate has a maximum offour sections:1st operand (memory address where the first operand is).2nd operand (memory address where the second operand is).Operator (operation code)Result (memory address where to store the result, or to which to jumpin case it is a jump operation).We will assume that both direct addressing andImmediate, that is, the address of an operand can be substituted for the operand itself. These types of instructions are called 3-way codes, triplets, or codes of maximum 3 operands.In essence, triplets are very similar to any assembly code,there are operations to add, subtract, etc. There are also instructions for control the flow of execution, and symbolic labels may appear in the middle of the code in order to identify the destination of the jumps.![](image_rsrcDJ8.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDJ9.jpg) Not all instructions have to have exactly all thementioned sections. By way of introduction, the triplets that we can use are:Binary assignment: x: = y op z , where op is a binary arithmetic operation or logic. Although there must be different operations depending on the type of data with which to work (it is not the same to add integers than to add real ones), to facilitate our study we will assume that we only have the type whole.Unary assignment: x: = op y , where op is a unary operation. The Major unary operations include unary minus, logical negation, the bit shift operators and the bit shift operators types.Simple assignment or copy: x: = y , where the value of y is assigned to x .Unconditional jump: goto tag .Conditional jumps: if x or relational and goto label .As can be deduced from the proposed triplets, the memory addresseswill be managed symbolically, that is, through names instead of numbers. Of course, to generate triplets closer to a machine code Generally, each of these names must be translated into a memory address only. The memory addresses would be taken consecutively taking into account it counts the size of the type of data that each one of them is supposed to house; by 237250Code generationFor example, you can assume that an integer value occupies 16 bits, a real one occupies 32 bits, a character occupies 8 bits, etc.With the above triplets we will cover all the examples proposed in thethis chapter. However, these triplets do not include all the possibilities of basic execution contemplated by a current microprocessor. For example, for calling a subroutine you have triplets to put the parameters on the stack calls, to invoke the subroutine indicating the number of parameters passed to it, to take a parameter from the stack, and to return:param x : puts the real parameter x on the stack.call p, n : calls the subroutine that begins at label p , and tells it totake n parameters from the top of the stack.pop x :​ takes a parameter from the stack and stores it in the x address .return y : returns the value y .Other triplets allow managing the indirect addressing and indexing:Indexed addressing: triplets are of the form y: = x [i] and x [i]: = y , where x is the base direction, and i is the displacement.Indirect addressing: triplets are of the form y: = & x and x: = * y , where the symbol & means "the address of ..." and the symbol * means say "the value of the address contained in the address ...".The choice of allowable operators is an important aspect in designintermediate code. The set of operators must be rich enough to allow to implement all source language operations. A set of small operators is easier to implement in a new target machine, but if it is Too limited can force the initial stage to generate long sequences of instructions for some source language operations. In such a case, the optimizer and the code generator will have to work harder if it is to produce good code.Below is an example of code for triplets that stores in![](image_rsrcDJA.jpg) the variable c the sum of a and b (note that b is destroyed as a consequence of this calculation): c = alabel etqLoop if b = 0 goto etqFin b = b-1 c = c + 1 goto etqLooplabel etqFin​ 8.3​ A simple compiled calculatorTo illustrate how the code of triplets is used in a grammar we are going tosuppose that our calculator instead of being an interpreted calculator is acompiled calculator, that is, instead of interpreting the expressions we are going to generate equivalent intermediate code.2382518.3.1 Construction stepsBefore starting to code the lexical and parsers it is necessarystate exactly what you want to do and with what grammar. For this you mustpropose a preliminary example of what the calculator should do to, at then create the grammar that recognizes the language, assign the attributes necessary and, once the purpose of the semantic actions is clear, begin the coding.8.3.1.1 Proposal of an exampleThe goal is for the calculator to produce an output text before a text fromentry. For example, if the input is:a: = 5 * b; c: = b: = d: = a * (4 + v); c: = c: = c;the output should be: tmp1 = 5 * b a = tmp1 tmp2 = 4 + v tmp3 = a * tmp2 d = tmp3 b = d c = b c = c c = cwhich is the equivalent triplet code.8.3.1.2 Starting grammarThe starting grammar based on production rules is:​ prog​ : assign ';'| prog assign ';'​ |​ error ';'| prog error ';';​ assign​ : ASIG ID expr​ |​ ASIG ID assigned;![](image_rsrcDJB.jpg) ​ expr​ :​ expr '+' expr​ |​ expr '*' expr​ |​ '(' expr ')'| ID| NUMBER;As can be seen, the empty program is not allowed, which makes it It is necessary to introduce a new error rule that manages the possibility of fails on the first assignment. Note that if an error occurs in this239252Code generationfirst assignment, the parsing stack does not yet have the nonterminal prog to its left, so it is not possible to reduce by rule prog: prog error ';' , but yes by rule prog: error ';' .On the other hand, the completion of each sentence is done with a semicolon';', instead of carriage return, which is closer to the actual treatment of current programming languages.In addition to allowing multiple assignments, the last difference withregarding the grammar of the section 7.3.1 is that thetypes and, therefore, conversion functions from one type to another do not make sense either.Finally, this grammar expressed in BNF notation is:grammar():{}{(sentFinished ()) *}sentFinished (): {} {CompositeAssign () <POINTANDOMA>​ |​ / * Error handling * /}CompositeAssign (): {} {LOOKAHEAD (4)<ID> ": =" CompositeAssign ()​ |​ <ID> ": =" expr ()}expr (): {} {term () ("+" term ()) *}term (): {} {fact () ("*" fact ()) *}fact (): {} {<ID>​ |​ <NUMBER>​ |​ "(" expr () ")"}8.3.1.3 Important considerations about the translatorTo facilitate the reading of the resulting intermediate code, use will be made of![](image_rsrcDJC.jpg) variable identifiers instead of directly using their address memory. In any case, do not forget that the symbol table must contain, together with the identifier of each variable, the memory address of each of them, in order to carry out the corresponding translation at the time of generating the final machine code.The grammar to be used will be practically the same as the one usedin previous sections. Remember that in each rule in which an operator appears arithmetic, only two expressions are involved, which fits with the generation of a240253binary assignment triplet, in which there are only two operands and one result. Without However, the reduction for each of these rules represents an intermediate result on the way to calculate the final result, as illustrated in Figure 8 .3 .These intermediate results should be stored somewhere so that thetriplets can work with them. For this purpose temporary variables are used automatically generated by the compiler in the amount that is necessary. The figure 8\. 2 shows the triplet code to be generated for an assignment thathas as r-value the expression of the figure 8 . 3 , as well as the temporary variables that you need to generate at every step. As is evident, the code of triplets resulting is equivalent to source code.Figure 8.3\. Intermediate results occur each time reduces by a production rule, and they intervene decisively in the calculation of the final resultThrough the mechanism of temporary variables it is possible to build, stepby step, any complex arithmetic calculation, in which parentheses can intervene,Figure 8.2\. Representation of the temporary variable to be generated in each reduction, and the triplet that must occur. The ultimate goal of these triplets is to store in the variable a the value 3 + 5 + 7 + 8 , which is achieved based on the temporary variables![](image_rsrcDJD.jpg) 241254Code generationother variables, etc. It is also interesting to note that the temporary variables they can be reused in different calculations. However, this is an optimization that we will not implement for the sake of concentrating exclusively on generating correct code.For the realization of our calculator, we must observe two very aspectsimportant:Properly manage attributes. In the calculator there is no part of declarations or type checking, since all variables are assumed are of type numeric and that are declared at the moment they do their First apparition. This is why a symbol table will no longer be necessary.that, in addition, the identifiers of the variables will appear in the triplets, and not the memory addresses they represent. This also allows us bypass a memory address manager that assigns them as that new variables appear.Carry out a printf sequence . Our purpose is to make a translation textual, in which you enter a text representing high-level assignments, and Another text comes out that represents the triplets. Therefore our actions semantics will aim to perform a sequence of printf orSystem.out.println to display these output texts. For this reason, incompile time, expressions will not be evaluated or performed calculations of any kind. Our translator is solely responsible for managing text strings; This means that, for example, the attribute associated with the token NUM does not have to be of type int, it just needs to be a char [] or a String, since it will not be manipulated arithmetically.In building a real compiler, the output is usually not text, buta binary file that contains the machine code grouped by blocks that will be managed by a linker to obtain the final executable file.Likewise, semantic actions must perform all the necessary type management before proceeding to code generation, leading to the need for a table of complete symbols.8.3.1.4 Required attributesAs already mentioned, all the management of the entry code will besymbolic, in the sense that you are only going to work with text strings. This form, both terminal ID and terminal NUM will have a string attribute as that stores the lexeme they represent.As for expressions, it makes sense to think that they must also have![](image_rsrcDJE.jpg) as an attribute to a character string that somehow represents the chunk of syntactic tree of which this expression is the root. Taking up, for example, the tree of figure 8 .2 , you can think of assigning to an expression the text that represents each variable, whether temporary or not; if we associate the same type of attribute to the non-terminal 242255assign , then it will also be possible to generate triplet code even for the multiple assignments. Regarding expressions that represent a single value numeric, you can choose to generate a temporary variable to store them, or save the numeric lexeme as an attribute of the expression, which leads to a optimization regarding the number of generated triplets and temporary variables used. The figure8 . 4 .a) shows the strict case where it is only allowed as an attribute the name of a variable, and figure 8 . 4 .b) shows the relaxed case. In both situations the attribute type is the same. In the solution proposed below case b) is chosen, since it involves optimization without any effort on the part from the developer.Finally, figure 8\. 5 shows attribute propagation in the case ofperform a multiple assignment. As mentioned before, the generation of code is made possible by using an attribute to the non-terminal assign that Figure 8.4\. Assignment of attributes to terminals and non-terminals of the calculator grammar. In both cases the type of the attributes is thesame. The difference between cases a) and b) lies in whether or not it is admitted that a expression can directly represent a numeric literal243256Code generation![](image_rsrcDJF.jpg) represents the l-value, in anticipation of it being, in turn, the r-value of an assignment multiple that is reduced from right to left.Figure 8.5\. Multiple assignments and the equivalent generated code8.3.1.5 Semantic actionsThe semantic actions are extremely simple, as there is no tableof symbols, nor to have type checking. They just need to be managed properly the attributes, propagating them when necessary, generating the temporary variables and perform the necessary printf .8.3.2 Solution with Lex / YaccIn this solution, the Lex program is limited to recognizing the necessary lexemesand to assign to the tokens NUM and ID the lexemes they represent. Also note that the nonterminal249262Code generationAssignComposite () also encompasses the concept of simple assignment as a base case of its recursion​ 8.4​ Code generation in control statementsAfter studying the general mechanism of code generation inarithmetic expressions, the next step is to approach the study of the code of triplets equivalent to high-level statements for control of the execution flow. These types of statements include the WHILE and REPEAT loops, as well as the IF and CASE conditional statements.As we saw in the section 8.2 , the control of the execution flow is performedlow level through the goto and call triplets . In this text we will not go into detail about subroutine management, so we will focus exclusively on the goto unconditional and conditional.8.4.1 Starting grammarOur purpose is to recognize programs that allow us to carry outassignments to variables of integer type, and make flow changes based on the condition assessment. Conditions can be simple or complex; in the simple only relational operators appear: > , < , > = , etc .; in the complex ones can use the logical operators AND , OR, and NOT in order to concatenate most basic conditions.As can be seen, loops of type WHILE and of typeREPEAT . In the former, a condition is evaluated before entering the body of the loop; if this is true, the body is executed and the process begins again; in caseOtherwise, it is continued by the next statement after the WHILE . Therefore the body executes as long as the condition accompanying the WHILE is true . On the other hand, the REPEAT loop evaluates the condition after execution of the body, so that the body is runs until the condition of the loop is true .As conditional statements, the use of the IF clause is allowed, which allows![](image_rsrcDJP.jpg) execute a block of code optionally, as long as the condition is true . Likewise, an IF can be accompanied by an optional ELSE part that is it would execute if the condition was false. On the other hand, it has also been included the CASE statement that evaluates an arithmetic expression and compares it with each of the elements of a default list of values; case of coinciding with any of they then execute the code block associated with that value. If it doesn't match none of them, happens to execute (optionally) the code block indicated by theOTHERWISE clause . Finally, it has been decided not to include the FOR statement with so that the reader tries to code his own solution, since it is an exercise interesting.To illustrate the pure mechanisms of code generation, and not to intermixin semantic actions no action related to the management of symbol tables250263nor type control, it has been decided that our little language only has the type integer and that it is not necessary to declare variables before use.The grammar to be used, in production rules format, is:​ prog​ :​ prog sent ';'​ |​ prog error ';'​ |​ / * Epsilon * /;​ sent​ :​ ASIG ID expr251264Code generation

![](image_rsrcDJR.jpg) It should be noted that the body of a WHILE , REPEAT , IF , etc. thisformed by a single sentence. If you want to include more than one, the mechanism consists in resorting to the compound sentence. A compound statement is made up of a list of statements delimited by braces, as indicated by the rule:sent: '{' sent_list '}'Furthermore, nothing prevents entering as the body of any sentence offlow control to another flow control statement. What's more, the nesting of Judgments can occur at any level of depth. This fact makes pay particular attention to error control. As can be seen in thegrammar, there are two error rules, one at the program level and the other at the sent_list , that is, in the body of a compound statement.If a syntactic error occurs inside a compound statement, andif you only had the error rule at the program level, a recovery would be madeincorrect, as the panic mode mechanism would pull items from the stack until find prog ; then it would extract tokens until it finds the semicolon. And finally would continue the parsing process thinking that it has recovered the error conveniently and that the following statements are at the program level, which is false since it was inside a compound sentence. This problem isillustrated in the figure 8 . 6 . To avoid this it is necessary to introduce a new error rule at the compound statement level.Figure 8.6\. Need for two error rules when using compound statements![](image_rsrcDJS.jpg) 252265From the BNF point of view for JavaCC, the grammar is:grammar void (): {} {(sentFinished ()) *}void sentFinished (): {} {(s = id () <ASIG> e = expr ()​ |​ <IF> cond () <THEN> sentFinished ()[<ELSE> sentFinished ()]<End> <IF>​ |​ <WHILE> cond () <DO>sentFinished ()<End> <WHILE>​ |​ <REPEAT>sentFinished ()<UNTIL> cond ()​ |​ <LKLAVE> grammar () <RLLAVE>​ |​ <CASE> expr () <OF>(<CASE> expr () <TWOPOINTS> sentFinished ()) *[<OTHERWISE> sentFinished ()]<End> <CASE>) <POINTANDOMA>| / * Error handling * /}String expr (): {} { term () (<MORE | <LESS> term ()) *}String term (): {} {fact () (<POR> | <ENTER> fact ()) *}String fact (): {} {(<LESS>) *(<ID>​ |​ <NUMBER>​ |​ <LPAREN> expr () <RPAREN>)}BlockCondition cond (): {} {​ condTerm ()​ (<OR> condTerm ()) *}BlockCondition condTerm (): {} {c1 = condFact () (<AND> condFact ()) *}BlockCondition condFact (): {} {(<NOT>) *​ (​ condSimple ()​ |​ <LCOR> cond () <RCOR>)253![](image_rsrcDJT.jpg) 266Code generation}BlockCondition condSimple (): {} {​ expr () (​ <MAX> expr ()​ |​ <MINOR> expr ()​ |​ <EQUAL> expr ()​ |​ <MAI> expr ()​ |​ <MEI> expr ()​ |​ <DIF> expr ())}This grammar has two differences with respect to that expressed with rules ofproduction. First of all the non-terminal list_sent has disappeared, since syntactically it is equivalent to prog (which we have called grammar here ). It us leads to needing a single error handling in the last rule of the nonterminal sent Finished . Obviously this could have also been done in grammarrule-based, but it has been preferred to illustrate the management of several error rules in one same grammar.The second difference lies in the management of conditions through adescending grammar. Before a sequence of characters such as: "((((27 * 8) ..." is It is necessary to know the content of the ellipsis in order to decide whether the three first parentheses are associated with an expression or a condition: if we find the character ">" the parentheses are associated with a condition, but if we find "7) ”then, at least, the third parenthesis is of an expression and we would still have to follow looking to see what the other two parentheses belong to. This problem has three clearly differentiated solutions:Establish an infinite lookahead .Differentiate the groupings of expressions and those of conditions; by For example, expressions can be grouped in parentheses, and conditions in brackets. This has been the solution adopted in this example.Consider that a condition is an expression of a logical or Boolean type . This is the most widely extended decision, although it has not been chosen in this case since it implies a type management on which we do not want to center.8.4.2 Preliminary examplesA few examples of valid programs are illustrated below.recognized by our grammar, as well as the equivalent triplet code that would be desirable to generate.The first example illustrates the iterative calculation of the factorial of a number n :ALPHA: = n;FACTORIAL: = 1;WHILE ALPHA> 1 DO254267![](image_rsrcDJU.jpg){FACTORIAL: = FACTORIAL * ALPHA;ALPHA: = ALPHA-1;};FIN WHILE;The equivalent triplet code would be:ALPHA = ntmp1 = 1;FACTORIAL = tmp1label etq1 tmp2 = 1; if ALPHA> tmp2 goto etq2 goto etq3label etq2tmp3 = FACTORIAL * ALPHA; FACTORIAL = tmp3 tmp4 = 1; tmp5 = ALPHA - tmp4;ALPHA = tmp5 goto etq1label etq3The following example illustrates the code generated for a CASE statement:CASE NOTE OF* We can use comments, dedicating a line to each of them.CASE 5: RATING: = OUTSTANDING;CASE 4: RATING: = NOTABLE;CASE 3: RATING: = PASSED;CASE 2: RATING: = INSUFFICIENT;OTHERWISERATING: = VERY_DEFICIENT; FIN CASE;Which is equivalent to:tmp1 = 5;if NOTE! 8.4.3 Condition managementAll flow changes supported by the language we just definedthey are conditional. In other words, based on the veracity or falsity of a condition, the program follows its flow of execution by one statement or another. Is by This means that the code that we decide to generate to evaluate a condition will be decisive to manage the flow in the different control statements.In this regard, there are two fundamental possibilities. Perhaps the simplestis to consider that the conditions are special expressions of logical type and257270Code generationwhose evaluation value is 0 or 1 (false or true respectively) and is stored in a temporary variable as it happens with expressions of type integer. The statement in which this condition is framed will ask for the value of the variable time to decide where the flow continues. For example, the rule:sent: IF cond THEN sent optional FIN IF​ 8​ 8​ 8​ Î​ Ï​ Ðwould establish in an action located at point Î a triplet of the typeif tmpXX = 0 goto etqFalsewhere tmpXX is the variable in which the result of having evaluated the condition. If its value is different from 0, that is when the condition is true, then the jump does not occur and the execution flow continues, which corresponds to the IF body statement. The result is that this statement is only executed when the condition is true. As optional it corresponds to the body of the ELSE (possibly empty), as this is exactly where you should jump in case![](image_rsrcDJX.jpg) the condition is false. In any case, once the body of the IF has been executed, it must jump behind the FIN IF , in order not to execute in sequence the optional part of theELSE . Therefore, at point Ï the triplets must be placed: goto etqFINIFlabel etqFalse:Finally, the end tag of the IF must be placed after the complete statement, in point Ð: label etqFINIF:with what the complete code generated would be:// Code that evaluates cond and puts its value in tmpXX if tmpXX = 0 goto etqFalse// Code of the IF statement goto etgFINIFlabel etqFalse:// ELSE sentence codelabel etqFINIF:To achieve this purpose, it is essential to insert actionssemantic interspersed in the consequent of a semantic action. In addition, Tags that have been identified here as etqFalso and etqFINIF must be, in actually, any labels generated ad hoc by our compiler, for example etq125 and etq563 respectively.In any case, this condition assessment mechanism incursa decrease in efficiency as composite conditions must be evaluated full. To avoid this problem without thinning the code, a technique is used that allows the evaluation of conditions using short circuit. For example, in a compound condition of the form cond OR cond , only the second will be evaluatedone              two condition if the first is false since, otherwise, it can be ensured that the​ OR result will be true regardless of the value of cond. One thing              twosimilar happens with the AND . This will be the method that we will use, and that is explained258271in detail below.8.4.3.1 Condition evaluation using short circuitThe mechanism is that each cond generates a block of code thathas two jumps ( goto ): a jump to a label A if the condition is met, and a jump to another label B if the condition is not met.Generating this code for simple conditions is trivial. Fromthey will study the short circuit in compound conditions.​ 8.4.3.1.1​ Simple conditionsAs a starting point it is assumed that the expressions and assignmentsgenerate the same intermediate code that was studied in the calculator in section 8.3.Therefore, in the face of a condition such as:7 * a> 2 * b-1the rule will be used:​ cond: expr '>' exprone​ twoand by the rules of expr , and without introducing any semantic action in the rule yet from cond , the code is generated:Ø tmp1 = 7 * aÙ tmp2 = 2 * bÚ tmp3 = tmp2-1![](image_rsrcDJY.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDJZ.jpg) where line Ø corresponds to expr and lines Ù and Ú correspond toone expr. Also, the attribute representing expr has the value "tmp1", whiletwo              one who represents expr is "tmp3". twoOn the other hand, we claim that a production rule of a conditionsimple like:

| cond: expr op exprone​ rel generate a code block of the form: | two |
| ​ if var op var goto etqone​ rel​ two | True |

​ goto etq​ Falsewhere var is the representative of the first expression, either a temporary variable,one a user variable or a numeric constant; var represents the second              two expression; and op represents the comparison operator: greater than (>), less thanrel(<), other than (<>), etc. Furthermore, etq​ True​ and etqFalse are ad hoc generated tags by the compiler sequentially: etq001, etq002, etc., similar to howgenerated the temporary variables. Continuing with our example, the code that we interested in producing is:if tmp1> tmp3 goto etq001 goto etq002where etq001 represents the etq​ True​ and etq002 represents etq              False . To generate this code it is enough to associate a semantic action to the rule of the condition, so that it would remain: cond: expr '>' exprone              two              {259272Code generationnewEtq ($$. etqTruth); newEtq ($$. etqFalse);printf ("\ tif% s>% s goto% s \ n", $ 1, $ 3, $$. etqTruth); printf ("\ tgoto% s \ n", $$. etqFalse);}As can be seen in this semantic action, the attributes associated witha condition is a pair of labels, one true and one false. Also, the block of generated code has two gotos (one conditional and one unconditional), to paths labels, but it has not located the destination of any of them, that is, they have not The label . Code generation for a simple conditionThis is because the rule that makes use of the condition (either in an IF, in![](image_rsrcDK0.jpg) a WHILE, etc.), you should place these labels appropriately. For example, a WHILE would place the destination of the truth tag at the beginning of the loop body, while that the false one would go after the body of the loop; this way when the condition isFigure 8.8\. Code that should generate a complete IF statement260273false skips the body, and when true a new cycle is executed. One thing similar would occur with the rest of the sentences; the figure8 .8 shows the case of an IF .​ 8.4.3.1.2​ Compound conditionsOne fact that follows from the above discussion is that the judgments in thethat a condition is framed (IF, WHILE, etc.) must perform a treatment uniform of these whether it is simple conditions or conditions compound in which the logical operators AND, OR and NOT intervene. So, A compound condition must itself generate a code block in which there are two gout for which the destination has not yet been established. Labels destination will be assigned as attributes of the non-terminal cond , thus the treatment of the compound conditions coincide with that of the simple ones.In addition, it is desired to use the short-circuit technique, whereby a conditionit is only evaluated if its truth or falsity value is decisive in the evaluation of the compound condition in which it intervenes.The following explanation can be better understood if we think about the blockof code generated by a condition such as a black box that executes, in some moment, or a jump to a fake tag, or one to a real tag, just like illustrates figure 8 .9 .a). Therefore, before a rule like​ cond: cond AND condone​ two![](image_rsrcDK1.jpg) the code blocks of the figure would be generated 8\. 9 .b). But, having to reduce everything the set to a compound condition, the blocks in the figure 8\. 9 .b) should be reduced to only one, in which there is only one true label and one false label, as illustrates figure 8 .9 .c). For other operators, such as NAND, NOR, IMPLIES (implication logic), etc., the procedure is the same. A particular case is that of the XOR operator, whose truth table is:

| TO | B | A XOR B |
| True | True | False |
| True | False | True |
| False | True | True |
| False | False | False |

This operator does not support short circuit so code must be generatedsupported by temporary variables so that it skips to a false label case that the two conditions are equal, and a true tag otherwise.8.4.4 Management of flow control statements265278![](image_rsrcDK5.jpg) Code generationAt this point we will address the use of the conditions and their labelsassociated, to generate the code of statements that alter the sequential flow of execution based on the value of such conditions. These statements are: IF, WHILE, CASE and REPEAT.8.4.4.1 IF-THEN-ELSE statementThe case of the IF statement is the simplest. Here it is sufficient to indicate that theThe condition's truth tag is associated with the code after the THEN , and the false label is associated with the code that may be behind the ELSE . In anyIn this case, once the THEN sentences have been completed, a jump must occur at the end of theIF , because we don't want the ELSE statements to be executed as well . So, after the THEN sentences , we create a new label to which we will produce a jump, and we place the destination of that tag at the end of the IF code . This can best appreciated with an example:

| Source code | Intermediate code |
| IF A> 0 THENS1: = 1;ELSES2: = 2;END IF | if A> 0 goto etq1 goto etq2label etq1: S1 = 1 goto etq3 |

label etq2:S2 = 2 label etq3:The block diagram can be seen in figure 8 .17 .Roughly speaking, to do this you need to insert two semantic actionsintermediate, one that displays the code marked in red and another for the marked code in blue. The etq3 tag is stored in a variable we will call etqFINIF;Thus, the necessary semantic actions would be as indicated below (inThis simple approximation assumes that every IF has an ELSE ; later I will will propose the general case):{printf (“label% s \ n”, $ 2.etqTruth); } 9sent: IF cond THEN sent ELSE sent FIN IF {printf (“label% s: \ n”, etqFINIF ); }8{newEtq ( etqFINIF ); printf ("\ tgoto% s \ n", etqFINIF ); printf ("label% s: \ n", $ 2.etqFalso); }However, here is a problem that will become a constant inthe rest of the control statements: where is the etqFINIF variable declared? (Note that This variable has been italicized in the example above because it has not yet been declared). Obviously, each different IF that appears in the source program266279![](image_rsrcDK6.jpg)Figure 8.17\. Block diagram of an IF statementyou will need a different IF end tag . Therefore, it is clear that this label does not must be stored in a global variable since the statement in the ELSEit could be another IF , which would make it necessary to reduce it by this same rule and crush the value of the global variable before reaching the final semantic action (in black).On the other hand, etqFINIF cannot be declared in the semantic action inblue (where it is given a value by passing it as a parameter to the new function Etq), since in this case it would be a local variable to a C language block and it would not be visible in the semantic action of the end.So you need to declare it somewhere so that it is global enoughas to be seen in two semantic actions of the same rule; but must also be local enough that nested IFs can assign local values  without crush the labels of the outermost IFs . It is noteworthy that a metacompiler how JavaCC solves this problem using the code area associated with each BNF rule; here you could declare the variable etqFINIF and use it in any action semantics of that same rule. If there are nested IFs , the recursion itself in the calls to the functions that represent non-terminals would be responsible for creating new scopes with copies of that variable.The solution adopted by JavaCC can give us some clue to solvethe problem in PCYacc: we need a variable local to the full rule, which is you can use in all your semantic actions, but make it different if there is IF nested. The solution is to use the IF token as a container for said variable, assigning it as an attribute: the IF token attribute is accessible by the semantic actions behind it, and if there are, for example, three nested IFs , each of them will have its own attribute. Assuming this, the rule remains:

Lastly, the labels declared on line 127 could have been merged into one as two of them will never be used simultaneously; this has not been done to improve the readability of the code.292305Chapter 9Run-time memory management​ 9.1​ Memory organization during executionAs already commented in the first sections, for a program to berun on an operating system, it is necessary the existence of a loader that supplies the program with a contiguous block of memory on which to execute. Therefore, the program code resulting from the compilation must be organized in form that makes use of this block, so the compiler must incorporate the object program all the code necessary for it.Memory management techniques during program executionthey differ from one language to another, and even from one compiler to another. In this chapter studies memory management used in imperative languages like Fortran, Pascal, C, Modula-2, etc. Memory management in another type of languages  (functional, logical, etc.) is, in general, different from the organization that here it arises.For imperative languages, compilers generate programs that will haveat runtime an organization of memory similar (roughly) to the that appears in the figure 9 . 1 .![](image_rsrcDM1.jpg) In this diagram the sections of:the codethe Fixed Size Data Zonethe Stack or stackHeap or heapFigure 9.1\. Structure of the memory while executing a compiled program293306Run-time memory management​ 9.2​ Code zoneIt is the area where the instructions of the executable program are stored inmachine code, and also the code corresponding to procedures and functions you use. Its size and content are set at compile time and by it is said that its size is fixed at runtime.In this way, the compiler, as it generates code, willplacing sequentially in this area, conveniently delimiting the start of each function, procedure and main program (if any, as it happens in languages such as Pascal or Modula-2).The final executable program will consist of this area together withinformation regarding memory requirements for runtime data, both the fixed size block and an estimate of the dynamic block size of data. This information will be used by the loader ( linker ) at the time of load the executable into main memory to proceed with its execution.9.2.1 OverlaysSome compilers fragment the object program code using![](image_rsrcDM2.jpg) overlays or “flaps”, when the main memory available is less than the size of the Full program. These overlays are sections of object code that are stored in independent files and that are loaded in the central memory dynamically, it is that is, during program execution. They are called "flaps" because two overlays they can occupy the same chunk of memory at different moments of time, overlapping. To make memory usage more efficient, the overlays of a program are grouped into zones and modules, each of which contains a set full functions or procedures. The figure9\. 2 shows how several are locatedFigure 9.2\. Examples of code zone configurations using overlays294307overlays in different zones of static memory.During runtime only one of the overlays of each of the Overlay zones can be stored in main memory. The compiler reserves in the code section a contiguous memory area for each set of overlays .The size of this area must be equal to that of the largest module loaded on it.It is the function of the programmer to determine how many overlay zones are defined, whatfunctions and procedures are encapsulated in each overlay module , and how they are organize these modules to occupy each of the overlay zones . A restriction to keep in mind is that the functions of a module should not refer to functions of another module of the same overlay , since they will never be simultaneously In Memory.Obviously, the execution time of a structured program withoverlays is higher than if it had no overlays and all the code was resident in memory, since during program execution it is necessary to load each module when a call is made to any of the functions it includes. Tooit is the programmer's task to design the overlays structure in such a way that it is minimized the number of these operations. The overlays technique is not only used when the program to compile is very large in relation to the memory availability of the system, but also when you want to obtain smaller programs that must coexist with other operating systems when memory is low.​ 9.3​ Data zoneThe data handled by a program is currently divided into three largeblocks:One dedicated to storing global variables accessible by anyline of program code. This block is the Size Data Zone Permanent.Another dedicated to storing the local variables to each function andprocess. These cannot be stored in the previous block for two main reasons: a) it is not necessary to store the variables of a function until the moment it is invoked and, once itsexecution, either; and b) during the execution of a recursive function must store multiple instances of your local variables, specifically as many as invocations to that same function have occurred. This block is the battery.![](image_rsrcDM3.jpg) A last block dedicated to storing managed memory blocksdirectly by the user through malloc / free , new / dispose , etc. The amount of memory required for these tasks varies from running the program, and the data located is not local to no function, but persist until released. This block is the Heap.295308Run-time memory management9.3.1 Fixed Size Data ZoneThe easiest way to store the contents of a variable in memory inruntime is in static or permanent memory throughout the entire program execution. Not all objects (variables) can be stored statically. For an object to be stored in static memory, its size (number of bytes required for storage) must be known in compile time. As a consequence of this condition, they cannot be stored in static memory:Local variables corresponding to procedures or functionsrecursive, since at compile time the number of times is not known that these variables will be needed.Dynamic data structures such as lists, trees, etc. since the number of elements that form it is not known until the program is run.Therefore, the variables local to a function are data of defined size,but for those who do not know the number of occurrences of the scope on which they depend, for what should be stored following some dynamic criteria. Nor is it possible store in this area the eminently dynamic structures, that is, those that are managed through pointers through dynamic free space creation: dynamic lists, dynamic graphs, etc. This data is of indefinite size so they cannot be saved in the fixed size data area. Although it may be thought that the code size of a recursive function also depends on the number of times the function calls itself, this is not true: the instructions to execute are always the same, and what changes is the data with which you work, that is, the local variables.Static memory allocation techniques are straightforward, just as![](image_rsrcDM4.jpg) illustrates figure 9 .3 . Starting from a position indicated by a reference pointerFigure 9.3\. Allocation of a memory block for a global variable X296309(limit of the fixed data area) the global variable X is stored, and the pointer is advanced as many bytes as are necessary to store said variable. In other words, to each variable is assigned an address and a chunk of memory from it according to with the type of the variable; these pieces are sequentially housed from the start of fixed size data area. For example, if we find the statement by Modula-2:a, b: INTEGER; c: ARRAY [1..20] OF REAL; c: CHAR; d: REAL;and assuming that an INTEGER occupies 2 bytes, a REAL occupies 5 and a CHAR occupies1, and assuming the beginning of the data area is at position 0000h (This address is relative to the DS data segment that can actually point to any memory address and that will be conveniently initialized by the charger), then we have the following address and size assignment:

Each function or procedure has associated an activation record of different size, in depending on how many local variables it has, the type of the return value, etc.9.3.2 Stack ( Stack )A language with a block structure is one that is composed ofmodules or pieces of code whose execution is sequential; these modules in turn can contain in their sequence of instructions calls to other modules, which in turn they can sometimes call other submodules and so on.The appearance of this type of language brought with it the need for techniquesmore flexible memory hosting, which could adapt to the demands of memory during program execution due to recursive invocations. In these languages, each time a procedure begins execution, a activation record to contain the objects necessary for its execution, eliminating it once it is finished.During a recursive invocation, only thelast invoked procedure, while the invoker sequence is found"Asleep." This means that, as the invoked functions finish, they must the calling procedures are reactivated in reverse order of the sequence of calls.298![](image_rsrcDM6.jpg) 311For this reason, the different activation registers associated with each block must be placed in a stack that they will enter when the execution of the block begins and will exit at finish the same. The structure of the activation records varies from language to language to others, and even from some compilers to others, this being one of the problems which is sometimes difficult to link the codes generated by two compilers different In general, procedure activation records usually have some of the fields that can be seen in figure 9\. 5 .Figure 9.5\. These values  must be replaced at the end of their execution. The code in charge of making the copy of the state of the machine is common for all procedures.The pointer to the parent's activation record allows access to variablesdeclared in other procedures within which the person is involved This activation record belongs to. For example, suppose a block of program like the following:PROCEDURE A1VARA1_a, A1_b: INTEGER;PROCEDURE B1VARB1_c, B1_d: INTEGER;BEGIN...END B1;PROCEDURE B2VARB2_e, B2_f: INTEGER;299312Run-time memory management![](image_rsrcDM7.jpg) PROCEDURE C2 VAR C2_g, C2_h: INTEGER; BEGIN...CALL B1;...END C2;BEGIN...CALL C2;...END B2; BEGIN (* Main program *)...CALL B2;...END;The pointer to the parent's activation record is used because aprocedure can make use of the local variables of the procedures in the that is declared. In this example, C2 can make use of variables:C2_g and C2_h (which are yours).B2_e and B2_f (which are from their parent, the one in which C2 is declared).A1_a and A1_b (which are from their father's father).but it cannot make use of either B1_c or B1_d, since C2 is not embedded in B1.As in the fixed-size data zone, the trigger logscontain space to store the actual parameters (values  associated with the variables that appear in the header) and local variables, (those defined within of the block or procedure) as well as a zone to store the value returned by the function and a zone of temporary values  for the intermediate calculation of expressions.Any two different blocks or procedures usually have recordsactivation buttons of different sizes. This size is generally known in compile time as there is sufficient information about the space occupied by the objects that compose it. In certain cases this is not so as per example occurs in C when using arrays of indeterminate length. In theseIn these cases, the activation record must include an overflow zone at the end whose size is not fixed at compile time but only when it actually reaches run the procedure. This complicates memory management a bit, so that some low-cost compilers remove this facility.Although it is essential to save successive versions of the dataAs recursive calls are made, the instructions that are applied to they are always the same, so these instructions only need save them once. However, it is clear that the same code will work with different data on each recursive invocation, so the compiler should generate300313code prepared for such eventuality, not making absolute reference to variables (except those defined globally whose memory location does not change in the course of program execution), but relative reference depending on the start of your activation record.The stack management procedure when one P procedure calls another![](image_rsrcDM8.jpg) procedure Q , is developed in two phases; the first of them corresponds to the code which is included in procedure P before transferring control to Q , and the second, to the code that must be included at the beginning of Q to be executed when it receives the control. Q premisesBEGIN...Q (...); // Recursive invocation from Q to Q...END Q;BEGIN...x = Q ( / * real params to Q * / ); // Invocation from P to QEND P;The first of these phases follows the following steps:1.1 The procedure that makes the call ( P ) evaluates the expressions of the invocation, using its temporary variable zone, and copies the result in the area corresponding to the actual parameters of the procedure that receives the call. Finally, P transfers control to Q , which places the return address on top from the stack.1.3 The receiver of the call ( Q ) saves the state of the machine before start your performance using the corresponding area of  your activation record.1.4 Q initializes its variables and begins its execution.At the end Q execution registration activation dislodges proceedingalso in two phases. The first is implemented by instructions at the end of theprocedure that has just finished its execution ( Q ), and the second in the procedure that made the call ( P ), after regaining control:2.1 The outgoing procedure ( Q ) before ending its execution places the value301314Run-time memory managementback to the beginning of your activation record.2.2 Using the information contained in its registry, Q restores the status of the machine and positions the end-of-stack pointer at the position it was originally.2.3 The calling procedure P copies the value returned by the procedure invoked Q within its own activation record (that of Q ).![](image_rsrcDM9.jpg) Fig . 9 .6 illustrates the status of the battery as a result of performing the Invoking P to Q , then a single recursive invocation of Q to Q . P does not have father since it is supposed globally declaredWithin a procedure or function, local variables are referencedalways as addresses relative to the beginning of your activation record, or to the beginning of local variable zone. Therefore, when it is necessary to access from a procedure to variables defined from other procedures whose scope is accessible, it will be necessary to dynamically provide the starting address of the variables of that procedure. To do this, the activation record is used pointer to the parent's activation record. This pointer will point to the beginning of the local variables of the immediately superior procedure (in which the call is found declared). The link pointer occupies a fixed position relative to the302315start of your local variables. It is important note that the concept of "parent procedure" is independent of that of "Calling procedure", although it may match this one.9.3.3 Stack ( heap )When the size of an object to be placed in memory can vary in time![](image_rsrcDMA.jpg) ![](image_rsrcDMB.jpg) execution, its location on the stack is not possible, much less in the data zone fixed size. Examples of this type of objects are lists and dynamic trees, variable-length character strings, etc. To handle this type of object the compiler must have a memory area of  variable size and not be seen affected by the activation or deactivation of procedures. This piece of memory it is called heap (from English heap ). In those high-level languages  that require the use of the heap , the compiler must incorporate in the generated object program, all the code corresponding to its management; this code is always the same for all built programs. The basic operations performed on the heap They are:Host: a contiguous block of memory is requested to be able to store aitem of a certain size.Evict: indicates that it is no longer necessary to preserve memory previouslyhoused for an object and that, therefore, it must be free to be reused if necessary by other hosting operations.Depending on the programmer or the system itself that invokes them, theseoperations can be explicit or implicit respectively. In case of accommodation explicitly, the programmer must include in the source code, one by one, the instructions that require a certain amount of memory for the location of each data or register (for example, Pascal provides the new statement , C provides malloc , etc.). The amount of memory required in each atomic operation of hosting, can be calculated by the compiler based on the corresponding type to the object to be hosted, or can be specified by the developer directly. The result of the host function is usually a pointer to a chunkcontiguous memory within the heap that can be used to store the value of the object. Imperative programming languages  often use hosting and Explicit evictions. On the contrary, declarative languages  (logical and functional) do most of these operations implicitly due to mechanisms underlying its operation.Heap management requires adequate techniques that optimize the space thattakes care (in order to prevent memory fragmentation) or access time, both factors being opposed as usually happens almost always in303316Run-time memory managementcomputing. In this regard, the techniques are usually divided into two large groups: those in which exactly the amount of memory requested is housed (they favor fragmentation), and those in which the block hosted is of a similar size, but generally higher than requested, allowing the heap to only store blocks of certain sizes preset at compiler build time (favors defragmentation).Finally, one of the most interesting features of languages![](image_rsrcDMC.jpg) very current imperatives, such as Java, is that they include an automatic garbage ( garbage collection ). The garbage collector is part of the heap manager , and has control over the pointers and memory chunks of the heap that are inaccessible through program variables, either directly or indirectly, this is, of the memory chunks hosted but not being pointed by anyone and which, therefore, constitute garbage. By means of this control they can effect automatic evacuation operations, freeing the programmer from performing explicitly this action. Among the techniques used to implement the garbage collectors we can quote reference counters, mark and sweep, generational techniques, etc.
